**Fixed.** Black churches are the center of Black Saint Jude: **Mount Zion Baptist**, **Bethel A.M.E.**, a score of storefront Pentecostal congregations. Pastors are political leaders, bankers, employers, and mediators. A major pastor can deliver 10,000 votes. Some pastors work with the numbers bankers, who give to the church. Some oppose them. The civil rights movement of the 1960s was organized in these churches.

### 10.4 Jews

**Fixed.** The Jewish community has moved from the Racks and the Eastside to Greenwood and the suburbs. Synagogues (Orthodox, Conservative, and Reform), the Jewish Federation, and a community center. The garment trade was once largely Jewish. Jewish lawyers, accountants, and doctors are common in the professional class, and the Jewish community has a long, mixed, and sometimes uneasy relationship with the Italian underworld as customers, partners, and victims.

### 10.5 Others

Greek Orthodox, Russian Orthodox, a small mosque (Black Muslims in the Eastside), and a Buddhist temple in Docks Chinatown.

---

## 11. Media and Public Culture

### 11.1 The Papers

**Fixed.**

- **The Saint Jude Daily Mirror** ("the *Mirror*"): the morning tabloid, loud, working-class, with crime, sports, gossip, and a good columnist. Circulation about 400,000. It prints on a union press schedule, which means the pressmen's union can slow it down.
- **The Saint Jude Chronicle** ("the *Chronicle*"): the evening broadsheet. Conservative, establishment, respected. Circulation about 250,000. It is in slow decline as evening papers everywhere are.
- **The Courier**: the Black weekly of the Eastside, founded 1912. It matters more than its circulation (about 40,000) suggests.
- **L'Eco della Corte** and **The Kerry Sentinel**: small ethnic weeklies.
- **The Fenwick Daily**: the student paper at the college.

### 11.2 Broadcast

**Fixed.** Three network television affiliates (**Channel 4**, **Channel 7**, **Channel 10**), a public station, and a UHF independent. Local news is an hour at six and eleven. Radio: a news station (WSJD), two Top 40 stations, a soul and gospel station on Dunmore Avenue, a classical station, an Italian-language hour on Sundays, and a station of Irish music on Saturday mornings.

### 11.3 Sports

**Fixed.** The **Saint Jude Admirals** (Triple-A baseball). The **Saint Jude's College Friars** (nationally ranked basketball, popular, and vulnerable). High school sports in the Catholic League, with the big Thanksgiving football game. **Bayview Downs** racetrack, thoroughbred racing in a season from March through November. Boxing at the **Civic Arena** on Friday nights. Sandlot softball and bowling.

**Open.** There is no major-league team in the city. Whether the city wants one, and who would pay for a stadium, is an open question.

### 11.4 Music, Food, Speech

**Fixed.**

- **Music.** The Hill sings Italian song and Sinatra. Dunmore Avenue's clubs play jazz, soul, and rhythm and blues. Kerrytown has ballad pubs. The Teatro della Corte offers opera and concerts. Rock in the college district. Disco arrives in the late 1970s.
- **Food.** Italian sauce (called "gravy" on the Hill), pork stores, pizza by the slice, fish stew on Friday, steamed crabs in summer (the Killbuck's bays), oyster stew, Portuguese sweet bread and linguiça, Polish pierogi, Jewish delicatessens, Black soul food, Greek diners.
- **Speech.** A broad Mid-Atlantic coastal accent. *Down the Point* means the docks. *Up the Hill* means San Lorenzo. A *hero* is a sandwich on a long roll. *Youse* is used by old men. People say "the Jude" only as a joke.

---

## 12. Women in Saint Jude

**Fixed.** Women are not decoration in this city. They run houses, shops, parishes, wards, hospitals, schools, and sometimes crime.

### 12.1 The Ordinary Facts

- About 45 percent of women of working age work outside the home in 1975 (Anchor: approximate for the period). Many work in the garment shops, as secretaries and clerks, as nurses and nurses' aides, in school and hospital kitchens, in shops, in bars.
- Women are rare in the top ranks of the city's institutions, but not absent. **At most one woman sits on the fifteen-member City Council, and there may be none. A woman on the bench is a rarity: one or two among dozens of judges, and each is watched. No woman has been mayor, police commissioner, County Prosecutor, union president on the docks, or a ward leader of a major ward. Women edit the *Chronicle's* society and features pages, and few reporters on the crime beat are women.** A Sister of Mercy runs Mercy General, **because Catholic hospitals and schools have been run by sisters for a century.**
- Women are admitted to the FBI as agents beginning in 1972 and to the SJPD as patrol officers beginning in 1973 (Anchor for the FBI and for the national trend). **They are well under 2 percent of either force, are kept out of the units that matter (organized crime squads, homicide), and face open hostility from many colleagues.**
- The women's movement has changed college life, newspaper offices, and some parts of the law. It has barely touched the Hill.

### 12.2 Women and Power in the Families

**Fixed.** Women are never inducted. They are never at the table. No price and no event breaks this. It is not an absence of power.

**Tendency.** In practice the wives, mothers, sisters, and widows of mob men are information networks. The Sunday table, the funeral home visit, the church bazaar, the hairdresser's, and the telephone link them across families. Women know who is sleeping with whom, who is dying, who is in debt, who is seeing a priest, and who has stopped coming to Mass. A boss's wife or mother may be the only person who can say a hard thing to him. Widows run businesses their husbands left. A mother can end a feud. A mother can start one.

**Example.** An old don's mother who insists on a war the family cannot afford, a consigliere's wife who runs the family's bakery and its books, a widow who inherits a social club and keeps its secrets.

### 12.3 Women in the Eastside

**Fixed.** In Black Saint Jude, women are among the strongest figures in the churches, the tenants' councils, and the schools, **which is where they were allowed to lead.** **Numbers "bankers" were sometimes women in the early twentieth century (Anchor: Harlem's Stephanie St. Clair is the best-known case). By 1975 the game is run by men, and women work in it as writers, clerks, and runners.** Women lead the Eastside Tenants' Council and run block associations, funeral homes, and beauty shops.

### 12.4 The Rule of Plausibility

**Convention for the Storyteller and the simulation.** A woman holds power in 1975 by one of four routes, and a new woman of consequence should come by one of them:

1. **Family:** wife, mother, sister, or widow of a powerful man, with real influence and no formal title.
2. **The Church:** a sister who runs a school, hospital, or charity; a lay parish leader.
3. **Small business:** a bakery, funeral home, boarding house, bar, beauty shop, or dress shop, often inherited from a husband or father.
4. **Female-dominated institutions:** nursing, teaching, clerical work, social work, the women's pages. Authority there is real and limited.

Formal power in male-dominated fields (police, courts, unions, politics, the mob**, where a woman is never made, 12.2**) is rare, slow, and resisted. A woman who rises there needs a cause the story can state (a sponsor, a scandal, a death, a quota, an exceptional record) and she meets friction at each step. **The numbers rise gradually through the late 1970s and 1980s as they did in the real world; they do not jump.** The simulation should not produce women in positions of power at modern rates, and should not make every woman a victim or a schemer either.

**Open.**

---

## 13. The Weather

This is a list of the standing conditions of the period that bear on the city as a whole. Law-enforcement, underworld, and technology Weather appears in later Parts. Each item gives a **window** and a **strength** (weak, moderate, strong). Weather is not scheduled. It builds, peaks, fades, and hits different people differently.

| Window | Condition | Strength | Who it presses |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1970 to 1985 | Containerization cuts dock work | strong | longshoremen, their unions, Pellegrino and Irish dock rackets, shipping lines |
| 1970 to 1985 | Deindustrialization of steel, chemicals, garment | strong | mills, the Racks, the Flats, Vannucci and Ferrante industries, union pensions |
| 1970 to 1980 | White flight and suburban growth | strong | the Hill, Kerrytown, the tax base, parish and school systems |
| 1974 to 1977 | School desegregation | moderate | Kerrytown, the Eastside, Kosciuszko Park, ward politics |
| 1975 to 1977 | Municipal austerity | strong | police, fire, sanitation, the Pad, the Mayor, bondholders |
| 1975 | End of the Vietnam War and return of veterans | moderate | labor market, drug markets, recruits |
| 1971 to 1985 | Soviet Jewish emigration | moderate, growing | Brighton Point, the Vorovskoy, the Racks |
| 1976 | Conrail forms; rail restructuring | moderate | the Rail Yards, trucking |
| 1976 to 1980 | Casino gambling arrives in the region | moderate | the Shore, state politics, organized crime |
| 1975 onward | State daily numbers game (Pick-it, 22 May 1975; Pick-3 from 1987) | strong | the Eastside combine, every numbers bank |
| 1977 to 1979 | Disco boom; collapse in 1979 and 1980 | moderate | the Strip, nightclubs, skimming |
| 1978 to 1980 | Hazardous waste becomes a national scandal | moderate | the Flats, waste hauling, the Vannucci |
| 1979 to 1980 | Second energy crisis | strong | trucking, prices, tempers |
| 1979 to 1983 | Very high interest rates | strong | developers, small business, homebuyers, loan sharks |
| 1980 | Motor Carrier Act and Staggers Rail Act deregulate trucking and rail | moderate, long | the Teamsters, the Ferrante trucking interests |
| 1980 to 1983 | Deep recession | strong | everyone |
| 1980 onward | Cocaine boom | strong, growing | the Strip, the Eastside, every family |
| 1981 to 1984 | Rock cocaine is rare in the Northeast; powder cocaine grows | weak | the Eastside, the Strip, police |
| 1985 to 1987 | Crack boom in Northeastern cities [memory, to verify] | strong | the Eastside, the Strip, police |
| Early 1980s | Gasoline excise-tax evasion schemes | moderate | Brighton Point, the Italian families' tax |
| 1983 to 1984 | Commercial cellular service begins (late 1983, [memory]); the telephone monopoly breaks up (1984) | moderate | communications, wiretaps |
| 1984 to 1988 | Federal prosecutors escalate RICO cases against the families | strong | every family |

This table is meant to be read across rows by the people it presses and down columns by the Storyteller. The same row will land very differently on a man who owns a trucking firm, a man who drives for one, and a man who lends money to both.

---

## 14. Names

### 14.1 Naming By Community

The Storyteller names characters by where they live, who their parents were, and when they were born.

- **Italian.** Given names (born 1900 to 1955): Salvatore, Vincent, Carmine, Giuseppe, Anthony, Michael, Frank, Joseph, Paul, Louis, Dominic, Gaetano, Nicholas, Alfonso, Rocco, Ralph, Sebastian, Sam, Angelo, Peter, Gabriel. Surnames from the south: Sabatini, Pellegrino, Vannucci, Ferrante, Basso, Scalise, Bellini, Spatafore, Lombardo, Santoro, DeLuca, Conti, Maranzano, Ragusa, Bellomo, Russo, Marotta, Pugliese, Marchetti, Cavallo, Grasso, Marino, Esposito, Bruno, Ferrara, Greco, Lupo. Nicknames are common. Girls: Maria, Rosa, Angela, Carmela, Concetta, Lucia, Anna, Theresa, Josephine, Antoinette.
- **Irish.** Patrick, Michael, John, Thomas, Brendan, Liam, Declan (rare before the 1960s), Dennis, Francis, Timothy, Eamon, Kevin. Gallagher, O'Malley, Boyle, Doyle, Sullivan, Murphy, Kelly, Dolan, Brennan, Fitzgerald, Walsh.
- **Polish and Slavic.** Stanley, Walter, Edward, Joseph, Casimir, Thaddeus, Henry, Bernard. Kowalski, Nowak, Wisniewski, Kaminski, Zielinski, Pawlak, Sokolowski, Hrynchuk.
- **Portuguese.** Manuel, Joao (John), Antonio, Francisco, Jose, Carlos. Silva, Pereira, Costa, Medeiros, Souza, Rodrigues, Fernandes, Oliveira, Ferreira.
- **Black.** Given names common among those born 1920 to 1955: James, Robert, Marcus, Darius, Willie, Leroy, Clarence, Calvin, Curtis, Reginald, Walter, Harold, Eugene, Cornelius. Women: Dorothy, Gloria, Loretta, Mavis, Lorraine, Bernice. Surnames: Holloway, Freeman, Dunmore, Whitaker, Pettiford, Gaines, Bishop, Greene, Washington, Jefferson, Coleman, Lawson.
- **Jewish.** Morris, Irving, Sidney, Leo, Harold, Arthur, Marvin, Herbert, Martin. Rosen, Weiss, Blum, Kesselman, Goldstein, Feldman, Katz, Shapiro, Levy, Rubin.
- **Old Protestant.** Harrison, Franklin, Theodore, Winthrop, Hal, Edwin, Charles. Sterling, Whitcomb, Hargrove, Pendergast, Hallam, Ainsley, Blackwood, Thorne.
- **Puerto Rican and Latino.** Luis, Jose, Juan, Angel, Ramon, Hector, Pedro, Carlos. Rivera, Ortiz, Colon, Santiago, Cruz, Rosario, Vega, Morales, Torres.
- **Greek, Lebanese, Armenian.** Nick, George, Peter, Gus, Tony, Sam, Steve. Papadakis, Stavros, Karras, Haddad, Najjar, Kasparian.
- **Soviet Jewish émigré (1975).** Evsei, Boris, Arkady, Yuri, Mikhail, Leonid, Igor. Volkov, Agranov, Fridman, Gurevich, Kogan, Rabinovich. (Some of these names are those of an assimilated Odessan Jewish community.)
- **Chinese.** Wong, Chan, Lee, Lau, Fong, Chin.

### 14.2 Avoid Collisions

Choose names for people that do not repeat. The old codex had four Sals, two Corrados, a Vance and a Vance-Miller, and two Vinnys. A hundred named people can be told apart only if their names do not look alike. If a name has already been used in the campaign, choose another.

### 14.3 Nicknames: Examples

Nicknames are a feature. Mob nicknames come from habit, body, history, or irony: *Eddie Glasses*, *Larry Nails*, *Paulie the Hammer*, *Danny Velvet*, *Uncle Bert*. A good nickname is something a man did not choose and would not have chosen.

---

## 15. What Is Open in This Part, and What Is Borrowed From The Real World

### 15.1 Open

- The names of all officials, judges, pastors, and publishers.
- The State's officials after January 1975.
- Which streets exist and what they are called, beyond those named here.
- Who owns any building, shop, or business not named here.
- The result of every election.
- The result of every strike, closure, and bankruptcy.
- Which parishes close and when.
- Whether the city defaults.
- Whether casinos ever come to the Shore, beyond Atlantic City.
- How the neighborhoods age, change, and decay.

### 15.2 Anchors Used In This Part

These are the real-world facts this Part leans on. They are here so that they can be re-checked.

- Maryland as a Catholic refuge (1634).
- Dutch *kil* as the word for a creek.
- English conquest of New Netherland (1664).
- Saint Jude as the patron of desperate cases, and the traditional account of why.
- The Feast of Saints Simon and Jude on 28 October.
- Irish migration after the Famine; Italian mass migration 1880 to 1924; the Great Migration from about 1916.
- Prohibition, 1920 to 1933.
- The Waterfront Commission of New York Harbor (1953) as the model for the Killbuck Waterfront Commission.
- The Kefauver hearings (1950 to 1951); the Apalachin meeting (1957); the Valachi testimony (1963).
- Interstate highway construction and urban renewal, 1956 to 1968; the riots of July 1967 and April 1968.
- Title III wiretaps (1968); RICO and the Organized Crime Control Act (1970).
- The Knapp Commission (1970 to 1972) as the model for the Dolan Commission.
- The Penn Central bankruptcy (1970); Conrail (1976).
- The end of the Vietnam War (April 1975).
- Court-ordered school desegregation in Northeastern cities (Boston from 1974).
- The New York fiscal crisis and municipal layoffs (1975).
- Federal Community Development Block Grants (1974).
- Atlantic City casino referendum (November 1976) and first casino (26 May 1978) [verified].
- The real State of New Jersey: Trenton, Governor Byrne (1974 to 1982), the State Commission of Investigation (created 1968, working from 1 January 1969), Atlantic City, the Pine Barrens, Route 9 [verified except distances].
- **The New Jersey Constitution of 1947: the Governor appoints Superior Court judges and county prosecutors with the Senate's consent [verified].**
- Women admitted as FBI special agents (1972).
- Soviet Jewish emigration to Brighton Beach from the 1970s; Evsei Agron's arrival in October 1975.
- Disco boom (about 1977 to 1979).
- Love Canal and the national waste scandal (1978).
- Motor Carrier Act and Staggers Rail Act (1980); prime rate near 20 percent (1980); recession peak unemployment 10.8 percent (1982).
- Breakup of the Bell System (1984).
- Harlem numbers queen Stephanie St. Clair (1920s and 1930s).

**Verification status.** The Strike Force structure, Knapp Commission, Agron's arrival, the Waterfront Commission and crack's emergence were checked against sources in the audit. The remainder are my own knowledge and should be re-checked before they harden into canon. The audit memo lists which.


# Saint Jude: The Setting Bible

## Part II: The Law

Baseline: the start of 1975. Dated items after that are **Weather**. Section 0 applies to the whole bible, not only to this Part.

---

## 0. The Period Mind

### 0.1 The Rule

Everyone in Saint Jude thinks, speaks, and judges as a person of 1975 would. The Storyteller writes them that way. It does not judge them by present standards, it does not let a modern attitude leak into a character who would not have it, and it does not steer the story toward what is comfortable now. The story is a story. Narrating a bigoted remark, a killing, or a cruelty is not endorsing it, and playing a man who does these things is not endorsing them either.

This does not make everyone the same. Inside the period, people differ. The rule is about what the *world* takes for granted. Individuals vary within it, and their variation comes from who they are, not from what the narrator would prefer.

### 0.2 What the World Takes For Granted

These are the common attitudes of the time and place. Any given person may hold a harder or softer version.

**Death and violence.** Death is close and familiar. Many men have been to a war. Cops, nurses, firemen, longshoremen, and butchers have seen bodies. Wakes last two or three days, often in the home or a funeral parlor on the block, and children attend. In the underworld a killing is business, discipline, or a message. It is not a trauma. A man may order a death and then bring a mass card and an envelope to the wake, and nobody in his world sees a contradiction. The dead are mourned, the killing is understood, and in some circles vengeance is a duty. Capital punishment is widely accepted. Ordinary people say a rosary for the dead, including the ones they think deserved it.

**The law and the police.** Cops are respected, feared, resented, and related to. In the Irish and Italian neighborhoods the police are cousins and neighbors. In the Eastside they are widely seen as an occupying force. Police brutality is routine, known, and often approved of. Courts are distrusted. Informing is the worst word in the language in most neighborhoods, and testifying against your own people is a disgrace that follows a family for generations. Faith in public institutions is at a low point after Vietnam and Watergate (Anchor: Nixon resigned in August 1974).

**Race and ethnicity.** The city is segregated in fact, by neighborhood, church, union, and club. Open prejudice is normal in speech, hiring, housing, and marriage. Slurs are in ordinary use. Italians, Irish, Jews, Poles, Puerto Ricans, and Black people all give and receive contempt. Marriage across the lines is opposed by most families. Black and white leaders deal with each other with little trust. Individuals differ: some men are decent across the line, some are hostile, many are hostile in public and decent in private. The Storyteller shows all of this as it is.

**Religion.** Catholic practice is ordinary, not special: Mass, confession, saints, holy cards, novenas, blessings, fish on Fridays (the rule has been relaxed but the habit holds). The fear of hell is real. Wiseguys go to confession and mean it. Nobody thinks the combination of faith and crime is hypocrisy in the modern sense. It is a human condition the Church has always known. Protestant, Black Church, and Jewish observance are likewise ordinary and serious.

**Sex, marriage, and gender.** Men run the public world. Women run the home and much of the neighborhood. There is a double standard: men keep mistresses (*comares*) and are expected to; a wife's infidelity is a scandal. Divorce among Catholics is a disgrace and sometimes a bar to the sacraments. Homosexuality is widely despised, hidden, and in the criminal code in some places. Gay men and women exist everywhere, including in the mob, and they live carefully. Domestic violence is treated as private business and the police are reluctant to enter a house. Abortion has been legal nationwide since 1973 (Anchor) and is fiercely opposed in Catholic neighborhoods. The women's movement is real and has not reached most of the city.

**Drink, tobacco, drugs.** Everyone smokes, including in offices, hospitals, and courtrooms. Drinking is the glue of social life, and drunk driving carries little stigma. Heroin is shameful and feared. Marijuana is common among the young. Cocaine in 1975 is an expensive amusement of the rich and of the entertainment world. **(Part VI 6.2 now sets 1975 regular cocaine users at about 2 per 1,000, about 2,200 people, to match this.)** Prescription pills are widespread among housewives and the middle aged.

**Money and debt.** Cash is king. A debt is a shame and a bond. A favor is a currency and is remembered. The loan shark is a known institution, as common as a pawnshop. Gambling is normal. Credit is hard to get without connections, and many families have never had a bank loan.

**Respect.** Respect is a thing a man can lose in public, and a lost respect can demand an answer. An insult to a man, his family, or his name is a debt. Shame is more binding than law.

**Family and children.** Physical discipline of children is normal and expected. Children work young. Sunday dinner is a duty. Elders are obeyed. A man's family is his reputation.

**Illness.** Cancer is not named aloud in many families. Mental illness is hidden. Men who came back from Vietnam are expected to get on with it.

### 0.3 Mixed Men

The old codex sorted men into four types: Rotten Patriarchs, Young Zealots, Old Butchers, and Pragmatic Sharks. **This bible does not use them.** They make men into types, and real men are not types.

**Onore is a bundle of separate obligations.** A man holds each to his own degree and for his own reasons:

1. loyalty to his *compare* and his crew,
2. care for widows and for men in prison,
3. silence before the law,
4. honesty toward other made men,
5. respect for the Boss and for the rituals,
6. his own limits on violence, drugs, women, and children,
7. faith, family, and the Church,
8. his word on a deal.

Most men are strong on some and weak on others. A capo may pay a jailed soldier's wife to the dollar and sell heroin behind his boss's back. A hardened killer may have a rule about women and children. A sentimental old man may sell out a friend to stay out of prison. The same man can be loyal one year and break the next, because the price changes.

**Age does not decide it.** Old men keep the code, and old men sell it. Young men believe it, and young men use it as a tool.

**People change.** A man's stance on each obligation is a state that events move. A strict traditionalist may soften after the code costs him a son, or after prison, or after a sickness. A cynic may return to the old ways after he watches money wreck a family and a crew. Promotion, debt, betrayal, a death, a priest, a wife, a wound, age, fear of dying in a cell: each can move a man in either direction. Nobody is fixed. The simulation should let people drift and surprise, and the Storyteller should remember that a man you trusted last year may not be the same man.

**Nobody is wholly good or wholly bad.** A man the reader hates may keep faith in one thing no one else keeps. A man the reader admires may do something unforgivable. The reader should be able to back a man for a time and turn on him later, or the reverse.

**The same applies outside the families.** Cops, agents, prosecutors, priests, union men, politicians, and businessmen are all mixed in this way.

### 0.4 Narration Standards

How the Storyteller narrates is set in the rulebook, not here. See the separate file "Saint Jude Rulebook Narration Standards".

---

## 1. Who Enforces the Law, and Who Fights Whom

### 1.1 The Map

Law enforcement in Saint Jude is many agencies, not one. They overlap, compete, distrust each other, and share information badly. This is a feature of the world, and it is why the underworld survives.

**Local.** The **SJPD** (the city police); the **Port Authority Police**; the **Harbor Police**; the **Transit Police**; the **Oakridge Borough Police** and other suburban departments; the **County Sheriff** of Marsh County and the others; the **Fire Marshal** (arson); and the **Killbuck County Prosecutor** and the suburban county prosecutors.

**State.** The **State Police**; the **State Attorney General** and his Organized Crime and Racketeering Section; the **State Commission of Investigation** (the real New Jersey body, **created in 1968, working from 1 January 1969** [verified]: a body with subpoena power that can hold public hearings but cannot prosecute); the **Waterfront Commission**; the **ABC Board** (liquor); the **Racing Commission**; and the **Lottery Commission**.

**Federal.** The **FBI** field office under a Special Agent in Charge; the **U.S. Attorney** and his Assistant U.S. Attorneys; the **Organized Crime and Racketeering Strike Force** (prosecutors from the Justice Department's Criminal Division, with investigators attached from several agencies); the **DEA**; the **ATF**; the **IRS Intelligence Division**; the **Customs Service**; the **U.S. Marshals**; the **Postal Inspectors**; and the **Labor Department** (union and pension audits).

### 1.2 Tendencies

**Tendency.** Agencies guard their cases and their sources. The FBI does not share informants with the SJPD, and the SJPD does not share its files with the State Police. The Strike Force and the U.S. Attorney's office compete over the same cases (Anchor: federal reviewers criticized the Strike Force program in the late 1970s for rivalry and weak results). The DEA and the FBI quarrel over drugs. Prosecutors want convictions, agents want cases, cops want a quiet street, and each answers to a different boss. A criminal who understands which agency wants what can use the gaps. A prosecutor who forgets can lose a year's work to a rival.

### 1.3 The Honest and the Corrupt Are Both Everywhere

**Fixed.** Corruption is a tendency, not an absolute. Every agency contains honest men, compromised men, ambitious men, lazy men, and men who are all of these at different times. The local police are *more* exposed to the neighborhood and the Pad. Federal agents are *less* exposed, and are still human (Anchor: FBI agents have been corrupted by informants, including in Boston from 1975 **[memory]**). A bribe or a friendly judge changes the odds. It never decides the outcome.

---

## 2. The Saint Jude Police Department

### 2.1 Size and Structure

**Design (range 4,200 to 5,400).** The SJPD has about **4,800 sworn officers** at the start of 1975, in 24 precincts and a set of citywide bureaus. It is led by a **Police Commissioner** appointed by the Mayor. Beneath him are a Chief of Department (the senior uniformed officer), a Chief of Detectives, and deputy commissioners. Ranks run: patrolman, detective, sergeant, lieutenant, captain, inspector, deputy chief, chief.

**Weather, strong (1975 to 1977).** The city's money crisis forces a hiring freeze and, in the worst version, layoffs. The force may fall to about 4,300. Overtime is cut. Morale drops, the union rages, and hungry cops look at the Pad.

### 2.2 Pay and Life

**Design (start $10,500 to $12,500; after three years $13,500 to $15,500).** A patrolman earns about **$11,500 to start and $14,500 after three years**. A sergeant earns about $17,500. A lieutenant about $20,000. A detective $17,000 to $19,000. A captain $24,000. The Commissioner $38,000. **Part VI 7.3 uses these figures.** Cops moonlight, as security guards, bouncers, drivers, and bar staff. Pensions after twenty years are a good reason to stay and a good reason not to rock the boat. Divorce, drink, and heart attacks are common. Many cops retire to Florida.

**The union.** The **Saint Jude Patrolmen's Benevolent Association** is powerful, defensive, and run by men who came up through the ranks. It defends officers in discipline cases and is hostile to civilian oversight and to Internal Affairs. Sergeants, detectives, and lieutenants have their own associations.

### 2.3 Who the Cops Are

**Fixed.** The force is overwhelmingly white and male in 1975, and overwhelmingly Irish and Italian in the Catholic parishes that supply it: the SJPD is the Irish Church's secular arm and the Italians' next stop after the building trades. Black officers are a small and growing share, pushed by court orders and the **Guardians**, a Black officers' association. Women patrol officers have served since 1973 (Anchor: national trend) and are under 2 percent of the force, kept out of the units that matter. Whatever the force's official color, its culture is that of the Catholic working class.

### 2.4 The Precincts

**Fixed.** The **14th Precinct** sits at **Mercer and Ninth** in a soot-stained 1920s stationhouse, and covers the Strip. It is the best-known precinct in the city. It is **not** the whole department. Other notable precincts:

| Precinct | Covers | Character |
|---|---|---|
| 2nd | The Heights | quiet, political, old money |
| 3rd | North San Lorenzo | Italian, closed-mouthed, a precinct of cousins |
| 4th | South Hill | working class, Pellegrino loyalists |
| 6th | Bellacorte quarter | polite, small |
| 8th | Crown Harbor | the Civic Center; brass and politics |
| 9th | The Point, North Piers | docks, the fish market |
| 11th | Kerrytown | Irish; the cathedral; the Brigades |
| 14th | Mercer Avenue | vice, nightclubs, the Pad |
| 15th | The Racks | garment, freight, loft burglaries |
| 17th | Dunmore Avenue | Black; strained relations with the force |
| 19th | The Eastside housing | high crime, understaffed |
| 21st | Brighton Point | quiet, old, a new language to learn |
| 23rd | The Flats | industrial, empty at night, bodies |

The other precincts exist and are **Open**. **The 3rd and 4th precincts split the district Part I calls San Lorenzo, the Hill: "North San Lorenzo" and "South Hill" are its two halves, and the boundary between them is Open. The "Eastside housing" of the 19th is the public housing Part I places in the Eastside.**

### 2.5 The Citywide Units

**Fixed.**

- **The Detective Bureau.** Precinct detective squads handle ordinary crime. The **Homicide Squad** works out of headquarters, takes the murders, and is proud, overworked, and politically sensitive.
- **The Organized Crime Control Division** (everyone calls it "the Mob Squad") gathers intelligence on the families, keeps photo files and charts of who sits with whom, and works with the FBI and the Strike Force when it chooses to. Small, wary, and not always trusted by the rest of the department.
- **The Public Morals Division** (Vice) enforces the gambling, prostitution, and liquor laws. After the Dolan Commission its old plainclothes squads were broken up and reformed. It is where much of the department's remaining gambling corruption lives.
- **The Narcotics Division.** Street drug enforcement. Under pressure from federal agencies and from the Mayor.
- **Internal Affairs Division** (IAD), with small field units in the precincts. Hated by the rank and file. Honest officers fear it, and corrupt officers fear it more.
- **Emergency Service, the Harbor Unit, the Crime Laboratory, Youth Aid,** and traffic.

### 2.6 What the Dolan Commission Left Behind

**Fixed.** Between 1970 and 1972 the mayor's commission found that gambling payoffs in the plainclothes squads were routine, organized, and paid weekly by bookmakers and numbers banks to cops who shared the money by rank. It sorted the department into "**grass-eaters**" (who take small gratuities, free meals, and a little from bar owners, and do not hunt for more) and "**meat-eaters**" (who seek out payoffs, extort bookies and dealers, and sometimes rob them). The commission's hearings put several officers in prison, and the Commissioner resigned.

The reforms were real. Plainclothes gambling squads were broken up. Cops were rotated through precincts so that no man stayed long enough to build a network. IAD got field units. Commanders were held responsible for corruption in their commands. Promotion for informing on a colleague was talked about, and a few were rewarded.

**Tendency.** The reforms changed how the Pad works and did not end it. See section 3.

**Open.** Which officers were convicted, who the commission's lawyer was, which reforms have already faded.

### 2.7 The Ordinary Department

**Fixed.** Most of what the SJPD does has nothing to do with the mob. Patrol, traffic, domestic calls, bar fights, burglaries, car thefts, drunks, accidents, junkies, missing children, noise complaints. A day-shift patrolman on the Hill and a night-shift patrolman on the Eastside live in different worlds. The majority of cops are neither saints nor villains. They are men trying to get through the week, pay the mortgage, and avoid trouble.

---

## 3. The Pad

### 3.1 What It Is

**Fixed.** "The Pad" is the system of regular payments by illegal operators to police, in return for protection from enforcement. The word comes from the list on which the payments are recorded. A **pad** may be a weekly or monthly fixed sum, paid by a bookmaker, a numbers bank, a bar that stays open late, a brothel, or an after-hours club. It is the cost of doing business, like rent.

### 3.2 How It Works After the Dolan Commission

**Tendency.** The Pad survives the reforms by changing shape.

- **It moves up and out.** Fewer cops touch the money directly. Payments are smaller in number and larger in size, and are routed through an **intermediary**: a bail bondsman, a lawyer, a bar owner, a retired cop, a union man. Cops fear that any collector may be an IAD plant.
- **Rotation weakens loyalty.** Because officers are moved every few years, no man holds a precinct long. A relationship built over a decade can be lost in a month. The rule among careful bookmakers is: pay the *position*, not the man.
- **Fewer men are in it, and they are more careful.** A lieutenant who survived the Dolan years may have done so by never touching the money himself. Fear has taught the department discretion.
- **Grass is still everywhere.** Free meals, free drinks, a cut-rate suit, a Christmas envelope, a "gift" from a grateful shopkeeper. This is not seen as corruption by most of the force.
- **Meat is rarer and nastier.** The men who actively hunt payoffs are fewer after Dolan, and the ones who remain are dangerous.

### 3.3 What the Pad Buys

**Tendency.** The Pad purchases limits, not immunity.

- **Warning of a raid** (a phone call that rings twice and stops, or a word passed through the intermediary).
- **Soft treatment**: a patrol car that does not stop, a Vice team that raids on a quiet night.
- **Lost paper**: a missing report, a mistake on a warrant, an unsigned evidence log that helps the defense get a suppression order.
- **Information**: who is being watched, who is asking questions.
- **A blind eye** to a late-night bar, a floating game, a shipment moved at 4 a.m.

### 3.4 What the Pad Cannot Buy

**Fixed.**

- It cannot make a public killing go away. A body in front of witnesses, a bystander hit, a front-page story will force an arrest regardless of who is on the Pad.
- It does not reach the FBI, the Strike Force, or the State Attorney General.
- It does not control the Homicide Squad's honest men, the Medical Examiner, or IAD.
- It does not cover an officer who is killed. A cop killing brings every officer in the city and every agency above them.
- It does not bind a new commander. When a captain is replaced, the arrangement is reopened.

### 3.5 The Human Side

**Tendency.** A cop on the Pad is not simply a crook. He may be a man with a sick child, a second mortgage, and a boss who takes more than he does. He may be a man who takes from a bookie and would never take from a dealer. He may take for twenty years and then turn on the men who paid him, out of fear or conscience. Most officers who take are mixed in exactly the way this bible says all men are mixed (section 0.3).

**Example.** A Vice sergeant who has not touched an envelope in five years and has a friend who does. A patrol captain who knows exactly what happens in his precinct and has decided not to know. A young detective who refuses a Christmas envelope and learns that it costs him a partner.

---

## 4. Other Local and State Agencies

### 4.1 Port Authority Police

**Fixed.** About 600 officers under the Port Authority's own chain of command (section 8.1 of Part I). They police the bridge, the tunnel, the airport, and the terminals. They are underpaid and politically appointed, and the Authority's patronage makes them a source of jobs for the machine. They have their own corruption, quieter than the SJPD's, tied to cargo theft and the unions at the terminals.

### 4.2 Harbor Police and Customs

**Fixed.** The Harbor Police patrol the Basin and the Narrows. The **Customs Service** inspects imports at the Gannet Terminal and the piers and is the first federal agency to see what comes in. A sealed container can be opened by Customs, or can pass on a stamp. A Customs inspector who looks away is worth a great deal.

### 4.3 Suburban Departments

**Fixed.** Every suburb has a police department and a prosecutor. They are small, local, and close to their voters. **Oakridge's** twenty-odd officers know the bosses who live there by name and by car. When something serious happens in Oakridge the Borough Police turn to the Marsh County Prosecutor, the State Police, or the FBI, and everyone involved understands that the quiet is over.

### 4.4 The State Police

**Fixed.** A professional force with a reputation for being cleaner than the city police. It patrols the highways, the Pines, and the smaller counties, and supports local departments in serious cases. Its detectives work gambling, auto theft, and drug cases. Its organized crime unit is small and sometimes well informed.

### 4.5 The State Commission of Investigation

**Fixed.** A standing commission of appointed members with the power to subpoena witnesses and records, hold public hearings, and publish reports. It cannot prosecute, but it can embarrass. Its hearings are the closest thing the state has to the Senate crime hearings, and a witness who pleads the Fifth in public is a story.

### 4.6 Regulatory Bodies

**Fixed.** The **Waterfront Commission** registers longshoremen, checkers, and pier workers, and can strike a man from the rolls. The **ABC Board** licenses every bar and can revoke a license for tax fraud or gambling. The **Racing Commission** governs Bayview Downs and licenses trainers, jockeys, and owners. The **Lottery Commission** runs the state game. Each is a prize to capture and a place where an honest investigator can do real damage.

---

## 5. The Federal Presence

### 5.1 The FBI Field Office

**Fixed.** The Saint Jude field office is run by a **Special Agent in Charge** (SAC). Under him are Assistant Special Agents in Charge and squads by subject: organized crime, foreign counterintelligence, bank robbery and fugitives, white-collar, civil rights, and so on. Agents are mostly white, male, Catholic or Protestant, former military or lawyers or accountants, and proudly clean. The Bureau after Hoover (Anchor: Hoover died in May 1972) is in a long hangover of scandal and reform, and its leadership is trying to prove it can work cases against organized crime and political corruption, not only radicals.

**Tendency.** Agents are proud, careful, and territorial. They do not talk to local cops about their informants. They trust their own paper. Some are brilliant and patient. Some are careerists. Some run informants too close and cross lines that they cannot see crossing. The Bureau as an institution says it is incorruptible, and its individual agents are mixed like everyone else (Anchor [memory]: in Boston from 1975 an agent began a long and corrupting relationship with an informant).

### 5.2 The Strike Force

**Fixed [verified in outline].** The **Organized Crime and Racketeering Strike Force** is a Justice Department program of prosecutors, placed in the field, who build cases against organized crime with investigators from the FBI, IRS, DEA, ATF, Customs, and Labor attached to it. It is prosecutor-led. It is not an FBI unit, and nobody should call it one.

**Tendency.** It competes with the U.S. Attorney's office over credit and cases, and its attorneys are often younger, more aggressive, and more willing to try new theories than the old hands. Federal reviewers in the late 1970s criticized the program for rivalry and uneven results (Weather below).

### 5.3 The U.S. Attorney

**Fixed.** A presidential appointee, usually political, who runs the Assistant U.S. Attorneys. The office tries federal crimes in the district, including bank fraud, bribery, narcotics, tax, and racketeering. A U.S. Attorney with ambitions is a danger to the machine, and a U.S. Attorney with a patron is a friend of it.

### 5.4 The Other Federal Agencies

**Fixed.**

- **DEA** (formed 1 July 1973 [verified]) works heroin and cocaine cases and is the rival of the FBI over drugs.
- **ATF** works guns, explosives, arson, and untaxed liquor and cigarettes. It is the agency most likely to catch a man with a bad pistol.
- **IRS Intelligence Division** builds tax cases. It is patient, thorough, and dangerous to anyone who spends more than he reports. Tax evasion is how many men of the old generation went down.
- **Customs** inspects what crosses the border and the docks.
- **U.S. Marshals** guard courts, move prisoners, and from 1971 run the witness security program [verified].
- **Postal Inspectors** work mail fraud and stolen mail, and have a long memory.
- **Labor Department** auditors examine union books and pension funds under ERISA (signed 2 September 1974, Public Law 93-406 **[verified, Part VI pass]**).

### 5.5 The Federal Courts

**Fixed.** The federal district court sits in a downtown courthouse. Federal judges are appointed for life and mostly decline to be bought, though a friend of a friend can be useful for a lenient date. Federal juries are drawn from a wider area and are less likely to be the defendant's neighbors than a county jury. Federal sentences for racketeering and narcotics are heavy by the standards of the time and heavier after 1984.

---

## 6. Law and Procedure

Everything here is dated. A rule changes when its date arrives, and **the Storyteller must not apply a later rule earlier.** Tags: [verified] means checked against sources, [memory] means from general knowledge and still to be checked.

### 6.1 Arrest, Questioning, and Counsel

**Fixed [memory].** Miranda warnings (1966) are in force. A suspect may refuse to talk and may have a lawyer. Wiseguys know this by heart: the standard policy is to say nothing, "ask for your lawyer," and keep walking. A man who talks to the police without counsel is considered a fool or an informant. Cops and detectives know all the older methods of pressure and use them where they can: the long wait, the friendly cop and the hard cop, the threat to the wife, the offer to deal. Roughing up a prisoner is more tolerated than it will later be, and is rarely punished.

### 6.2 Bail

**Fixed [verified].** Before the Bail Reform Act of 1984, federal defendants in non-capital cases had a statutory right to release on bail unless a judge believed they would flee; dangerousness was not a ground for detention. Most mobsters therefore walk out after arraignment on a bond, and often on property. State rules are similar. The bondsman is an institution and often a friend of the families.

**Weather (1984).** The 1984 act allows detention before trial for dangerousness, and the Supreme Court upholds it in 1987 [verified]. Before 1984, do not hold a boss without bail on the strength of his reputation.

### 6.3 Search Warrants and Wiretaps

**Fixed.** A search needs a warrant from a judge on probable cause, with exceptions. Cops without one find ways: a pretext stop, a "consent," a lie in the affidavit. Evidence found by an illegal search can be suppressed in court. A bribed or sloppy cop can make the difference at a suppression hearing.

**Fixed [verified].** The federal wiretap statute (Title III, 1968) permits electronic eavesdropping only on a judge's order, for a limited set of serious crimes, for limited periods (30 days, renewable), with a duty to "minimize" listening to irrelevant calls. Federal applications need a Justice Department sign-off before they go to the judge. Agents prefer to say that "bugs" are placed by "surreptitious entry," which is a burglary with a warrant. Illegal bugs and taps (the "black bag" jobs of earlier years) are discredited after the Church hearings and are now rarer and more dangerous to a case.

**Fixed [verified].** An informant may carry a transmitter or recorder, and agents may testify to what they hear, without a warrant (*United States v. White*, decided 5 April 1971, six to three). An informant with a body wire is therefore legal in federal court, and is the main way a family is broken from within. State law may differ. New Jersey's wiretap act (1968) **allows recording with the consent of one party to the conversation (N.J.S.A. 2A:156A-4) [verified in outline, Part VI pass]**.

**Fixed [verified].** A pen register (a record of numbers dialed) needs no warrant after *Smith v. Maryland* (June 1979). Before then its status is murkier. Phone records can otherwise be had by subpoena.

**Tendency.** Mob men know the phones are listened to. They speak in code, use pay phones, meet in parks, walk and talk in motion, and write on paper that goes into the toilet or the oven. The talk of bugs is a daily fear. Careful bosses leave the house to speak.

### 6.4 Grand Juries, Immunity, and Contempt

**Fixed [memory].** The grand jury hears witnesses in secret and issues indictments. A witness may plead the Fifth Amendment unless granted immunity, and a witness who refuses to testify after immunity can be jailed for civil contempt until he talks, until the grand jury's term ends, or for 18 months at most, whichever comes first **[verified, 28 U.S.C. 1826, Part VI pass]**. Mobsters often prefer a year in jail to testifying, and are regarded with respect when they do it. A man who takes immunity and then talks is feared.

### 6.5 Plea Bargaining and "Reaching the Box"

**Fixed.** Most cases end in a plea. Defense lawyers bargain a lesser charge and a short sentence, and the best lawyers are known to the prosecutors on both sides. A made man typically refuses to plead if it requires naming others. A man who "takes the box" (testifies for the government) in 1975 is a rarity.

### 6.6 RICO

**Fixed [verified].** The RICO statute was enacted in October 1970, but it was used little in its early years: one secondary account says it "remained largely unused until 1975" and was used about 200 times by the late 1970s. **RICO convictions came early but were few. In *United States v. Parness* a jury convicted under the statute in October 1973, affirmed in 1974 [verified, 503 F.2d 430 (2d Cir.)]. A case against a New York longshoremen's union leader (*United States v. Scotto*) was brought in the Southern District of New York in September 1979 [verified in outline, a legal-history account, Part VI pass]. Use against Mafia leadership came later.** The first major Mafia leadership case is the Commission case, **indicted on 25 February 1985 and tried in 1986, with verdicts on 19 November 1986 [verified, Part VI pass]**. Before the late 1970s prosecutors prefer older tools: gambling, extortion, tax evasion, narcotics conspiracy, Hobbs Act, interstate theft, labor racketeering.

**Weather (late 1970s to 1985).** RICO moves from the law books to the front line. A prosecutor who can show an "enterprise" and a "pattern" can charge an entire family for twenty years of crime in a single indictment, with forfeiture and long sentences.

### 6.7 Witness Protection

**Fixed [verified].** WITSEC is authorized in 1970 and begins in 1971, run by the U.S. Marshals. A man who flips can be moved with a new name. The program is real in 1975 and is a strong inducement to a man facing long prison, though it is untested in a mob context at scale until the late 1970s.

### 6.8 Forensics

**Fixed.** The tools of 1975: fingerprints, ballistics, blood typing, hair and fiber comparison, handwriting, tool marks, photographs, autopsies, and the Medical Examiner's findings. There is **no DNA** testing [memory: forensic DNA profiling arrives in 1986 and reaches courts from 1987]. Evidence is gathered with wide variation in care. Contamination, lost evidence, and sloppy crime scenes are common. A body in water or a fire can destroy much. A clean killing with a stolen gun, a plain car, gloves, and no witnesses can go unsolved forever, and many do. The Medical Examiner's office is a respectable institution with a political head.

---

## 7. Informants, Cooperators, and Undercover Agents

### 7.1 Informants

**Tendency.** Every agency has informants: junkies, bar owners, bookmakers, wives, cousins, small crooks with a case. They give cops and agents tips, and rarely much more. A soldier or associate who is arrested and frightened can talk. The risk of being killed for informing is real, the world's culture treats it as the worst crime, and so informants are rarely the made men who see everything.

**Fixed [verified, Part VI pass].** The FBI's Top Echelon Criminal Informant Program began in 1961, when Hoover told every Special Agent in Charge to develop live sources "within the upper echelon of the organized hoodlum element". It ran until 1978, when the Bureau replaced it with a general Criminal Informant Program. The first Attorney General guidelines on informants came in 1976, and suitability reviews follow from them. In 1975 a Saint Jude agent may therefore have a source near the top, and the Bureau will not share him (1.2).

**Fixed [memory].** The government has had a few big cooperators, most famously Joseph Valachi (1963). Their testimony revealed the existence of the Cosa Nostra to the public but did not by itself break the families.

**Weather (1977 to 1985).** Cooperation by high-ranking men becomes more frequent. A West Coast acting boss turns informant in 1977 and enters WITSEC in 1980 [verified]. By the mid-1980s the Omertà that held the families is visibly weakening, and prosecutors have learned how to build a case from the inside. This should feel like a slow change in climate, not a switch.

### 7.2 Undercover Agents

**Fixed [verified].** The FBI had avoided undercover work among the Mafia under Hoover. After he dies, the Bureau begins to use it. A famous operation puts an agent inside a New York family from September 1976 to July 1981 [verified]. In Saint Jude this is **Weather**: such an operation, if it occurs, begins no earlier than 1976, and the Storyteller should treat it as rare and hard. A man must be proposed and vouched for, he must show he can "earn," and the family's suspicion of outsiders is at its keenest.

### 7.3 The Honest Question of the Rat

**Tendency.** When a man is suspected of informing, the family does not usually act on suspicion alone. It watches, tests, and sometimes feeds him false information to see where it goes. A wrongful killing of a loyal man for suspicion is a tragedy that happens, and it can break a crew.

---

## 8. Prisons and the Courts' Aftermath

**Fixed.** The prison system has several levels. The county jail holds the unsentenced and short sentences; it is dirty, overcrowded, and dangerous. The federal detention center downtown holds federal defendants awaiting trial (name **Open**). State prison (the **Ironhaven** complex upstate) holds state felons for long terms. Federal prisoners are sent to penitentiaries and lower-security camps in other states (Lewisburg, Atlanta, Leavenworth, Danbury, and others). **Opening years [verified, Part VI pass]: Atlanta 1902, Leavenworth 1903, Lewisburg 1932, Danbury 1940, Marion 1963 (built to replace Alcatraz). Lewisburg was criticized by a federal judge in 1976 for inmate violence, so it can be written as a rough place in 1975. Marion is a penitentiary in 1975; its maximum-security area is described by 1979 and its supermax-style lockdown dates from 1983, so do not write it as the end of the line before 1978. Danbury held both men and women in the 1970s and is a lower-security place; its exact security class in 1975 was not found. Which mobster does time where is Open.**

**Tendency.** Prison is part of life for many in the underworld. A made man doing time expects his family looked after (obligation 2 in section 0.3), expects deference from other prisoners, and often finds that old and new friends are there. Prison hierarchy follows race and neighborhood. Mob men live relatively well inside if they have money. Time inside can cost a man his place outside, because somebody else takes his job and his money. It can also make his name.

**Fixed.** Parole is available in many cases. A **Parole Commission** reorganized in May 1976 [verified] sets release dates by guideline. A man out on parole can be sent back for associating with criminals. This is a real constraint on mobsters, who are forbidden by their parole terms to meet each other.

**Open.** Capital punishment in the state. The U.S. Supreme Court suspended executions in 1972 and allowed new statutes in 1976 [memory]. **[verified, Part VI pass] New Jersey's death penalty was under a de facto ban from 1972, was reinstated in 1982, and was abolished on 17 December 2007. That no one has been executed since 1963 is now [verified]: Ralph Hudson, executed on 22 January 1963, was the last.** The five-year run may give a reason to treat it differently. Mob killings are overwhelmingly prosecuted as ordinary murder.

---

## 9. Law Weather, 1975 to 1990

Dated conditions, not plots. Each has a window and a strength. The Storyteller consults the calendar and does not apply a condition before its window.

| Window | Condition | Strength | Tag |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1975 to 1976 | Federal scandal over intelligence abuses (the Senate "Church Committee", formed January 1975, report April 1976). Bureau cautious about bugs and informants | moderate | [verified] |
| 1976 | New Attorney General guidelines for FBI domestic security investigations (6 April) and for the use of informants (15 December) | moderate | [verified] |
| 1975 to 1977 | City fiscal crisis. SJPD hiring freeze and layoffs, morale low, Pad pressure | strong | design |
| 1976 onward | Undercover work against the Mafia becomes possible | rising | [verified] |
| 1977 | First major cooperation by a top-ranked mobster (not in Saint Jude, but its news travels) | strong | [verified] |
| Late 1970s | Strike Force program criticized for rivalry and weak results | moderate | [verified in outline] |
| 1978 to 1980 | A sting (Abscam) begins in March 1978 and becomes public in early February 1980; congressional convictions follow in 1981 | moderate | [verified] |
| 1978 onward | RICO begins to be used against organized crime | rising | [verified] |
| 1979 | Pen registers need no warrant | fixed after | [verified] |
| 1980 onward | WITSEC numbers grow; more men weigh it | rising | [verified] |
| 1980 onward | Cocaine changes the drug market, and the DEA pressure rises. Crack follows in Northeastern cities from about 1984 to 1985, with its boom in 1985 to 1987 (Part V) | strong | [memory, to verify] |
| 1984 | Bail Reform Act, forfeiture, tougher sentences | strong | [verified for bail] |
| 1985 to 1986 | First major prosecution of a Commission by RICO **(indicted February 1985, verdicts November 1986)** | strong | [verified] |
| 1987 | Detention without bail upheld | fixed after | [verified] |

**Open.** Which of these touch Saint Jude directly, and what the city's own scandals are.

---

## 10. Open Items for Part II

**Open.** The exact title of the commission that investigated the department, the name of the Police Commissioner, the SAC, the Strike Force chief, the U.S. Attorney, the County Prosecutor, the Marsh County Prosecutor, the State Attorney General, and the Medical Examiner are all unfixed. The Storyteller creates them when needed. If an example is needed for tone, **Victor Hallam** is the SAC of the Saint Jude field office in the codex's version, and he may be used or replaced.

**Still to verify.** **(New Jersey's one-party consent is now checked, 6.3.)** **Which prison holds which type of inmate in 1975 (security classes, apart from the opening years); earlier RICO appeals before 1978; Miranda, the rights to counsel and the older interrogation practices in 6.1 (general knowledge)**. Title III's court order, 30 days, minimization and Justice Department authorization, *United States v. White*, the DEA (1 July 1973), the Parole Commission (May 1976), the Church Committee, the 1976 Levi guidelines **(6 April and 15 December 1976, from the Justice Department Inspector General's report)**, Abscam **(began March 1978, public early 1980, convictions 1981)**, **the Top Echelon program (1961 to 1978), the opening years of Atlanta, Leavenworth, Lewisburg, Danbury and Marion, ERISA (2 September 1974), the 18-month contempt limit (28 U.S.C. 1826), New Jersey's last execution (22 January 1963) and the Commission case dates (indicted 25 February 1985, verdicts 19 November 1986)** were checked against sources.

**Anchors used:** Nixon's resignation (August 1974), Roe v. Wade (1973), Knapp-type commission history (1970 to 1972), the Waterfront Commission model, RICO (1970), WITSEC (1971), pen registers (1979), the Bail Reform Act (1984), Pistone's undercover period (1976 to 1981), the first mobster cooperation of rank (1977).


# Saint Jude: The Setting Bible

## Part III: The Underworld

Baseline: the start of 1975. Section 0 of Part II (the Period Mind) governs everything here. Dated items after 1975 are **Weather**.

---

## 0. How to Read This Part

**Fixed / Open.** Everything in this Part describes the underworld as an institution, with its customs, ranks, money, and the shape of its habits. It does not decide what any man will do. Nothing here is a plot, a secret, or a pressure. Those are born in the simulation from men and circumstance.

**The codex is changeable.** The names, ages, secrets, and stories in the old codex are source material, not canon. Any of them may be changed, dropped, reversed, or invented again. The suggested starting seats in section 4.6 are suggestions only. A man named there can turn out to be an honest man, a fool, a coward, a saint, or dead in 1974.

**Tendencies, not rules.** Where this Part says "the Family does X," it means the Family usually does X and individual men, bosses, and moments will vary. A custom that has a name can still be broken. The consequences of breaking it are the interesting part, and section 9 sets them out.

**Sizes and sums are design estimates.** Membership figures, tribute rates, and prices are tagged **design** and are meant to be changed. Where a fact about the real world is used, it is tagged **[verified]** or **[memory]** as in Part II.

**A note on scale.** The old codex gave four families a combined 265 made men. The largest real families, in New York, had a few hundred members each, and a city of about a million (Philadelphia, say) held one family of roughly a hundred. Four families in a city of 1.1 million (2.4 million metro) with a major port is a stretch. This Part therefore sets the four families at **30 to 60 made men each**, **about 180 to 190 in all (see 3.2)**. Even so, that is generous. The port, the rail yards, and the highways justify it, and the four are described as small, crowded, and quarrelsome because of it. If you want them bigger, say so.

**A note on names.** The old codex's Castellano and Moretti families are renamed **Pellegrino** and **Ferrante**, because Castellano is the name of a real Gambino boss (Paul Castellano, boss from November 1976 until he was shot on 16 December 1985 [verified]) and Moretti is the name of a real New Jersey mobster of the 1940s and early 1950s. The old names may survive in the codex and the repo, and Code needs to carry the rename through. Pellegrino means "pilgrim," which suits a family of the Saint Jude docks.

---

## 1. The Thing

### 1.1 What It Is Called

**Fixed.** The organization has many names, and which one a man uses tells you who he is.

- **"This thing of ours"** (*questa cosa nostra*, *la cosa nostra*, *cosa nostra*, "our thing"). What the members call it among themselves. The public learned the name *La Cosa Nostra* from the testimony of Joseph Valachi in 1963 [memory]. Members who dislike the publicity still use the phrase in private.
- **"The Family"** or **"the Life."** What the men inside call the local organization and the way of life.
- **"The outfit,"** **"the combination,"** **"the mob,"** **"the syndicate."** What outsiders, newspapers, and the old Irish call it.
- **"The Mafia."** A word used mostly by outsiders, the press, and the police. Italian-Americans of the older generation bristle at it as an insult to the whole people. Made men do not use it for themselves.

**Fixed [memory].** Two phrases carry the whole social structure. A made man is introduced to another made man as **"a friend of ours"** (*amico nostro*). An associate who is vouched for but not made is introduced as **"a friend of mine."** A man who cannot be introduced either way is nobody to the Life. The distinction is so sharp that a man will stand in a room with ten friends of his and not be allowed to speak when "a friend of ours" walks in.

### 1.2 What It Is

**Fixed.** The Family is an inherited body of obligation, held together by blood, oath, fear, money, and the Church. It is not a corporation. It has no written constitution, no payroll, and no headquarters. It is a network of men bound to one another and to a head, who in turn is bound to his men.

**The Father.** A man's Family is, in the Life's own telling, his second family and, for some men, the first. The Boss is the **father** of it. This is accurate as the Life describes itself [memory]:

- He is called "the Boss," "the old man," "the Don," or by his surname with a "Mr.", and he is treated with filial deference.
- He settles quarrels as a father does, between brothers, and his ruling is accepted.
- He gives to those who are in need. He sends the doctor when a man's wife is ill, pays for the funeral, finds the job for the son, lends the money, and stands godfather to the child.
- In return he expects obedience, silence, a share of the earnings, and a man's life if it is asked.
- Both halves are fatherhood to the men inside. The man who feeds your mother in her old age is the same man who may one day order you killed. Neither act is thought to cancel the other.

Real men differ in how much of this they feel. A hard-headed capo may regard the language as a tool for controlling young men. A young soldier may weep at the boss's funeral. The Storyteller shows both, and does not tell the player which is the truth.

**The Brotherhood.** Among the made men, the bond is that of brothers. Each is "a man of honor" or "a man of respect" (the older phrases, *uomo d'onore* and *uomo di rispetto*), and all owe each other fidelity. In practice there is also envy, rivalry, debt, and grievance. "Brothers" is a word for the duty, not for the feeling.

### 1.3 Honor and Money

**Tendency.** The romantic version and the real one are both true, and they share the same men.

The romantic version holds that the Life is a code: loyalty, silence, respect for elders, the protection of the weak in one's neighborhood, justice done outside a corrupt court, a man's word as law, no hands on women and children, and the dignity of a man who needs nothing from the state. It has a literature (*The Godfather*, the novel of 1969 and the film of March 1972, with *Part II* released in December 1974 [memory]), and its heroes are the men of the old school who are said to have lived by it.

The real version holds that the Life is a business of extortion, gambling, loan-sharking, theft, and murder, that the code is applied when convenient, and that most men are in it for the money and the status.

**Fixed.** *Both* sit inside the same man. A capo can weep at a wake and have the dead man's brother's legs broken the following week. A young soldier can believe in the old code with a whole heart and still skim from his crew. A boss can keep faith with a dying friend and cut a rival's throat. The Storyteller never reduces any man to the cynic or the believer, and lets the player discover which obligations that particular man actually keeps.

### 1.4 The Godfather in the World

**Fixed [memory].** By 1975, *The Godfather* (the book, the film, and now its sequel) is part of the world, and everyone has seen it. This matters to how the Life behaves.

- **Older Sicilian-born men** (the "Mustache Petes," in the American slang) mostly dismiss the film as theater, though many saw it three times.
- **Younger Italian-Americans**, especially in the 1970s and 1980s, borrow its style. Black coats, the phrase "an offer he can't refuse," the kiss on the cheek, and the grave courtesy of the "Don" are all, in part, imitations of an imitation.
- **Real mobsters** are on record saying the film is wrong about nearly everything (the dignity, the family loyalty, the refusal of narcotics) and also saying they loved it [memory]. Cade's romantic version is therefore part of the world as well as part of the game: men of the Life *want* to be what the film shows them.
- **The police and the press** use the film as shorthand and are often misled by it.

The Storyteller can use all of this. A young soldier can try to be Michael Corleone in a restaurant and be laughed at by an old man. An old man can hold the same courtesy that the film borrowed from men like him.

---

## 2. Courtesy and Ceremony

The romance lives here. The Storyteller should write these scenes with weight and care, since they are the scenes players remember.

### 2.1 Deference

**Tendency [memory].** In the older families, deference to a boss and to an elder is real and physical:

- **The greeting.** A man approaches a boss with his head slightly bowed and takes the boss's hand in both of his, or kisses it, or kisses the boss on the cheek. The old Sicilian formula is *Bacio le mani*, "I kiss your hands," said to an elder in sign of respect. Kissing a ring is the film's invention, but an old man with a ring who allows his hand to be kissed fits the period, and the Storyteller may stage it for an old-school boss where it suits the man. A modern boss will not tolerate it and will pull the hand away.
- **The standing.** Men stand when the boss enters. They do not sit until he sits. They do not speak until spoken to, and they answer questions and do not volunteer opinions. A man who interrupts a boss is giving offence.
- **The seating.** The boss sits at the head or with his back to the wall. The underboss and the consigliere sit near him. The captains by seniority. The soldiers at the lower end, or stand.
- **The title.** "Don" is used by Sicilian-born men and by those who want the tone. "The boss" or "the old man" is more common. A man in conversation with another family's boss uses "Mr." and the surname, or "the gentleman," and never a nickname to his face.
- **The silence.** A man does not speak of the Family in front of women, children, or strangers. He does not use names on the telephone. The common phrases are *that thing*, *our friend*, *the gentleman*.

### 2.2 Gifts, Favors, and the Ledger of Obligation

**Tendency.** The Life runs on favors. A favor given to a man puts him in your debt, and a debt is remembered. A boss's favor is the greatest bond of all, because it can be called in with a life. The Life has no written contracts. It has a memory.

- **The envelope.** A gift of money at a wedding, a christening, a funeral, a Christmas, or a man's coming out of prison. It is a mark of respect and often a payment.
- **The sit-down at the table.** Being invited to a boss's Sunday dinner is a sign of rising favor. Being excluded is a sign of falling favor.
- **Asking permission.** A younger man asks his sponsor, his capo, or his boss before he marries, before he moves house, before he starts a business, and before he changes his job. The asking itself is the courtesy. The answer is nearly always yes.

### 2.3 Godparents

**Fixed.** The Catholic bond of the *compare* and *comare* (godfather and godmother) is real and strong. The relationship between a man and the sponsor of his child is spiritual kinship, and it is used. A boss is often godfather to the sons of his captains. A made man's sponsor is called his *compare* (a different use of the same word), and is bound to the recruit for life. Betraying one's *compare* is the worst betrayal in the Life.

### 2.4 Feasts and Funerals

**Fixed.** The Life shows itself publicly at feasts and funerals.

- **The Feast of San Rocco** (16 August), the Hill's great procession, with dollar bills pinned to the saint's statue, bands, a stage, food stalls, and the families' men visible in the crowd [memory: feast processions with money pinned to the statue are a real Italian-American custom]. Bosses and politicians attend and are seen to attend.
- **The Table of Saint Joseph** (19 March), the Sicilian feast of the poor, with altars of bread and food (see Part I).
- **The wake.** A made man's wake lasts two or three days at the funeral parlor, with a long procession of men, floral arrangements shaped like the dead man's trade, a priest saying the rosary, and other families' bosses paying their respects. The wake is also neutral ground, and a place where the Life's politics are carried on in murmurs. An enemy comes to a wake and kisses the widow's hand.
- **The funeral Mass and burial.** The Archdiocese decides case by case (Part I, section 10).

### 2.5 The Making

**Fixed.** To be "made" is to be inducted as a member of the Family. In the Life's own telling, there are four things a man must be: Italian by his father's blood (the usual rule; some families insist on both parents, and individual cases are **Open**), proposed by a sponsor, proven in the Family's work, and approved by the Boss. The Family votes in the sense that the Boss consults his administration, and a single capo's objection can sink a man.

**Tendency [memory].** The ceremony, as reported by Valachi and by later witnesses, runs along these lines. Details differ between families.

- It takes place in a private room, with the Boss, the Underboss, the Consigliere, and some captains present, and the sponsor beside the candidate.
- The candidate has been told only that he is going to meet "some friends," and learns what is about to happen only when it begins.
- The Boss explains what the Family is. He tells him the rules, and says that once in, a man leaves only by dying ("blood in, blood out" is a modern phrase; Valachi reported *I enter alive and I will have to get out dead* [memory]).
- The Boss pricks the candidate's trigger finger and lets the blood fall on a holy card of **Saint Jude, patron of lost causes and of the city**. The card is set alight, passed between the candidate's cupped hands, and he swears, in dialect or in English, that he will burn like the saint if he betrays the Family.
- A gun and a knife may lie on the table, symbols of the life he will lead.
- He is told that the Family comes before his own blood family, his wife, and his faith. In practice the Church and the mother remain in first place, and the Boss knows it.
- He kisses the Boss, and the others, and is introduced to the room as "a friend of ours."

**Tendency.** In the older families, a man was expected to have "made his bones" (killed a man for the Family) before he was made. This was never universal and became less so, and the practice is **Open** by family. After the FBI's use of undercover agents begins (Weather, section 13), the rule is revived in some families as a defense, since an agent cannot be allowed to commit murder [memory].

**Tendency.** The books, the lists on which a family may add new members, are not always open. After Apalachin in 1957, many families closed their books for years. In February 1976 the heads of New York's five families agreed to admit new members for the first time in about two decades. Carlo Gambino opposed it, fearing informants, and was outvoted four to one; each family was to make ten [verified: an FBI memo of 17 February 1976, reported in the press]. Other cities follow the trend in their own time, as the old men die and the ranks thin. Whether a Saint Jude book is open in a given year is **Open**, a thing the simulation decides. The codex put the ceremony around the Feast of San Rocco, and that is a pleasing local custom.

### 2.6 The Sit-Down

**Fixed.** A sit-down is a meeting to settle a dispute. The parties come under a guarantee of safety, meet on neutral ground, state their grievances in turn, and accept a ruling from the persons present. It can be called by anyone with standing, and refused only at risk. Its rules are well known to everyone in the Life:

- Who calls it names the place, and the other side accepts or names another.
- No one comes armed to the table, though the cars outside hold weapons.
- A ruling is binding, and breaking it after the sit-down is a worse offence than the quarrel.
- A man who cannot be satisfied may ask for a ruling from a higher table (his boss, then the other boss).
- A sit-down also serves as a way to lure a man, and everyone knows it, so the guarantee of safety is only as good as the host's reputation.

The neutral grounds in Saint Jude are listed in section 6.

---

## 3. Structure, Ranks, and Real Power

### 3.1 The Ladder

**Fixed [memory].** Every family has the same structure.

| Rank | Also called | Role |
|---|---|---|
| **Boss** | the old man, the Don, the head, "Mr. X" | Final authority. Settles disputes, approves inductions and killings, receives a share from every crew. Often insulated from the street. |
| **Underboss** | *sotto capo*, the number two | The boss's deputy for operations. Runs the captains, carries orders down, and often has the family's violence at his disposal. |
| **Consigliere** | the counselor, the adviser | The elder statesman. Has no crew of his own. Advises the boss, represents the family at sit-downs, and mediates. |
| **Capo** | *caporegime*, captain, skipper | Runs a regime (crew) of soldiers and associates in an area or a line of business. The workhorse rank. |
| **Soldier** | made man, wiseguy, button, "a friend of ours" | An inducted member. Has standing, protection, and the right to an associate crew. |
| **Associate** | "a friend of mine," connected guy, earner | An unmade criminal attached to a made man. Does the work. No standing of his own. |

**Tendency.** Below the associates are the people who pay and do not belong: bookies who rent protection, shopkeepers who pay a "tax," cousins who drive, girlfriends who carry, bartenders who look away. They number in the thousands and are the Life's real surface.

**Open.** The names, the exact numbers, and whether a family has a formal Underboss or Consigliere at any given date. Men die and the seats fall vacant. An **acting boss** or **street boss** may run a family while the real boss is in prison, ill, or in hiding, and a **panel** of captains may share the work [memory].

### 3.2 Sizes (Design)

**Design.** At the start of 1975, approximate made membership:

| Family | Made men | Associates per made man | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sabatini | 50 to 60, default 57 | 5 to 8 | richest, most political, oldest men |
| Pellegrino | **40 to 50, default 45. A run may push the count to 55 only by a dated cause (Part VI 9.9, item 2)** | 5 to 8 | docks and trucks; the biggest street presence |
| Ferrante | 40 to 50, default 44 | 6 to 8 | the Racks and the highways; many gunmen |
| Vannucci | 30 to 40, default 36 | 5 to 7 | smallest, hardest, most closed |

Total around **180 to 190** made men (**the four ranges sum to 160 to 200**) and roughly 1,200 to 1,500 associates. These are estimates and the simulation may move them. **The count is Open and tied to revenue (Part VI 9). The engine draws a family's count inside its range from its revenue, and a revenue shock (a lost Club contract, a docks decline, a Pick-3 hit) shrinks the count over years by attrition, not at once. Part VI's scale test does not rule out about 190 and does not prove it. The Pellegrino default is lowered because their modeled revenue is thinnest per man (Part VI 9.5). That is a design call made in the Part VI pass and **accepted by Cade**, **and a count above 50, up to 55, needs a dated cause (the table above)**. Read "associates" as people who touch a made man's business, not as people he supports.** The defaults in the table are the peaks of the engine's draw and sum to 182, inside 180 to 190. Part VI's scale test uses the range midpoints (55, 45, 45 and 35, which sum to 180). A family's strength is not its head count alone. See section 3.4.

### 3.3 Money Flows Upward

**Tendency [design].** Earning is done by associates and soldiers, and money is passed up as tribute, the "envelope." The usual lines:

- An **associate** pays his soldier a fifth to a quarter of what he earns.
- A **soldier** pays his capo roughly a sixth to a fifth of his net.
- A **capo** pays the boss a tenth to a seventh of his regime's net, and his own soldiers' kick-ups add to what he keeps.
- The **boss** pays no one, but pays for peace, lawyers, and upkeep, and receives a share of every venture the family touches.

The base is **net**, never gross. An associate who runs a numbers bank does not tithe on the money bet, only on the money left after paying winners, runners, and the Pad. (The arithmetic is in section 7.) A man who hides a score and does not kick up is "holding out," and it is a grave offence (section 9). **The Ladder shows who owes whom. It does not explain where a boss's money comes from. In the Part VI scale test the kick-ups carry only about a fifth of what a family spends (Part VI 9.6). A boss's wealth rests on rackets he runs himself: the docks, the Club, carting, the Strip, the fronts. Do not make the envelope the only source of a boss's income.**

**Fixed.** Payday is Friday. The Life keeps a week.

| Day | Business |
|---|---|
| Monday | The count. Bookies and numbers banks tally the weekend. Winners are paid by noon. |
| Tuesday | Losers settle by evening, or the interest starts to grow. |
| Wednesday, Thursday | The work. Sit-downs, collections, union visits, scores. |
