## 4. Politics and the Machine

### 4.1 What the Earlier Parts Already Assert

| Source | Assertion |
|---|---|
| Part I 4.3 | Strong-mayor government. The Mayor appoints the police commissioner, the fire chief and the heads of Buildings and Inspections, Sanitation, Public Works and Law, and sets the budget. A fifteen-member Council is elected by ward. Ward leaders control precinct captains, who control the vote. Councilmen control zoning votes, paving and licence approvals in their wards. |
| Part I 4.3 | The **Board of Public Works** has three members, two appointed by the Mayor and one elected citywide. It awards every major contract. The Zoning Board and the Buildings Department control what is built. The Licensing Board issues taxi medallions, peddler permits and entertainment licences. The State ABC Board licenses liquor. |
| Part I 4.4 | A Democratic machine has run the city for a century. It was Irish through most of the twentieth century. Italians have been rising since the 1950s, and the Black community's share is growing against stubborn resistance. Reform candidates win about one election in three. City jobs are the machine's currency. In the mid 1970s its money is running out. |
| Part I 4.6 | Mayoral elections fall in odd-numbered years on a four-year cycle, on the first Tuesday after the first Monday in November, with a party primary in September. The 1975 election is Open. |
| Part I 8.1 | The Port Authority board is appointed by the Governor and the Mayor. It has no voters, a great deal of patronage and a long record of being run for whoever controls the board. |
| Part I 12.1 | At most one woman sits on the Council. No woman has been Mayor, police commissioner, County Prosecutor or ward leader of a major ward. |
| Part II 2.6, 3 | The Dolan Commission (1970 to 1972) and the reforms that followed. The Pad survives through intermediaries. A new commander reopens every arrangement. |
| Part II 4.5 | The State Commission of Investigation can subpoena and hold public hearings but cannot prosecute. |
| Part III 4.1 | The Sabatini bosses have been politicians for two generations, with the most friends in City Hall, the Buildings Department and the courts. |
| Part III 2.4, 6.4 | Bosses and politicians attend the Feast of San Rocco and are seen to attend. Funeral homes and wakes are neutral ground. |
| Section 0.4 | The Governor appoints Superior Court judges and County Prosecutors. |

### 4.2 How the Machine Works

**Fixed (Part I 4.4).** The machine is a chain of favors that ends in votes. At the top stands a **county party chairman** and the county committee. Below them are the **ward leaders**, one for each of the fifteen wards. Below those are the **precinct captains** (the committeemen of the election districts). Each level owes the one above it turnout and holds a list of jobs to give.

**The shape of it (Tendency).**

- **The county chairman.** The party's working head, who may or may not hold public office. He decides slates, controls the party's money and treasury, and speaks to the Governor's counsel on appointments (section 0.4). Who he is and what faction he comes from is **Open**.
- **The ward leader.** Often a councilman or the man who picks one. He runs a ward club (a storefront with a name on the glass, an office, a bar or a card room), holds office hours, and settles the ward's troubles: a job, a licence, a ticket, a landlord, a funeral, a turkey at Thanksgiving. His power is the list of people who owe him.
- **The precinct captain.** Knows every household in a few city blocks, who is sick, who is out of work, who is feuding, who will vote and who needs a ride. In New Jersey each election district has a committeeman and a committeewoman [memory, to verify], so the women of the machine hold real posts at the bottom of the ladder and are kept off the rungs above it (Part I 12.4, route 1).
- **The line.** On the primary ballot the party organization's endorsed candidates stand together in a favored position, and an independent must run off it [memory, to verify]. The line is worth more than a platform.
- **Election Day.** Captains pay "street money" to the workers who get voters out. Old and sick voters are helped to the booth or the absentee ballot. Machines are lever machines. The county board that runs the election is appointed by the Governor [memory, to verify for 1975]. Most of this is ordinary and legal, and part is not, and the line between them moves with the neighborhood.

**Tendency.** The machine is not the mob. It is a patronage organization that needs money, votes, jobs to give and a quiet city. The families are one of its customers and one of its suppliers. A ward leader may take a family's money and refuse a family's request. A family may give to every ward in a district and fear none of them. Each side uses the other and each side knows the other will leave when it must.

### 4.3 The Factions

**Tendency (Period Mind, Part II 0.2).** The machine is not one voice. It is a set of ethnic and occupational wings that cooperate when they must and distrust each other when they can. The prejudices are real and are spoken aloud, and individuals vary within them (Part II 0.3).

| Wing | Base | Tools | Wants | Edges |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Irish old guard** | Kerrytown wards, the firehouses, the PBA, the parish and the Archdiocese | A century of seniority, the party treasury, the police and fire jobs | To keep the Mayor's office, the chairmanship and the key departments | Shrinking numbers. It regards the Italians as upstarts and the Black wing as a danger. |
| **Italian wing** | The Hill wards, the building trades, contractors, the Italian Credit Union | Votes, labor, campaign money, the Sabatini family's friends and a growing bench of lawyers | The Board of Public Works, more judgeships, a share of the Mayor's appointments and the respect it has been denied since the 1950s (Part I 4.4) | Held back by the Irish. The old Bellacorte families look down on it, and it looks down on them (Part I 5.4). |
| **Black wing** | The Eastside wards, Mount Zion Baptist, Bethel A.M.E., the tenant councils, the *Courier* | Votes, pastors who can deliver thousands of them (Part I 10.3), and the federal and state money for housing, health and jobs that passes through Black institutions (Part I 5.6) | Power in proportion to 30 percent of the city. **Design:** three or four of fifteen Council seats | Resisted by the white wings. Divided among ministers, younger activists and the numbers money (Part III 5.2). |
| **Polish and Eastern European Democrats** | Kosciuszko Park, the Flats, the Steelworkers' halls | Reliable votes, union men, nuns, cops | Jobs for its sons, the schools kept as they are | Conservative on school desegregation. It supplies voters and seldom the top seats. |
| **Liberal and reform Democrats** | Greenwood, Fenwick, parts of the Heights, the League of Women Voters, the *Chronicle*'s editorial page | Money, lawyers, the press, the State Commission and the U.S. Attorney | Clean contracting, a professional police and a merit civil service | Few precinct workers. It wins when the machine splits. |
| **Business establishment** | The Saint Jude Club, the Chamber of Commerce, the banks, the Heights and Oakridge | Money, bond rating, the newspapers | A solvent city, low taxes, building downtown | Mostly Republican. It funds reformers when the machine seems to threaten the city's credit. |
| **Smaller blocs** | Greeks (Sixth Ward), Portuguese (the Point), Puerto Ricans (the Eastside and the Point), Chinese (the Docks) | A ward, a bloc of votes, a church or association | Recognition and a first seat | Puerto Rican politics is only beginning to claim seats (Open). |
| **Labor within the machine** | PBA, firefighters, building trades, Teamsters, sanitation | Endorsements, volunteers, pension lobbying, legal funds | Contracts, no layoffs, no oversight | Divided among themselves, and the layoffs of 1975 to 1977 put them in open conflict with the Mayor (section 6). |

### 4.4 The Wards (Design sketch)

**Fixed.** There are fifteen wards (Part I 4.3). The **Sixth Ward** is the old Greek, Lebanese and Armenian quarter near the Basin (Part I 6.4). Each ward holds about 70,000 people.

**Design.** The remaining ward lines are **Open**. As a sketch only: three wards on the Hill (North Hill, South Hill, the East Slope with Mercer Avenue), two in Kerrytown and the South Piers, two Black wards on the Eastside, one Eastside ward for the Racks, the Rail Yards and the Puerto Rican rooming houses, one for the Point and Pescadores Row, one for Crown Harbor and Bellacorte, the Sixth Ward, one for the Flats and Ironworks, one for Kosciuszko Park and the Greenwood line, one for the Heights and Fenwick, and one for Brighton Point. That makes fifteen. The Storyteller may redraw any line except the Sixth.

**Tendency.** A councilman holds his ward as a fief, and the Council defers to him on what happens inside it (a custom often called councilmanic courtesy [memory]). He decides which variance passes, which street is paved, which tavern gets its licence and who gets the sanitation job. A man who crosses a councilman in his own ward learns how long the courtesy runs.

### 4.5 The Mayor and the Departments

**Fixed (Part I 4.3).** The Mayor is the machine's head in practice. He appoints the police commissioner, the fire chief, and the heads of Buildings, Sanitation, Public Works and Law, and two of the three members of the Board of Public Works. He sets the budget and sends it to the Council.

**Tendency.** A mayor who is the machine's creature uses these powers to pay its debts. A mayor who came up against the machine uses them to break it, and finds how little he controls: the Council holds his zoning votes, the courts and the Prosecutor belong to the Governor's appointments, the Port Authority is shared with the Governor, and the unions can stop the city. The police commissioner is the Mayor's man (Part II 2.1), and a new commissioner reopens every arrangement under the Pad (Part II 3.4). The Mayor's real discretion is where he spends his appointments and what he lets his commissioners do.

**Open.** Who the Mayor is in January 1975, whether he is the man who created the Dolan Commission, whether he is running again, and what he owes.

### 4.6 The Board of Public Works

**Fixed (Part I 4.3).** Three members. Two are appointed by the Mayor and one is elected citywide. The Board awards every major contract: dredging, bridge repair, paving, school construction. This is where the Concrete Club and its competitors do business.

**What the Board decides (Tendency).**

- **The specification.** Before bids, an engineer writes what is to be built. A specification that fits one firm's equipment, one supplier's product or one contractor's experience limits the field before a bid is made. The staff, not the Board, often writes the spec, and a spec writer is a seat worth having.
- **The bid.** The job is advertised and sealed bids are opened. The law requires award to the lowest responsible bidder [memory, to verify], and "responsible" is the soft word (3.6).
- **Change orders.** The contractor wins at a low price and is paid more as the job changes. Change orders are where a low bid is made whole, and where the Board's staff and the contractor share a secret.
- **Emergency contracts.** When a bridge, a school boiler or a main fails, the Board may skip bidding for urgent work [memory, to verify]. Deferred maintenance (Part I 4.5, 8.2) produces emergencies, and emergencies produce no-bid contracts.
- **Pre-qualification and bonding.** The Board keeps a list of firms it considers fit to bid, and demands bonds (3.6, 5).

**The elected member (Tendency).** The third member is not controlled by the Mayor. He cannot outvote the other two. He can demand records, ask questions in public, dissent, leak to a newspaper and give a reform campaign a place to stand. The seat is the machine's pressure valve and the reformers' pulpit. **Open:** the member's election cycle, who holds it, and what he has seen.

**Audit.** New Jersey municipalities are audited each year by an accountant they themselves hire [memory, to verify]. The man who audits the Board's books is chosen by the city that owns them. The audit rarely finds what the Board does not wish found.

### 4.7 Zoning, Buildings and Licensing

**Zoning (Fixed, Part I 4.3).** The Zoning Board decides variances and the Council votes on rezoning. Rezoning is a councilman's gift in his ward. A variance is a lawyer's art: the petition, the hearing, the neighbors, the "hardship." The same few lawyers appear again and again, and an **expediter** (a man who knows who must be asked) is a trade.

**Buildings and Inspections (Fixed).** An inspector can close a job site in an hour. Inspectors are poorly paid, and the sum that makes one hurry or look away is small beside what a delay costs a contractor. The great majority are honest. Whether a given inspector is bought or merely afraid is often hard to tell from outside.

**The Licensing Board (Fixed).** Taxi medallions (about 3,500, Part I 8.3), peddlers' permits, and entertainment, dance, amusement and adult-business licences. A medallion is an asset that can be sold, and the Board's discretion makes it a favor. The loosening of obscenity law through the 1970s (Part I 5.5) multiplies the licences the Strip's operators want.

**Liquor (Part I 4.2).** The State ABC Board licenses every bar. In practice a bar owner needs the blessing of his councilman, the tolerance of his precinct and a clean enough record, and the ABC Board can revoke a licence for tax fraud or gambling (Part II 4.6).

**Tendency.** Each of these offices sells speed more than outcome. A permit that would be granted in a year is granted in a month, and the month costs money. A reformer's first discovery is that most of the corruption is in the delay.

### 4.8 Judges, the County Prosecutor and the Governor

**Fixed (section 0.4).** The Governor appoints Superior Court judges and County Prosecutors with the Senate's consent [verified]. A judge serves an initial seven years and, if reappointed, holds tenure until 70 [verified].

**The path to a judgeship (Tendency).** A lawyer with a practice and a party record comes to the county chairman's attention. The chairman, the state senators from the county's districts and the county bar all weigh in with the Governor's counsel. The lawyer is a machine man, a respected trial lawyer, a prosecutor's assistant, a friend of the right ward leader, or all of these. At the first reappointment, seven years later, the judge is still within reach of the same people. After tenure he owes them little, and many judges are tenured and independent.

**What a friendly judge can do (Tendency).** Grant an adjournment. Set bail (Part II 6.2). Rule on a suppression motion (Part II 6.3). Pick a lenient sentence. Delay a trial until a witness forgets. The county's **assignment judge** decides which judge hears which case [memory, to verify], and the assignment is itself a power. Most judges are honest, and an honest judge who is also a machine man is the common case. A bribed judge is rare, because a judge has much to lose and little to gain from a bribe the Prosecutor might hear of.

**Municipal Court (Part I 4.3).** Traffic, misdemeanors and preliminary hearings. Its judges are appointed by the city [memory, to verify], and the court is the machine's best-known small favor: the fixed ticket, the lost summons, the easy fine. Clerks matter more than judges.

**The county row offices (Open).** New Jersey counties elect a sheriff, a county clerk and a surrogate [memory, to verify]. In Saint Jude, where city and county are one government (Part I 1.1), these are machine prizes housed in the Hall of Records. Part I does not name them. They are Open.

**The County Prosecutor (Fixed, section 0.4).** The Governor appoints him for five years [verified]. His office prosecutes murder, robbery and assault, and decides which cases of police or union corruption to bring (Part I 4.3). He can convene grand juries (Part II 6.4), and he works alongside the State Attorney General's office, which supervises prosecutors statewide [memory, to verify]. In New Jersey of this period, assistant prosecutors were often part-time and kept private law practices [memory, to verify], which means a man can prosecute by day and defend by night. Detectives on the Prosecutor's staff are a small force of their own.

**Tendency.** A Prosecutor who prosecutes the machine may not be reappointed. A Prosecutor who prosecutes well and carefully is often rewarded with a judgeship. He serves two masters, the Governor who appointed him and the county that recommended him, and he sometimes serves a third, his own ambition. He is a mixed man like any other (Part II 0.3).

**Open.** The Prosecutor, the Governor's counsel, the assignment judge, the senators from the county's districts and the county chairman are unfixed. The Storyteller creates them when needed. A Governor of the other party after January 1982 narrows the pipeline for a machine whose friends were appointed by his predecessor [memory, to verify].

### 4.9 The Port Authority Board

**Fixed (Part I 8.1).** The Killbuck River Port Authority is a public corporation. The Governor and the Mayor appoint its board. It has a salaried executive director and no voters. It owns the Gannet Terminal, the container piers, the Narrows Bridge, the Killbuck Tunnel and the airport, and runs its own police of about 600 (Part II 4.1).

**Design.** A board of nine commissioners with staggered terms, some named by the Governor and some by the Mayor. The size and the split are **Open**.

**What it controls (Tendency).**

- **Terminal leases.** Who runs the Gannet Terminal and the container piers, and on what terms. The leases bind to labor (2.7A, B).
- **Construction and maintenance contracts.** Bridge, tunnel, terminal and airport work, on a scale larger than the city's.
- **Jobs.** Police, toll collectors, clerks and engineers, in great number. Tolls are collected in cash by the Authority's own collectors (Part I 8.2), and a toll booth is both a machine job and a skim risk.
- **Concessions.** Airport parking, restaurants, ground transport.
- **Bonds.** The Authority issues bonds against tolls and rents. Underwriters, bond counsel and financial advisers are paid on each issue (6.4).

**Tendency.** The Authority is the only place where the Governor, the Mayor and the families all have a stake, and none of them answers to a voter. Control of the board is a quiet fight that decides the port's future more than any election.

**Anchor (offstage, Part I 8.1).** The real Port Authority of New York and New Jersey is the model. In Saint Jude's world it is a sister body, and the real one does not operate in the city.

### 4.10 Campaign Money and Patronage

**Where the money comes from (Tendency).** Contractors who want city work. Unions with a contract at stake. Lawyers who appear before the Zoning Board. Banks and bond firms. Developers. Liquor distributors, vendors and licensees. Men who want a favor in the future. And, through fronts and friends, the families.

**How it travels (Tendency).** Testimonial dinners for a ward leader or judge. Advertisements in the souvenir journal printed for the dinner, which cost more than any advertisement is worth. Cash in an envelope. Tickets sold to the contractors in a ward. A donation to the feast committee or the parish. Legal limits and reporting rules exist (the State's campaign reporting law of 1973 and the federal law of 1971 and 1974 [memory, to verify]), and in a ward race few are checked.

**What it buys (Tendency).** A hearing. A seat at the table when the Board decides. A phone call to an inspector. A judge's attention. A job for a cousin. And, above all, the right not to be bothered.

**Design (unsourced). Part VI 7.4 gives defaults and ranges.** A ward race costs on the order of tens of thousands of dollars, a citywide primary a few hundred thousand.

**Patronage (Part I 4.4).** City jobs are the machine's currency. Some are classified civil service and filled by examination. Others are exempt or provisional and filled at will [memory, to verify]. Federal job programs in the 1970s add public-service jobs that the city may fill (6.7). A hiring freeze (Part I 4.5, Part II 2.1) deflates the currency, and a machine with fewer jobs to give has less to buy. This is the quiet reason it weakens.

**The families and the machine (Tendency).**

- Each family gives where it has business: the **Sabatini** in the Italian wing and the Board; the **Pellegrino** in the dock wards; the **Vannucci** in the Strip's ward; the **Ferrante** in the Racks and the Eastside's white wards. They give through fronts, friends and donations to the right feast, and they rarely give openly.
- A made man does not stand for office, because his record and his oath make him a liability. His friends and his nephews may.
- The machine keeps the families at arm's length in public and takes their help in private. Its best-known meeting places are the ones the Life already uses: the Feast of San Rocco, the wake, the funeral home (Part III 2.4, 6.4).
- A reformer's best charge is "the machine's friends," and a machine's best answer is that everyone in the city has a cousin on the Hill.

### 4.11 Reform

**Fixed (Part I 4.4).** Reform candidates win about one election in three.

**Who reformers are (Tendency, not types).** A reform candidate may be an honest Greenwood lawyer, a young Fenwick graduate, a dissident ward leader who lost a fight, a Black minister, an ambitious assistant prosecutor, a woman from a civic league, an ex-cop from the Dolan years, or a machine man who has decided he can win alone. Some are saints, some are cranks, some are opportunists, and many are several at once (Part II 0.3).

**How a reformer wins.** Through a split in the machine. Through a scandal that reaches the front page of the *Mirror* and the *Chronicle*. Through the turnout of the Eastside, the Hill's young families and the Greenwood professionals. Through a fiscal crisis that makes the machine's waste visible. Through the U.S. Attorney, the State Commission or the County Prosecutor, if one is willing (Part II 4.5, 5.3).

**How the machine answers (Tendency).**

- **Co-opt.** Offer the reformer a seat and a title.
- **Technical defeat.** Challenge his nominating petitions, deny him the line, redraw his ward.
- **Isolate.** Starve his supporters of jobs and favors.
- **Smear.** Raise his marriage, his religion, his race, his debts.
- **Wait.** A reformer in office must govern a city he does not control, and the machine can outlast him.
- **Violence** is rare and costly, and is seldom the first or the best tool. When it comes it brings the press and the federal government (Part III 9).

**After a reform win (Tendency).** The reformer discovers that the mayor's office controls the departments and not the ward leaders, that the Council is a fief of fifteen men, that the unions can stop the city, and that the money is gone (section 6). The families do not leave. Prices change. The Pad is reopened, the contracts are re-bid, and the Concrete Club learns to deal with a new Board.

**Anchor [memory, to verify].** The model is real New Jersey politics: Newark's mayor was convicted of extortion in 1970 and Jersey City's mayor and several others in 1971, which are cautionary tales of how far federal prosecutors would go against a machine. Both are offstage news.

### 4.12 The 1975 Mayoral Election (Open)

**Fixed (Part I 4.6).** The election falls in November 1975, ten months after the seed, with a party primary in September. In January 1975 it is an approaching fact, and the slates are being made.

**Open.** Who runs, who wins, and what each costs. This Part does not decide them. It sets the dials the simulation reads. Each dial is a pressure, not an outcome.

| Dial | Range | Default (Design) |
|---|---|---|
| **The incumbent** | runs / retires / is ill, indicted or dead before the primary | runs |
| **Machine unity** | united / split between the Irish and Italian wings / split by a Black-wing challenger | split, mildly |
| **Reform strength** | none / credible / strong | credible (one chance in three, Part I 4.4) |
| **Black mobilization** | low / moderate / high | moderate and rising |
| **Money** | what contractors, unions and the families put in, and where | divided |
| **Salience** | the fiscal crisis, school desegregation, crime, jobs | crisis strong, desegregation moderate to strong (Part I 13) |
| **Scrutiny** | the U.S. Attorney, the State Commission, the Prosecutor, the Governor, the press | moderate |
| **Union posture** | whom the PBA, firefighters, trades and Teamsters endorse | split |
| **The Mayor's past** | whether the incumbent made or inherited the Dolan reforms | Open |

**Tendency (what each result presses on the families, not what happens).**

- **The machine holds.** Contracts, the Pad and the Board continue on known terms. The families' friends keep their seats. The fiscal crisis is managed by cuts that fall on the weakest.
- **A new face from the machine.** The same people under a new name. A new police commissioner reopens the Pad (Part II 3.4).
- **A reform win.** Contracts are re-bid and the Concrete Club re-prices. The Pad is disturbed. A fight begins over the Board and the Port Authority. Reformers seldom control the Council.
- **A Black-led coalition win.** A shift of federal housing and job money toward the Eastside. A sharp resistance from the Irish and Italian wings and the unions. The numbers game, already shrinking (Part I 9.8), loses a protector or gains one.
- **An Italian-wing win.** The Sabatini friends rise, the Hill gets paving, and the Irish and the Black wing feel the loss.

A mayor does not end the families. He changes what they pay and what they are owed.

### 4.13 Politics Weather (also listed in section 7)

| Window | Condition | Strength | Who it presses |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1975 | Mayoral election, November | moderate | the machine, reformers, contractors |
| 1975 to 1977 | Austerity and layoffs | strong | the machine's patronage, the unions, the Pad |
| 1974 to 1977 | School desegregation | moderate | Kerrytown, the Eastside, Kosciuszko Park, ward politics |
| 1975 onward | Federal and State scrutiny of local corruption | moderate, rising | the machine, the Board, contractors |
| 1970 to 1990 | Black political power grows | moderate | the Irish and Italian wings, the Eastside |
| 1979, 1983, 1987 | Further mayoral elections | moderate | as above |
| 1982 | A new Governor (January). A change of party narrows the machine's appointment pipeline [memory, to verify] | moderate | judgeships, the Prosecutor, the Port Authority |

### 4.14 Open Items for Section 4

- The Mayor, council members, county chairman, ward leaders, the Governor's counsel, the senators from the county's districts, the Prosecutor, the assignment judge, the Port Authority board and director, and the Board of Public Works members, including the elected one and his election cycle.
- The ward map beyond the Sixth Ward.
- Who the factions' leaders are.
- Whether the incumbent made the Dolan Commission.
- The result of every election.
- The county row offices.
- **Needed from Part VI:** salaries of elected and appointed officials, campaign costs, the sums that move at each point.
- **Still to verify:** the NJ county committee structure and the ballot line; senatorial courtesy; the assignment judge's power; Municipal Court appointments; the county row offices; part-time assistant prosecutors; the county election board's appointment; the lowest-responsible-bidder rule and the emergency-contract exception; annual municipal audits; campaign finance laws of 1971 to 1974; civil service categories; the Newark and Jersey City convictions.

---

## 5. The Professionals

### 5.1 What the Earlier Parts Already Assert

| Source | Assertion |
|---|---|
| Part I 5.4 | Bellacorte is where the families' lawyers, accountants, doctors and restaurateurs come from, and "where dirty money goes to be cleaned." |
| Part I 10.4 | Jewish lawyers, accountants and doctors are common in the professional class, with a long, mixed and sometimes uneasy relation to the Italian underworld. |
| Part II 3.2 | The Pad is routed through intermediaries: a bail bondsman, a lawyer, a bar owner, a retired cop, a union man. |
| Part III 4.1 | The Sabatini family has the best lawyers and the most friends in the courts. |
| Part III 5.5 | Jewish financiers, bookmakers and specialists hold layoff banks, garment and jewelry finance, arson and insurance fraud, law and accounting. They cannot be made, and are useful. |
| Part III 6.4 | Russo and Sons Funeral Home is neutral ground. |
| Part III 11 | The bookkeeper, the bank teller, the funeral director, the court clerk, the nurse and the numbers banker's wife have real, unspoken power. |
| Part I 12.4 | Women gain power through family, the Church, small business and female-dominated institutions. |

### 5.2 What a Professional Is to the Life

**Fixed.** A professional is a man (or, rarely, a woman) who sells skill, respectability and discretion. The families need all three. A made man can be a gunman, a boss or a collector. He cannot be a lawyer in a courtroom, an accountant who signs a return, or a banker who approves a loan, because the world will not believe him.

**Tendency.** The professional stands in one of three relations to a family.

1. **Hired.** He sells his skill for a fee and keeps his independence. Most do, and most are honest in the ordinary sense.
2. **Partnered.** He has a stake in the family's business and shares its risk.
3. **Absorbed.** He started as hired, did a favor, did another, and found one day that he could not leave. This is the way most professionals who go bad go bad, and it takes years.

**The price (Tendency).** A professional's exposure is his license, his name and his liberty. A prosecutor who cannot reach a boss may reach his lawyer or his accountant. A professional who is squeezed has the best chance of any man in the Life to cooperate, and the family knows it.

### 5.3 Lawyers

**Tendency.** Four groups, which overlap.

- **The family's counsel.** A lawyer, usually of the same stock as the family and often from the Hill or Bellacorte, who handles its legitimate business, advises its men, and appears for its soldiers. He is rarely a made man, and a good one never learns more than he must. Sabatini has the best of these (Part III 4.1).
- **The criminal defense bar.** A few dozen lawyers known to every prosecutor and judge. They are paid in cash or by retainer, plead cases and try the ones that must be tried. A defense lawyer's value is whom he knows and how he reads a judge.
- **The machine's lawyers.** County committee counsel, ward lawyers, zoning lawyers, municipal bond counsel, and the lawyers who appear before the Zoning and Licensing Boards. Their value is access (4.7).
- **The downtown firms.** Old Protestant and Jewish firms serving the banks, the insurers, the developers and the Port Authority. Mostly clean, and exposed at the edges: a partner who handles a front's closing, a closing that goes through.

**Others.** Public defenders (the office exists in New Jersey from the late 1960s [memory, to verify]), legal aid, the NAACP's lawyers, and a handful of Black and women attorneys, who are few and conspicuous (Part I 12.1).

**Rules of the trade (Tendency).** A client may tell his lawyer what he did, and the lawyer need not repeat it. The privilege does not cover a crime still being planned [memory]. A lawyer who carries messages for a client in jail (Part III 6.4), who holds money for a client, or who advises a client how to hide an asset has stepped outside it. Fees are often paid in cash. A grand jury can subpoena a lawyer's fee records (Part II 6.4), and federal prosecutors treat a lawyer who works too closely with a family as a target.

**Weather (1984 onward, [memory, to verify]).** New federal law lets prosecutors seize assets used to pay a defense fee. Lawyers who serve the families become targets in racketeering cases through the 1980s.

### 5.4 Accountants

**Tendency.** Three kinds.

- **The large firms.** The big national accounting firms serve the banks, the Port Authority and the steel and chemical companies. They are clean and cautious.
- **The neighborhood CPA.** Keeps the books and prepares the returns of a hundred small cash businesses. Many are honest. Some keep two sets of books for a regular client. He has no incentive to know more than he must.
- **The tax man and the bookkeeper.** A bookkeeper in a bakery, a bar or a club often does the real arithmetic, and she is very often a woman (Part III 11).

**What the accountant knows.** The truth about the business that the return does not say. He is the one professional who can reconstruct a front or a skim (3.5).

**Exposure (Tendency, [memory, to verify]).** A federal court held in 1973 that no accountant-client privilege covers a client's records in the accountant's hands, so a tax preparer can be compelled to give them up. Penalties for preparers of false returns tighten in the mid 1970s. An accountant is therefore a likelier witness than a lawyer.

### 5.5 Bail Bondsmen

**Fixed (Part II 3.2, 6.2).** A defendant who cannot post bail pays a bondsman a premium, and the bondsman posts it. Before 1984 most defendants have a right to release (Part II 6.2), so a bondsman does steady work.

**Tendency.** The premium is on the order of ten percent of the bail [memory, to verify]. The bondsman takes collateral, knows the clerks and the judges, keeps an office near the Civic Center, and hires men to find those who run. He is the family's friend because he gets men out fast, and he is a Pad intermediary because he knows everyone who matters in the courthouse. His risk is a defendant who flees, so he prefers a man with a family and a reason to stay.

**Weather (1984).** The Bail Reform Act (Part II 6.2) lets federal courts detain a dangerous defendant before trial and shrinks the federal bondsman's trade.

### 5.6 Bankers

See 3.7. The professional banker is the branch manager, the loan officer and the trust officer. Many are honest, some are friends of a family, and the one who is neither is often the one who notices.

### 5.7 Developers

**Tendency.** Four kinds.

- **Old-line developers** of the Heights and the downtown towers, whose family names are on the buildings. Clean in their own telling.
- **Italian contractors turned developers**, who built the Hill's row houses and now build downtown. Often tied to the Concrete Club (3.6) and the Italian wing (4.3).
- **Suburban tract builders** in Marsh and Shore counties, who answer to suburban zoning, not the city's. A state court decision of 24 March 1975 against a township's exclusionary zoning changes what suburbs may refuse [verified, date; the holding is [memory]].
- **Community developers** of the Eastside and the Point, who use federal block-grant money (Part I 9.5) and the churches.

**Fixed (Part I 4.5).** "No one else will build here" is a developer's best argument with a desperate mayor. A developer needs zoning, permits, financing, concrete and labor, and can be squeezed at every one.

### 5.8 Insurance and Surety

**Tendency.** Insurance is a business of trust and paper.

- **Brokers and agents.** A broker who lands a union's welfare plan or a contractor's whole book earns commissions for years (2.6). That business is a favor.
- **Surety.** A contractor must be bonded to bid on public work (3.6). A bonding agent who is a friend is a gate.
- **Fire and property.** Decaying buildings in the Eastside and the Flats are underwritten for little and insured for more. Arson for profit is a crime of the period (Part I 7.4, Part III 5.5), and the Fire Marshal (Part II 1.1) and the insurer's own adjusters are its two watchers. After the 1968 riots, federal and state plans provide fire insurance in high-risk neighborhoods [memory, to verify], and a plan that covers a building no private carrier will touch is a temptation.
- **Marine and cargo.** The port's insurers pay for the cargo that vanishes (Part III 7.5).

### 5.9 Funeral Directors

**Fixed.** In a city of Catholic neighborhoods the funeral director is one of the best-placed men in the city. He knows every family on his block. He is trusted with grief, money and silence. His trade has a high margin (3.2). Everyone, sooner or later, is his customer.

**Tendency.** The funeral home is a bank (for the families that need an advance), a political club (the wake is the machine's town hall, Part III 2.4), a meeting room (Russo and Sons, Part III 6.4) and a place where the dead are put into the hands of a man who asks no question. Funeral directors on the Hill, in Kerrytown and on Dunmore Avenue each serve a community and each keep that community's confidence. A funeral director who betrays a confidence loses his trade. One who is a family's man loses his independence.

**Fixed (Part III 6.4).** A wake is the safest meeting in the city. The Storyteller should treat this as a courtesy the whole city keeps and may break.

### 5.10 Other Links

| Person | Role | Touch |
|---|---|---|
| **Doctors** | Fund clinics, workers' compensation, private practice | Referrals from welfare funds (2.6). A doctor who treats a wound and reports it is bound by law (Part I 8.4). One who does not is a risk to himself. |
| **Engineers and architects** | Write specifications; certify work | The spec writer shapes who can bid (4.6). |
| **Realtors and title men** | Sell, appraise and insure title | Aging owners on the Hill (Part I 5.3), front purchases, transfers. |
| **Notaries and court clerks** | Stamp, file, schedule | The clerk who loses a paper or sets a date. |
| **Priests and pastors** | Moral authority, trust | Funeral Masses, charities, the Church's silence (Part I 10). Not professionals in the sense of this Part, but men every one of the others consults. |
| **Journalists** | Print, withhold, investigate | The *Mirror*, the *Chronicle* and the *Courier* (Part I 11). A reporter's murder brings a hundred reporters (Part III 9). |

### 5.11 Women and the Professions

**Fixed (Part I 12).** Women hold fewer posts here than anywhere, and not none. A few women practice law, keep accounts, run funeral homes inherited from husbands and fathers, hold clerkships and sit on civic boards. **Tendency.** The bookkeeper, the teller, the clerk, the nurse and the funeral director's wife see what the men do not look at. The **League of Women Voters** and similar civic groups are among the most effective reform bodies in the city (4.11), because they are female-dominated institutions and the authority of such institutions is real and limited (Part I 12.4, route 4). The numbers rise slowly through the late 1970s and 1980s, as in the real world.

### 5.12 Mixed Men

**Tendency.** Every professional is mixed. **Examples (not canon):** a defense lawyer who has never taken a dishonest dollar and has defended every kind of man; a CPA who keeps a second set of books for one client because he once owed him a favor and has been trying to leave for ten years; a bondsman who is generous to his clients' mothers; a developer who builds well and pays the Club because he has no choice; a funeral director who has buried both sides of every feud and has never repeated a word. Each can change. A professional who is squeezed may cooperate, and one who has been cheated may turn.

### 5.13 Open Items for Section 5

- Every named lawyer, accountant, bondsman, developer, broker and funeral director.
- Which are the family's, which are partnered and which are absorbed.
- Which firms are clean.
- **Needed from Part VI:** fees and retainers, bail premiums, commissions and surety premiums, funeral costs.
- **Still to verify:** the New Jersey public defender's start date; the accountant-privilege holding of 1973; the preparer-penalty law; bail premium rules; the 1984 forfeiture law and its application to legal fees; post-1968 fire-insurance plans; the holding of the 24 March 1975 zoning decision.

## 6. The City's Money

### 6.1 What the Earlier Parts Already Assert

| Source | Assertion |
|---|---|
| Part I 3.5 | The municipal fiscal year begins on 1 July. Layoffs and budgets land then. |
| Part I 4.5 | Weather, strong, beginning 1975: a falling tax base, high debt, layoffs and cuts across the Northeast. For Saint Jude: hiring freezes at the SJPD and Fire Department, bridges, schools and streets falling behind, a mayor desperate for private building, a bond market that does not trust the city, poorly paid civil servants and developers who can say no one else will build. Open: how hard the crisis bites, whether the city defaults, which services are cut, who profits. |
| Part I 9.5 | Federal Community Development Block Grants from 1974 replace the old urban-renewal money and are smaller and more political. |
| Part I 9.10 | The bond rating is low and a default is possible. Money problems feed every other problem. |
| Part II 2.1 | The SJPD may fall from about 4,800 to about 4,300. Overtime is cut. |
| Part II 2.2 | Pensions after twenty years are a reason to stay and not rock the boat. |

### 6.2 The Budget as an Operating Condition

**Fixed.** The city is also the county (Part I 1.1), so its budget carries the police, fire, sanitation, streets, parks, health, the courts' local costs, the hall of records, public works and the schools' construction (Part I 4.3).

**Design (unsourced, Part VI to supply).** Most of an old city's operating budget is payroll and its benefits, on the order of three-quarters of it [memory, rough]. Debt service and pensions take a further share. What is left for services, repairs and capital is small. The total budget, the payroll and the debt are **needed from Part VI**.

**Where the money comes from (Tendency).** The property tax on a shrinking base. State aid. Federal aid (6.7). Fees and fines. Payments from the Port Authority, if any (Open). Borrowing.

**The cash calendar.** The year begins on 1 July (Part I 3.5). Taxes come in on a schedule, and the city's bills do not, so it borrows short-term between collections. A city whose short-term borrowing is refused cannot make payroll. That is what a crisis is (6.4).

**Flag (carried, not changed).** New Jersey municipalities commonly use the calendar year as their fiscal year, and school districts are separate bodies [memory, to verify for both]. Part I sets a July year and places school construction under the Board of Public Works. These are Saint Jude's own choices, and this Part follows them.

### 6.3 The Tax Base

**Tendency.** Property values on the Eastside, the Flats and downtown are soft or falling. Owners appeal their assessments, and each successful appeal shrinks the base. The assessors and the county tax board are a touchpoint (the Governor appoints county tax boards [memory, to verify for 1975]). Abandoned and burned buildings (Part I 7.4) bring no tax. Tax abatements to developers are a bargaining chip with a mayor who needs buildings. The statute and its date are **to verify**.

### 6.4 Debt, Bonds and the Market

**How a city borrows (Tendency, [memory]).** For long-term capital, the city sells **bonds**. For the gap between taxes and bills, it sells **short-term notes** in anticipation of taxes, revenues or bond sales. Rating agencies grade the city, and a low grade raises every rate. **Underwriters** (the banks, Part I 9.6) buy and resell the issue. **Bond counsel** (a law firm) certifies that it is legal. **Financial advisers** help the city price it. Each is a fee, and each fee is a favor. A bond issue is the biggest single piece of patronage the city makes outside its payroll.

**The 1975 model.** New York City's crisis is the national case and Saint Jude's reference. The Municipal Assistance Corporation was created on 10 June 1975. The city came close to default on 17 October. The President announced on 29 October that he would refuse a federal bailout. In December Congress passed a seasonal financing act that lent the city money on conditions, including cuts to its workforce [verified for these dates, from a summary page]. Cities across the Northeast watched, and the bond market's trust in all of them fell.

**Anchor [memory, to verify].** Cleveland defaulted on short-term notes in December 1978. A default is therefore a real event of the period, and Saint Jude's own is Open.

**State oversight (Tendency, [memory, to verify]).** New Jersey supervises local finance through a state division and board that review local budgets and borrowing. A Saint Jude too deep in debt would not be left to default quietly. The State would step in, and its terms would be as bitter as the unions' fear. How far, and when, is **Open**.

**Who profits from the bond business (Tendency).** Underwriters, bond counsel, financial advisers and the banks that hold the paper. In a distressed market, the buyers of short-term notes at high rates. A man who lends to a city workers' union or a contractor who is waiting to be paid, at a street rate (Part III 7.3), earns on the city's late payments.

### 6.5 Layoffs, Seniority and the Unions

**Tendency.** When the city cuts, it cuts by seniority, last in and first out [memory]. The men most recently hired are the youngest and, because of court orders and pressure in the early 1970s (Part II 2.3), a disproportionate share of the new Black and Puerto Rican officers and firefighters. A layoff therefore removes the people the department had just been made to hire. The PBA defends seniority. The Guardians, the Black officers' association, protest. The Mayor and the Council are caught between them, and the quarrel becomes part of ward politics (4.3).

**Fixed (Part II 2.1).** The SJPD may fall from about 4,800 to about 4,300 by 1977. Police and firemen under pay and overtime cuts take the Pad's temptations more seriously, and the union fights loudly.

**Public-employee unions (2.5).** They cannot legally strike, and use the sick-out and the slowdown. A sanitation slowdown that leaves garbage on the street in July is a political weapon the machine understands.

**Weather (1975 to 1977, strong).** Cuts, freezes and layoffs. **Open:** their size and timing.

### 6.6 Contracts Under Austerity

**Tendency.** Less money means fewer contracts and more competition for each. Deferred maintenance (Part I 8.2) makes the contracts that are let into emergencies, and emergencies skip bidding (4.6). A city that cannot pay promptly pays contractors late, and a late-paid subcontractor is a borrower from the street. The Concrete Club's share of a smaller pie matters more to its members. The families look to private work, to the Port Authority and to the suburbs.

### 6.7 Federal and State Money

All entries are [memory, to verify] except the block-grant program named in Part I.

| Program | What it pays for | Who controls it | What touches it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Community Development Block Grants** (from 1974, Part I 9.5) | Housing rehab, demolition, public works, neighborhood groups | The Mayor and Council, under federal rules | Developers, contractors, Eastside community groups, ward leaders |
| **General revenue sharing** (from 1972) | Unrestricted aid to localities | The city's budget | The budget gap |
| **Public-service jobs** (1973 federal job-training law and its 1974 expansion) | Temporary jobs in the city | The city and local sponsors | The machine, which fills them |
| **Law-enforcement aid** (from 1968) | Police equipment, studies, new units | The State and the city | The SJPD, the Mob Squad, vendors |
| **Education and health aid** | Schools, clinics, hospitals | Federal and State agencies, the schools, hospitals | Hospitals, the Archdiocese, vendors |
| **State aid** | Schools, roads, local relief | Trenton | The Governor's appointees, the city's senators |

**Weather (State, 1976, [verified]).** **New Jersey's first income tax is passed in the summer of 1976 and the first withholding is on 3 September 1976 [verified, Rutgers Eagleton Institute, Part VI pass]. That the final votes fell in July is **now [verified, same source]: the New Jersey Supreme Court's order of 13 May 1976 closed the public schools from 1 July, they stayed closed for seven days while the Assembly voted, and the Senate passed the package two days after the Assembly. The exact dates of the votes are not given.** New Jersey enacts a state income tax in 1976, which enlarges the State's capacity to aid cities.

**Weather (1981 onward, [memory, to verify]).** Federal budget cuts shrink aid and job programs. General revenue sharing ends in 1986.

**Tendency.** Federal money comes with rules, a paper trail and an auditor. It is harder to skim than city money, and it flows to the Eastside's Black institutions (Part I 5.6), whose leaders gain real power from it and whose critics call them machine men.

### 6.8 Pensions

**Tendency (to verify).** In New Jersey most local police, fire and civil-service pensions are paid through state-run retirement systems [memory, to verify], so a city like Saint Jude holds little pension money of its own to lend. This makes the city's pension funds a smaller touchpoint than in cities that run their own, and a larger political issue (a state system that the city's cuts shorten).

### 6.9 Who Gains and Who Loses

**Open (Part I 4.5).** How hard the crisis bites, whether the city defaults, which services are cut and who profits. This Part does not decide them. It lists what each outcome presses on.

**Tendency.**

- **Losers.** The poor, the Eastside, the elderly on the Hill, the laid-off, the newest officers, the schools and the men with debts to the street.
- **Gainers in a deep crisis.** Developers who can buy cheaply and demand abatements. Contractors with cash and friends. Lenders, legal and illegal. Bondholders paid first. The suburbs.
- **The machine.** Weaker with each job it cannot give (4.10), stronger in the short run where it controls what is left.
- **The families.** Their upperworld friends have less to sell, their fronts have fewer customers, and their lending grows (Part I 9.10). A city with no money is a city where a bribe is cheaper and a loan is dearer.

### 6.10 City-Money Weather (also listed in section 7)

| Window | Condition | Strength | Who it presses |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1975 | Municipal Assistance Corporation (10 June), near-default (17 October), federal refusal (29 October), federal loan act (December) in New York [verified] | strong, national | bond markets, every Northeastern city |
| 1975 to 1977 | Saint Jude austerity, hiring freeze, layoffs | strong | the police, fire, sanitation, the Pad, the Mayor |
| 1976 | State income tax (summer; first withholding 3 September) **[verified, including July passage]** | moderate | State aid, the city's budget |
| 1978 | Cleveland note default (December) [memory, to verify] | moderate | bondholders, the city's rating |
| 1979 to 1983 | Very high interest rates | strong | the city's borrowing, developers |
| 1981 onward | Federal aid and job-program cuts [memory, to verify] | strong | the city's budget, the Eastside's institutions |

### 6.11 Open Items for Section 6

- The size of the budget, payroll, debt and cash gap; the rating.
- Whether and when Saint Jude defaults, and how the State steps in.
- How many officers, firemen and sanitation men are laid off, and who.
- The Port Authority's payments to the city, if any.
- **Needed from Part VI:** the budget, payroll, debt service, pension costs, tax rates, assessments, bond yields, federal aid levels.
- **Still to verify:** the State's local-finance oversight; the fiscal year and school-district facts for real New Jersey; **(the July 1976 passage of the income tax is now verified, 6.7);** Cleveland's default date; the 1973 and 1974 federal job-program acts; revenue sharing; the 1981 cuts; tax-abatement law.

## 8. Open Items, Anchors, Verification and Consistency

### 8.1 What Part IV Decides (Fixed)

- The four currencies (jobs, contracts, credit, paper) and the touchpoint table (section 1).
- The ordinary honesty of the upperworld (1.3).
- The nature of a local, its elections, hiring, strikes and funds (2.2 to 2.6), and the local-by-local survey (2.7). No family controls the Steelworkers, hospital, police or fire unions, though an individual officer or a local may be compromised.
- How a business earns, the six ways it relates to the Life, and what a front and a skim look like from the owner's books (3.2 to 3.5).
- How the Concrete Club works and who pays for it (3.6).
- How a bank works and how the families use one (3.7).
- How the machine works, its factions, the Board of Public Works, zoning and licensing, the sponsorship of judges and prosecutors, the Port Authority board, campaign money, reform (section 4).
- The professionals as a class and each trade (section 5).
- The city's money as an operating condition (section 6).
- The corrected rule that the Governor appoints Superior Court judges and County Prosecutors (0.4).

### 8.2 What Part IV Leaves Open

Every named person and office-holder. Every election result. Which businesses are fronts or skims. The Club's members and sums. Who has the Gannet Terminal. The state of every fund. Whether and when the city defaults, and how the State steps in. Which strikes happen. The ward map beyond the Sixth Ward. The county row offices. The Port Authority board's size and members. Sections 2.10, 3.9, 4.14, 5.13 and 6.11 list them in full.

### 8.3 New Open Items Created by This Part

- Who controls the Gannet Terminal and how many container jobs it carries (2.7B).
- Whether Local 1201's officers knew of the 1973 strike beforehand, and whether the independents have a surviving organization (2.7B).
- Which locals have a family's man at the top (2.10).
- Which individual officers in the Steelworkers, hospital, police and fire unions are compromised (2.7F, H, J).
- Who the Club's members were and who died in 1974 (3.6).
- Which bank officers are friends of which family, and the state of Mount Zion's credit (3.7).
- Who the county chairman, the Governor's counsel, the senators from the county's districts, the assignment judge, the county row officers and the Port Authority board are (4.2, 4.8, 4.9).
- Whether the incumbent Mayor made the Dolan Commission (4.5, 4.12).
- The election cycle and holder of the elected seat on the Board of Public Works (4.6).
- The size of the Port Authority board and its split (4.9).
- Whether and how the State intervenes if Saint Jude nears default (6.4).
- Which professionals are hired, partnered or absorbed (5.2).

### 8.4 Amendments to Earlier Parts

**Applied here and in Part I.** The appointment fix changed Part I 4.3 (two bullets), 4.6, 5.8 and the anchors in 15.2, and all four now carry it. It rests on checked sources (0.4).

**Flags only, not changed.**

- Part I 9.1 job totals (3.1).
- Part I 3.5 sets a 1 July fiscal year and Part I 4.3 places school construction under the Board of Public Works. Real New Jersey municipalities commonly use the calendar year and have separate school districts [memory, to verify]. Left as Saint Jude's choice (6.2).
- Part I 4.6 puts the primary in September. Real New Jersey practice differs [memory, to verify]. Left as written.
- Part I 1.1 makes city and county one government, which real New Jersey does not allow [memory, to verify]. A settled design choice. It means the county row offices (sheriff, clerk, surrogate) are city prizes, and they are Open (4.8).
- Part III 7.4 lists pension and welfare-fund loans as a racket. ERISA changes the method from 1975, not the fact (2.6). Read it as a tendency that survives in altered form.

### 8.5 Anchors Used

**Checked against sources in this drafting session [verified].**

- The New Jersey Constitution of 1947: the Governor appoints Superior Court judges with the Senate's consent, initial term seven years, tenure on reappointment until 70, and appoints county prosecutors for five-year terms. Sources: the State of New Jersey's government page, the record of the 1947 Constitutional Convention, and a New Jersey Globe account of the first appointments.
- The New Jersey Senate has been elected from 40 single-senator districts since 1973.
- ERISA was signed on 2 September 1974 and created the federal pension insurer, and its rules bar certain transactions between a plan and parties in interest.
- *Southern Burlington County NAACP v. Mount Laurel* was decided on 24 March 1975. The holding was not retrieved.
- New York City: the assistance corporation (10 June 1975), the near-default (17 October), the federal refusal (29 October), the December 1975 federal loan act with conditions.
- Carried [verified] from earlier Parts: Pick-3 (22 May 1975), the casino vote and the opening of Resorts, Governor Byrne's term dates, Abscam and the Bail Reform Act (Part II).

**From general knowledge [memory].** Everything else in this Part that describes the real world is [memory] and listed in 8.6.

### 8.6 Still to Verify (all [memory])

- **Labor law.** ERISA's enforcing agencies and fiduciary standard. (**The container royalty is now verified, section 2.10;** the 1959 act's terms, ERISA's effective dates and the 1966 guaranteed annual income are verified). New Jersey's public-employee labor act and its police and fire arbitration act (1977). The 1974 hospital amendments. The steel no-strike arrangement. The 1969 contract-compliance plans. The air traffic controllers' strike date. The first civil racketeering trusteeship suits and the national Teamsters suit.
- **Business and banking.** The share and threshold of the real New York concrete cartel. New Jersey's public-contracts law and its "lowest responsible bidder" rule. Regulation Q and usury ceilings in 1975. The 1977 federal set-aside. The 1977, 1978 and 1980 banking acts. The 1984 and 1986 money-laundering, forfeiture and currency-reporting laws. IRS audit methods.
- **Politics and courts.** County committee structure and the ballot line. Senatorial courtesy. The assignment judge's power. Municipal Court appointments. The county row offices. Part-time assistant prosecutors. The county election board's appointment. Emergency-contract exceptions. Annual municipal audits. Campaign-finance laws of 1971 to 1974. Civil-service categories. The Newark and Jersey City convictions. The holding of *Mount Laurel*.
- **Professions.** The public defender's start date. The 1973 accountant-privilege holding. The preparer-penalty law. Bail premium rules. Post-1968 fire-insurance plans.
- **City money.** State oversight of local finance. **(The July 1976 passage of the income tax is now verified, section 6.7.)** Cleveland's default date. The 1973 and 1974 federal job-program acts. Revenue sharing. The 1981 cuts. Tax-abatement law. State-run local pensions. Whether the State appoints county tax and election boards in 1975.

**Carried from the handoff, untouched by Part IV.** New York's and Pennsylvania's daily-game start dates, the 1975 and 1980 cigarette tax rates, the federal cigarette trafficking law, New Jersey one-party consent, and the New Jersey death-penalty dates. They stay on the Part II and Part III lists. **Status after the Part VI pass: Pennsylvania's daily number began 1 March 1977 [verified]; New York's Numbers game appears from 1980, exact date Open; the Contraband Cigarette Trafficking Act was enacted 2 November 1978 [verified in outline]; New Jersey is a one-party-consent State for recordings (N.J.S.A. 2A:156A-4) [verified in outline]; New Jersey's death penalty was under a de facto ban from 1972, reinstated in 1982 and abolished on 17 December 2007 [verified]; the 1975 and 1980 cigarette tax rates remain unverified, though collections are (Part VI 5.1).**

### 8.7 What Part VI Must Supply

- **Labor.** Hourly wages by trade, dues, fund contribution rates and assets, benefit levels, guaranteed-annual-income and royalty levels, officers' pay.
- **Business.** Prices, sales, wages and rents by trade, real net margins, protection rates, shark rates tied to real borrowers, contract values by job type, bond premiums, sales-tax rate.
- **Government.** The city's budget, payroll, debt service, pension costs, tax rates and assessments, bond yields, federal aid levels, salaries of elected and appointed officials, campaign costs.
- **Professions.** Fees and retainers, bail premiums, commissions, surety premiums, funeral costs.
- **The scale test.** The handoff asks that the roughly 190 made men be tested against the revenue model. Part IV supplies the legitimate side of that model (union, business, contract and city flows), and every figure in it is a Design estimate. The test cannot run until Part VI supplies real prices. It is not done here. **Part VI supplies the prices and runs the test (Part VI 9). Where each item is answered: wages and funds, Part VI 1.3 and 4.1 to 4.3; sales, rents, wages and margins by trade, 4.4; contract values by job type, 3.4; bond premiums, 4.5 and 7.6; city money, 7.2; officials' pay, 7.3; campaign costs, 7.4; fees, bail and funerals, 7.6 and 7.7. All are Design except the few marked [verified].**

### 8.8 Consistency Check Against Parts I to III

Checked by reading Part IV against Parts I, II and III as held in the project.

**Consistent and carried.**

- The two dock locals, their sides of the river, their ethnic bases, their pool hiring under the Waterfront Commission, and their guaranteed income and royalties (Part I 5.1, 6.1, 9.2).
- The Board of Public Works as three members, two Mayor-appointed and one citywide-elected (Part I 4.3).
- Fifteen wards, and the Sixth Ward near the Basin (Part I 4.3, 6.4).
- Reform winning about one election in three (Part I 4.4).
- Mayoral elections in odd years with a September primary and a November general (Part I 4.6).
- The Port Authority as a sister body with a board named by the Governor and the Mayor, about 600 police and cash tolls (Part I 8.1, 8.2, Part II 4.1).
- The families' territories and trades: Pellegrino on the docks, Ferrante on trucking and the Racks, Sabatini on construction and the Club, Vannucci on the Strip, the mills and waste (Part III 4).
- The Concrete Club at about two percent, and the 1974 events (Part III 7.4, Part I 2.5).
- Fronts, skims, banks and the Bank Secrecy Act (Part III 7.8, Part I 9.6).
- The Pad's intermediaries and the effect of a new commander (Part II 3.2, 3.4).
- Women's routes to power and their near absence from the top posts (Part I 12).
- Real people kept offstage.
- No em dashes and no named secrets.

**Interpretations made, not contradictions.** Local 1201's geographic claim on the Gannet Terminal (2.7B). The 1973 strike as a strain on the 50/50 deal (2.7B). ERISA changing the method of fund loans (2.6). The Sabatini family as the political family of the Italian wing (4.3, 4.10). Made men not standing for office (4.10). Layoffs falling first on the newest, often Black, officers (6.5).

**Conflicts found in earlier Parts.** The appointment of judges and prosecutors (corrected, 8.4). The flags in 8.4 are noted, not changed.

### 8.9 Anchors Added to Part I's List

If you want Part I's anchor list to carry them: the New Jersey Constitution of 1947, the 40-district Senate of 1973, ERISA (2 September 1974), *Mount Laurel* (24 March 1975), and New York's 1975 crisis dates.

---

*End of Part IV.*


# Saint Jude: The Setting Bible

## Part V: The Outside Groups and the Drug Markets

Baseline: the start of 1975. Dated items after that are **Weather**. Section 0 of Part II (the Period Mind) governs everything here.

**Draft status.** Complete draft, sections 0 to 12. **Bold marks edits made after Part VI was drafted (9.1, 9.2 and 12.7).** Parts I, II and III were amended in the project to fit it (section 1).

---

## 0. How to Read This Part

### 0.1 What the Outside Groups Are

**Fixed.** The outside groups are the criminal worlds of Saint Jude that are not the four Italian families. Some are old and rooted (the Irish of Kerrytown, the policy men of Dunmore Avenue). Some are small and new (the Russian handful in Brighton Point). Some are trades more than organizations (the Jewish dealers and financiers of Hunts Row). None is a made man. None is bound by the Italian oath. Each holds its own code, its own loyalties and its own grievances, and each meets the families at points of need.

This Part describes them as institutions in early 1975. It does not decide what any man in them will do.

### 0.2 Conventions

Tags follow Parts I to IV: **Fixed**, **Open**, **Weather** (dated, with strength), **Anchor**, **Tendency**, **Design**, **Example**. A real fact is **[verified]** only if checked against a source in this drafting session. **This Part was written from memory, so a real-world item is [memory] and carries "to verify" unless Part VI or the Index marks it verified. All of them are collected in section 12.6.**

**Design.** Dollar figures are 1975 dollars, net not gross (Part III 7.1), and unsourced. **Part VI gives the price book. This Part gives only rough figures; 12.7 lists what Part VI was asked to supply.**

**Named men.** Every name is an **Example**. The engine may change or drop it.

**Real people and organizations.** Real people appear only as history and offstage news. Real organizations (the Provisional IRA, a national motorcycle club, a Soviet-era emigration, a federal agency) are offstage context, tagged [memory]. A real club's real internal events are never Saint Jude history. The Saint Jude groups are stand-ins and are not copies.

### 0.3 What This Part Will Not Do

It does not write a price book (Part VI) or the narration rules. It gives no operational detail on making, moving or hiding drugs, weapons or explosives, and none on the arms trade to Ireland or any attack. Part I 0.5 principle 7 governs. It invents no named secret, conspiracy or plot hook. It does not write the 1980 state. The run decides it.

### 0.4 A Gap in the Source Material

**Flag.** The handoff asks that this Part keep to "the codex's old narcotics material." That material is not in the project. The only narcotics rules in the four attached Parts are Part III 7.6 (the families' contradictory attitude) and Part III 9 (the taboo row for dealing where a family forbids it). This Part keeps to those. If the codex has more, it should be compared against section 9 before the Part is locked.

---

## 1. Conflicts Found in Parts I to III, and How They Were Resolved

The handoff said to flag, not silently fix. Eight conflicts or weak joints were found. Each was resolved by a direct replacement in Parts I, II or III (already applied in the project), and the resolution is carried here so that Part V and the earlier Parts agree.

| # | Where | Problem | Resolution |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Part III 5.1 | The old 50/50 port arrangement with the Pellegrinos gave the Brigades "a share of the port," but Part I puts the north bank and Local 402 under the Pellegrinos and the south bank and Local 1201 under the Irish. A share of the whole port conflicts with that. | The 50/50 covers **cross-river items only**: tugs and lighterage, and ship-side pilferage on vessels that work both banks in one call. Each side keeps its own bank. The Gannet Terminal is outside it and stays Open (Part IV 2.7B). |
| 2 | Part III 5.2 | The combine's seat was "the Eastside, Dunmore Avenue, the Strip," but the Strip (Mercer Avenue) is Vannucci ground (Part I 5.5). | The seat is **Dunmore Avenue and the Eastside projects**. The Strip is Vannucci ground and is customer ground for the combine. |
| 3 | Part III 5.2 | The name "Mercer Street Kings" claims Mercer Avenue ground, which is Vannucci's, and it was written as if it could exist in 1975. | The Kings do not exist in 1975. The name has to be earned by territory (section 4.7, Weather only). |
| 4 | Part III 5.3 | "Whether the Russians arrive in 1975 or after" was Open, but Part I 5.7 already puts a handful in Brighton Point. | **Settled:** a handful are there at the start of 1975. **Open:** an Agron-like figure, and how the Italians treat them. "Vorovskoy" is a bible label. In 1975 people say "the Russians" or "the émigrés." |
| 5 | Part III 5.7 | The first line was tagged **Fixed** but its content is Open (which groups exist). | Retagged **Tendency**. Section 8 decides which get an entry. |
| 6 | Part III 5.5 and 4.3 | The "master fence" of Hunts Row was written as one institution, but 5.5 says Jewish fences and 4.3 puts the family there. | A **mixed class of Jewish and Italian dealers** on Ferrante ground, protected and taxed by the family (section 7.1). |
| 7 | Part III 7.2 | The Bayview handle as the numbers source ignores that the track races only in season (Part I 11.3), and Pick-3 changes the number after 22 May 1975. | Bayview handle in season, an out-of-state published figure in winter [Design], and many street banks adopt the State's own number [memory, to verify] (section 4.5). |
| 8 | Parts I, II and III weather | Crack was dated to 1981 or "early 1980s." In Northeastern cities rock cocaine is rare until about 1984 and the crack boom is about 1985 to 1987 [memory, to verify]. | Cocaine's rise is dated **1980 onward** and crack **1984 to 1985 onward, boom 1985 to 1987** (section 11). |

**Counterargument, offered as the handoff requires.** A reader may say that resolution 1 shrinks the Brigades unfairly, since Part III 5.1 plainly meant them to hold half the harbor. The strongest case for that reading is that the Irish are "older than the Italians by a generation in the city." The answer is that age does not decide geography. Part I puts the Italian locals on the north bank and the Irish on the south, and a 50/50 split of one pier system would make the Irish and Italian locals the same local. The cross-river reading keeps both facts.

**Flag, not changed.** Part I 6.4 still carries the heading "Kosciuszko Park, Greenwood, and the Smaller Worlds" and names the Sixth Ward and Docks Chinatown. Pescadores Row was removed from that heading because it is a district in Part I 5.1. Part V treats it under section 8.

---

## 2. The Common Frame

### 2.1 What Makes a Group "Outside"

**Fixed.** An outside group has four things the Italian families have in some other form: a **base** (a neighborhood, a pub, a club, a church), a **trade** (the thing it earns on), a **code** (what its men owe each other) and **a relation to the families**. It lacks a made man's oath, the Ladder (Part III 3.1) and a seat at a sit-down. When a boss wants to talk to one, he sends a go-between, or he asks a man he respects.

### 2.2 How Onore Applies to an Outside Group

Part II 0.3 and Part III 10 treat Onore as eight separate obligations, none of which a man must keep all of. The outside groups hold some and not others. The table is a Tendency, and individuals differ.

| Obligation (Part III 10) | Irish (Brigades) | Black underworld | Russians | Bikers | Jewish specialists | Chinatown |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Silence before the law | strong, tied to the parish and the cause | strong, tied to the block | weak in the handful; fear of police is strong | strong inside the club, weak outside | strong, tied to the lawyer | very strong |
| Respect for the elder | strong | strong (the older policy man) | weak (no elders yet) | club rank, not age | strong | very strong |
| Keeping one's word | strong among themselves | strong to the church and the block | uncertain | within the club only | the whole basis of the trade | very strong |
| Family before the group | strong | strong | strong (kin are the only trust) | the club is the family | strong | strong |
| Honor in the neighborhood | strong, a rule against hurting the parish | strong | none in 1975 | none | strong | strong |
| Vengeance for blood | strong and long | strong | uncertain | strong inside the club | rare | by association rule |
| Deference to the stronger power | grudging | grudging | high (a stranger in a strange town) | resented | high | by arrangement |
| One's own limits | varies | varies | varies | varies | varies | varies |

**Tendency.** The Storyteller uses the table to make each group read as itself. An Irishman will not cooperate with a cop for a reason an Italian would. A Black policy man will not break the church's peace for a reason a biker would. A Russian will not trust a stranger for a reason no one else would.

### 2.3 The Gold Coast Sanctuary and the Outside Groups

**Fixed (Part III 6.4).** The Oakridge sanctuary is a convention among the four bosses, backed by self-interest and by a borough's own police.

**Tendency.** It binds an outside group only through the family that can hurt it. A biker or a Russian who brings gunfire to Oakridge answers to the four bosses together, and the bosses' revenge is the sanction, because the group has no seat at the convention. The Brigades respect it out of old courtesy and out of fear of the heat. Chinatown has no business there. The Black underworld's leaders live in a few Eastside and Fenwick streets and have their own sanctuary habit (the church, the funeral). **Open:** whether the outside groups have a sanctuary of their own.

### 2.4 The Go-Betweens

**Tendency.** A family reaches an outside group through a man both sides can stand. The usual types are a lawyer, a funeral director (Part IV 5.9), a bar owner, a retired cop or a ward leader (Part IV 4.2), and, for the Black underworld, a pastor. A go-between is paid in favor, not money. He is also the first man a prosecutor leans on.

---

## 3. The Charlestown Brigades

### 3.1 The Institution

**Fixed (Part III 5.1).** The Brigades are the Irish underworld of Kerrytown and the South Piers. The **Harp and Crown**, a pub, is the headquarters. They are older in the city than the Italians by a generation and proud of it. Their power rests on the south-bank docks (Local 1201, Part IV 2.7B), tugboat yards, the South Pier crane crews, cargo theft and a share of the machine's old Irish wing (Part IV 4.3).

**Tendency.** "The Brigades" is an outsider's word, and it is more a set of loyalties than a roster. The parish, the pub, the firehouse, the union hall and the family are the real ties. A man is "with the Harp and Crown" because his father was. Nobody is inducted. The Storyteller should keep the lines blurred, with older men who give counsel, middle-aged men who hold a crane crew or a union post, and younger men who do the work.

### 3.2 The Trade

**Tendency.** In order of weight:

- **The south-bank docks.** Hiring in Local 1201's list, pilferage on the South Piers, no-show jobs, and the favors that go with a job at the hall. The decline of the docks (Part I 9.2) cuts all of it.
- **Tugs and lighterage.** The old yard families run the tugs. The cross-river part is the 50/50 with the Pellegrinos (section 1, #1).
- **Cargo theft.** Warehouse and truck-yard theft on the South Flats side of the river. The Ferrante family's trucking and fence meet it at the road (Part III 4.3).
- **Union posts.** Seats in several locals and in the firemen's and cops' associations (Part IV 2.7J, with the limit that no family controls the police or fire unions and a single officer may be compromised).
- **Gambling and the loans of the pub.** A small, old and local bookmaking and shylock business for the parish.
- **The cause.** Money for Ireland (3.5).

**Open.** The size of each branch of the trade, and who holds each.

### 3.3 The 50/50 and Its Strain

**Fixed (section 1, #1; Part III 5.1; Part IV 2.7B).** The old 50/50 arrangement with the Pellegrinos covers cross-river items only. It was made by older men on both sides and is honored as a promise between the old men, though neither side's young men like it.

**Fixed (Part I 2.5, Part IV 2.7B).** In 1973 Pellegrino crews broke an independent longshoremen's strike on the South Piers. A man was killed whose brother is still in Kerrytown. The arrangement is strained by it.

**Tendency.** The strain is a pressure, not a war. The Irish old guard want to keep the deal because the docks are shrinking and they cannot afford a fight. The younger men want a reckoning for the dead man. The Pellegrino old guard want the deal for the same reason as the Irish. A Pellegrino hothead sees an Irish debt to collect. Each side watches the other's funerals and weddings for a slight (Part III 6.5).

**Open.** Whether the dead man's brother is a man of the Harp and Crown or a man outside it. Whether Local 1201's officers knew of the strike beforehand (Part IV 2.7B). Who holds the Gannet Terminal, and whether the 50/50 is stretched to cover it.

### 3.4 Local 1201, the Firehouses and the Machine

**Tendency.** The Brigades touch the upperworld through three doors. **Local 1201** (Part IV 2.7B) is the nearest. The **Irish wing of the machine** (Part IV 4.3) is the second, because the old guard of that wing came from the same Kerrytown wards and parishes. The **firehouses and the Kerrytown Firemen's Association** (Part I 6.1, Part IV 2.7J) are the third. None of the three is "owned." Each is a place where Kerrytown men sit and where Kerrytown loyalties run.

**Tendency.** The Irish old guard in the machine is shrinking, and its men are anxious about the Italians and the Black wing. A Brigades man who also sits in a ward club is a common figure, and one who is both is torn when the machine's interest and the Harp and Crown's interest diverge.

### 3.5 The Troubles, at Institution Level

**Anchor [memory, to verify].** The conflict in Northern Ireland is at a high pitch in the 1970s. The Provisional IRA is the leading republican armed group, and American support for the cause runs partly through sympathetic civic and fund-raising bodies. The FBI and the Irish and British governments follow it. All of this is offstage.

**Tendency.** In Saint Jude the cause is a real thing to some Kerrytown families and a tavern slogan to others. It gives the Brigades a set of loyalties the Italians lack and a federal interest the Italians do not draw. Money, favors and shipping contacts move on the strength of those loyalties. The bible gives no more than this. It does not describe how anything moves, and it does not describe any attack.

**Tendency.** The cause cuts three ways inside the Brigades. Some men give out of belief. Some use it as a cover for profit. Some want nothing to do with it because they fear the federal heat on the docks. The Storyteller shows all three.

**Weather (1975 to 1990, moderate to strong, [memory, to verify]).** Federal attention to the American side of the conflict grows through the late 1970s and 1980s, with prosecutions and seizures reported as offstage news. The 1981 republican hunger strikes in Northern Ireland are an offstage event that may press the Kerrytown mood (strong, but only as mood). Any Saint Jude event is the run's to decide.

### 3.6 How the Brigades Meet the Families

**Tendency.** The Brigades pay no tribute to the Italians. They keep a working arrangement with the Pellegrinos (the 50/50), a courtesy toward the Sabatinis (the politicians) and a wary distance from the Ferrantes (trucking and the road). They regard the Vannuccis as new men. An Irish go-between in a quarrel is usually a priest, a funeral director or an old ward man.

**Grievances.** The 1973 strike. The decline of the docks. The Italian rise in the machine (Part IV 4.3). The sense that the Italians are "taking" the city that the Irish built.

**Tendency.** The Brigades prefer to deal with a family on terms of equals. A made man who treats an Irishman as a subordinate has made an enemy.

### 3.7 Narcotics
