## 2. Gambling

All figures are 1975 dollars, net to the bank or the family unless a column says handle. Handle means the money bet. Nothing in this section is a method.

### 2.1 What is sourced and what is not

**Anchor [verified, Reuter and Rubinstein, *Illegal Gambling in New York*, 1982, abstract].** Numbers margins were smaller and more variable than the popular picture. Clerks and managers cheated, bets were unevenly spread, and balances were hard to collect. Bookmaking margins were often very low, and bookmakers had repeated financial trouble. The Mafia financed numbers but did not control entry, regular pricing or individual operations. In bookmaking its role was mainly financing, and it was not the only lender.

**Anchor [verified, Christian Science Monitor, 30 May 1984].** Officials gave the reasons illegal games survive a state lottery: bets of a few pennies, credit, higher payouts and untaxed winnings. A Massachusetts estimate put the lottery's cut into illegal numbers profits at 10 to 15 percent, with no formal study behind it. New York lottery officials saw no effect on New York City rackets. A New Jersey legislator said there was no evidence the lottery had hurt them.

**Flag against Part III 7.2.** Part III 7.2 put the net to the bank at 10 to 20 cents on the dollar. The two sources above say real margins were lower and more variable. The default here is 8 cents (range 3 to 15). The arithmetic chain is in 2.2. **Part III 7.2 has been changed to match in the Part VI pass.**

No handle figure for any real city's illegal numbers was found this session. Every handle below is **Design**.

### 2.2 The numbers

**Fixed (Part III 7.2):** street payout 600 to 1 on a true chance of 1 in 1,000. Expected payout is 60 cents per dollar, so the hold is 40 cents [arithmetic].

The chain from a dollar of handle:

| Step | Per dollar | Mark |
|---|---|---|
| Handle | 1.00 | |
| Expected payout to winners (600 to 1 at 1 in 1,000) | 0.60 | arithmetic from Part III 7.2 |
| Hold | 0.40 | arithmetic |
| Runner's commission | 0.10 | Part III 7.2 |
| Controller's cut | 0.05 to 0.10, default 0.075 | Part III 7.2 |
| Left to the bank before costs | 0.225 | arithmetic |
| Pad, bad debt, cheating, variance | 0.145 default, 0.075 to 0.195 | **Design** |
| Net to the bank | 0.08 default, range 0.03 to 0.15 | **Design** |

**Handle, early 1975 (Design):** city-wide numbers handle **$25 million a year** (range $15 million to $40 million).

| Source of play | Share | Handle, default | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Eastside banks (Part V 4.2) | 64 percent | $16.0M | The combine's core |
| Puerto Rican blocks, *bolita* and the number (Part V 8.2) | 12 percent | $3.0M | Own game, own bankers |
| Charlestown, Kerrytown and other Irish and mixed blocks | 16 percent | $4.0M | Smaller banks, many independents |
| Chinatown lottery and fan-tan side (Part V 7.2) | 8 percent | $2.0M | **Included in the $25M. The Italian share below is not taken on this row** |

**Net to banks, default:** $25M times 8 percent is **$2.0 million** (range $0.75M to $3.75M at the default handle). The Italian families take their share through financing, the controller's seat and street tax, **35 percent of net by default (range 20 to 50)**, **taken on the Eastside banks only ($16.0M of handle, $1.28M of net), which is $0.45 million** (range $0.1M to $1.2M, taking the ranges of handle, net and Italian share together). The combine's share to the families is Part V 4.3. **The Irish, Puerto Rican and Chinatown games pay the families nothing through this line. The bolita tax (8.7) and the Chinatown courtesy (8.5) are their only payments.** The Italian share is carried to section 9 under the name numbers_it. **The State's lottery was created on 20 November 1969 [verified, Wikipedia, New Jersey Lottery], so the State game of Part I 9.8 predates the baseline by five years; Pick-3 is the daily game that matters.**

**Counterargument.** The Reuter and Rubinstein result suggests the Italian share should be lower than 35 percent. The reply: Saint Jude's combine is organized and has an old relation to the Italian families (Part V 4.3). 35 percent is the default and the 20 percent end is as likely as the 50.

### 2.3 Pick-3

**Anchor (carried, Part I 9.8):** starts 22 May 1975. **New Jersey's prizes were parimutuel, not a fixed 500 for 1 [verified, Wikipedia, New Jersey Lottery: prizes were parimutuel until 14 November 2022]. A winning ticket paid a share of a prize pool, so the payout varied with play. A Rutgers mathematics seminar notice says the early payouts were unusually high and fell over the game's first ten years while the rules did not change [verified in outline]. Revision 1 had a fixed $500 for $1 [memory]; that is withdrawn.** The State's rake of about 50 percent (Part III 7.2) stays Design, so the State's expected return is about 50 cents on the dollar, against the street's 60 cents, plus the street's credit, pennies and untaxed wins. **The game was first called Pick-it and was renamed Pick-3 in 1987 [verified, same].**

**Two paths (Part V 4.5).** The State's number is a rival, and it is also a supplier: a street bank may use the State's drawn number as its own, which removes its own drawing risk and the doubt about a fixed number. The first path loses handle. The second keeps handle and gains trust. The table shows the default (a mix) with the low and high bounds for 1980 only.

Variable net per dollar of handle is **13.75 cents (the 0.225 above, less 0.03 for the Pad, less 0.0575 for the half of bad debt, cheating and variance that rises and falls with handle). Fixed cost is Design: half of the remaining 0.115, $1.44M a year (range none to all of it). At the default a fall in handle cuts net about 1.7 times as fast; 1980 net loss 24 percent (range 14 to 34).**

| Year | Handle lost to Pick-3 | City handle ($M) | Bank net ($M) | Italian share, 35% of the Eastside's net ($M) | Net lost vs 1974 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1974 | 0 | 25.00 | 2.00 | **0.45** | 0 |
| 1975 | 1% | 24.75 | **1.97** | **0.44** | **2%** |
| 1976 | 6% | 23.50 | **1.79** | **0.40** | **10%** |
| 1977 | 10% | 22.50 | **1.66** | **0.37** | **17%** |
| 1978 | 12% | 22.00 | **1.59** | **0.36** | **21%** |
| 1979 | 13% | 21.75 | **1.55** | **0.35** | **22%** |
| 1980 | 14% | 21.50 | **1.52** | **0.34** | **24%** |
| 1985 | 19% | 20.25 | **1.35** | **0.30** | **33%** |
| 1990 | 20% | 20.00 | **1.31** | **0.29** | **34%** |

All dollars are 1975 dollars at constant real play. 1980 bound: **1.76M** if the State's number is mostly a supplier (half the loss), **1.28M** if mostly a rival (one and a half times the loss).

**Tendency.** Weak banks fail first, because fixed cost is a larger share of their net. The combine thins from many small banks to a few (Open per Part V 4.5, who replaces a banker who loses his handle). Nominal handle still rises with prices: held at constant real play it is $32.9M nominal in 1980 and $48.6M in 1990.

**Pennsylvania's daily number began on 1 March 1977 [verified, Wikipedia, Pennsylvania Lottery]. New York's Numbers game has winning-number records from 1980 [verified in outline, New York open-data catalogue]; its start date is still Open.** They bear on Saint Jude only by the border and the press, and are carried as Weather in section 10.

### 2.4 Bookmaking and the layoff bank

| Item | Default | Range | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sports and horse handle, city, 1975 | $80M a year | $50M to $120M | **Design** |
| Net margin on handle | 2.0 percent | 0.9 to 3.5 percent | **Design**, set below the 4.5 percent theoretical vig on a balanced book (10 for 11) because of the Reuter result |
| Net to bookmakers, city | $1.6M | $0.45M to $4.2M | arithmetic |
| Italian share (financing, layoff, street tax) | 55 percent | 35 to 70 percent | **Design** |
| Italian net | $0.88M | | arithmetic |
| Layoff handle (Sabatini bank) | $10M | $6M to $20M | **Design** |
| Layoff net | 3.5 percent, $0.35M | | **Design** |
| Family keeps (after partners and swings) | half, $0.175M | | **Design** |

**Counterargument.** Many bookmakers are not tied to any family and pay only for peace. The 55 percent share is the share of net that reaches an Italian pocket. It is not the share of the bookmakers who are Italian.

**Bayview in season (Part I 11.3).** On meet weeks the illegal horse handle rises 15 to 25 percent, **Design**. The Bayview track's own legal pari-mutuel handle is not modeled here. The Bayview takes are a by-product of the Strip's cash and the loan trade on the grounds.

**Drift.** Handle grows with incomes in nominal terms, and with Sunday football in real terms. **Design** real growth of the sports handle: 3 percent a year, 1975 to 1990, which gives about $125M in 1975 dollars by 1990 at the default. Margin stays at 2 percent. Bookmakers' weakness is slow losers, and credit cost.

### 2.5 Cards, dice and the Strip

| Item | Default | Range | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|
| Steady games run by or for the families | 35 | 20 to 55 | **Design** |
| Net per steady game per week | $1,400 | $700 to $2,200 | **Design** (rake of 5 to 10 percent of a $15,000 to $25,000 weekly pot) |
| Floating dice, per year | $0.5M | | **Design** |
| Cards and dice net, city | **$3.0M** | $1.5M to $5.0M | **Design** |
| Strip club gambling | counted inside the Strip in section 9 | | |

Allocation (Design): Vannucci 50 percent (the Strip and the clubs), Ferrante 20, Sabatini 15, Pellegrino 15.

### 2.6 Casino effects after 1978 (Weather)

**Anchor (carried):** Atlantic City is the only casino site; vote November 1976; Resorts opens 26 May 1978.

**Weather, from May 1978, strength moderate.** **Design default response if nothing in the run deflects it (10.1):**
- Steady-game and Strip gambling net falls 10 percent in 1978 to 1980, then half of that recovers, because the better players can now go to the tables on the bus. Cost to the family-held cards and dice: about $0.3M.
- Bookmaking is hit less, a few percent in the Bayview weeks.
- Loan business gains. A man who loses at the tables needs cash at the door. The Storyteller may put a lender there. Whether the Saint Jude families are permitted to is **Open**, since the AC territory belongs to others.
- The building boom and the hotel locals are a labor flow and are counted in section 4.

**Counterargument.** The casinos could add to illegal gambling by teaching it. The cleaner reading is that a legal table does not take the bookmaker's customer, who bets on games he cares about.

### 2.7 Open from this section

- The real handle behind the 25 million. **Open** to the run within the range.
- Whether the State's number is mostly rival or supplier in Saint Jude. **Open**, default a mix.
- **New York's daily-game start date. [memory, to verify].**
- NJ Pick-3 first-year sales were not found. **Open**.

## 3. Loansharking and Protection

No method. Rates, books and flows only.

### 3.1 Rates by borrower (Part III 7.3)

| Borrower | Weekly vig | Typical loan | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|
| Wholesale, to another lender or a bookmaker | about 1 percent | $5,000 to $50,000 | Part III 7.3 |
| Commercial, a small firm or restaurant | 2 to 3 percent, default 2.5 | $2,000 to $20,000 | Part III 7.3 |
| Street, a worker or gambler | 5 percent | $100 to $1,000, default $300 | Part III 7.3 |
| Six-for-five | 20 percent a week | $50 to $300 | Part III 7.3 |

Annual rates are in section 1.5. The legal ceiling is **a flag**: New Jersey's criminal usury level and the 1975 civil ceiling are [memory, to verify] and Part IV 8.6 lists them as unchecked. The federal extortionate-credit offence is in force in 1975 (section 1.5).

**Tendency.** The shark's real income is not the vig. It is the interest on the part of the book that pays, less the part that does not. Most street borrowers pay interest only and never retire principal, so the principal is a long annuity (the "juice only" loan).

### 3.2 The book, Saint Jude, early 1975

All figures **Design**. Balances are principal outstanding.

| Book | Principal out | Range | Weekly rate | Interest due, 50 weeks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Street | $0.8M | $0.4M to $1.4M | 5% | $2.0M |
| Commercial | $1.2M | $0.6M to $2.2M | 2.5% | $1.5M |
| Six-for-five | $0.06M | $0.03M to $0.12M | 20% | $0.6M |
| **Total due** | | | | **$4.1M** |

Collection rate on interest due: 72 percent (range 55 to 88), **Design**, which gives $2.95M collected. Principal lost each year: 15 percent of the book (range 8 to 25), **Design**. Collection costs: 6 percent of collections. Retail net:

$2.95M collected, less $0.31M lost principal (15 percent of the $2.06M book), less $0.18M collection costs, less $0.75M paid to the wholesale capital behind the retail book, is **$1.72M**.

Wholesale capital: $1.5M (range $0.8M to $2.6M) lent at 1 percent a week, 50 weeks, is **$0.75M** a year to whoever holds it. Total loansharking net, city, **$2.47M** (range $1.0M to $4.5M), of which the Italian families hold most. Allocation (Design): **the Brigades' blocks 10 percent (section 8.1); of the rest,** Sabatini 30 percent, Ferrante 30, Pellegrino 20, Vannucci 20. The Sabatini and Ferrante share is higher because their territories have the larger commercial borrowers and the pension and union money (Part IV 2.6).

**Flag.** The Reuter literature finds that loansharking is smaller and less tied to the Mafia than the popular picture, and that sharks lose heavily on bad debt [memory, to verify]. The collection and loss rates above are set with that in mind. If the real collection rate is 55 percent, **the retail net falls to about $1.1M and the total to about $1.8M.**

**Drift.** Loan books grow with incomes and with credit squeeze. The 1979 to 1982 rise in legal interest rates (prime 15 to 20.5 percent in 1980 and 1981, section 1.5 **[verified]**) raises the shark's cost of capital and, more, the number of people refused by banks. **Weather, 1979 to 1982, moderate:** legal credit is scarce. **Design default response if nothing in the run deflects it (10.1): the street and commercial books rise by 20 to 40 percent in real terms.** 1983 to 1990: a fall back to trend as credit cards spread [memory, to verify].

### 3.3 Protection by trade

Part IV 3.4 gave "a few dollars a week for a candy store to a few hundred for a large club." The table fixes it. "Payers" is the count of businesses that pay someone in an Italian-held territory. Not every business pays, and not everyone who pays pays the family. The share paying is **Design**.

| Trade | Businesses in the city | Share paying | Payers | Weekly, default | Range | Annual, city |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Candy store, newsstand | 800 | 10% | 80 | $5 | 3 to 10 | $21K |
| Bar and tavern | 1,400 | 25% | 350 | $25 | 15 to 50 | $455K |
| Restaurant | 900 | 15% | 135 | $35 | 20 to 80 | $246K |
| Bakery, pork store, grocery | 700 | 15% | 105 | $15 | 8 to 30 | $82K |
| Garment shop | 1,200 | 20% | 240 | $20 | 10 to 60 | $250K |
| Trucking firm | 400 | 30% | 120 | $40 | 20 to 150 | $250K |
| Nightclub, supper club | 40 | 80% | 32 | $200 | 100 to 400 | $333K |
| Strip club, after-hours | 30 | 100% | 30 | $300 | 150 to 600 | $468K |
| Laundromat, vending, amusements | 150 | 20% | 30 | $15 | 8 to 40 | $23K |
| **Total** | | | | | | **$2.13M** (range $1.3M to $3.2M) |

Business counts are **Design**. They give about 5,600 small firms in the listed trades, which is plausible for a city of 1.1 million [memory, to verify]. The count would also check against the Census of retail and service trades, which Part VI did not pull.

Allocation (Design): **the Brigades' blocks 10 percent (section 8.1); of the rest,** Vannucci 30 percent (the Strip), Pellegrino 25, Ferrante 25, Sabatini 20.

**Flag, protection against extortion.** Many payers receive something. Peace from another collector, settlement of a dispute, an after-hours license, a quiet word to a beat cop (the Pad is a separate flow, section 7). The Storyteller should not treat every payer as a victim. The Period Mind (Part II, 0) says the man who pays thinks of it as the cost of staying open.

**Drift.** Weekly sums are sticky in nominal terms. A $25 payment in 1975 buys 65 percent as much in 1980 and 41 percent in 1990 (section 1.1). Collectors raise payments in steps of 20 to 40 percent every three or four years. Default nominal path for the bar: $25 (1975), $30 (1978), $38 (1980), $45 (1984), $55 (1988). In 1975 dollars that is $25, $25, $25, $23, $24. **Design.**

### 3.4 The Concrete Club's two percent, tested

**Anchor (Part III 7.4):** the Club takes about 2 percent of covered contract value. The real New York concrete cartel's share and threshold are still [memory, to verify] (Part IV lists them).

Test, Design inputs:

| Item | Default | Range |
|---|---|---|
| Building and civil construction put in place in the city, a year | $330M (about $300 a resident) | $220M to $450M |
| Share covered by the Club (large private and public jobs) | 12 percent | 7 to 18 percent |
| Covered volume | **$40M** | $20M to $70M |
| Club take at 2 percent | **$0.8M** | $0.4M to $1.4M |
| Contractor net margin on a job | **2 to 6 percent (4.4)** [memory, to verify] | |

**Result.** A pure 2 percent tax on a contractor with a **2 to 6** percent net margin takes **33 to 100** percent of his profit. That is not plausible as a levy on honest bids. It is plausible only if the Club's rigging raises winning bids above a free market price by 5 to 10 percent, so that the 2 percent comes out of the overcharge. An overcharge of 5 to 10 percent on $40M is $2M to $4M, of which the Club's take is a fifth to two fifths. **The 2 percent passes the test only as a share of a bid-rigging overcharge.** The rest goes to the contractors who play, to the rigging costs and to the Pad. This ties the Club to the public-contract rules in Part IV 4. Where public bidding is strict, the overcharge shrinks.

Two further Sabatini flows, both **Design**: profit on Sabatini-owned construction firms $1.0M (range $0.4M to $1.8M); share of the Club's rigged-contract profit held by Sabatini, $0.9M (range $0.3M to $1.8M). Together with the Club's take these give $2.7M a year to the Sabatini under concrete_club.

**Counterargument.** The Club may be a smaller institution than Part III 7.4 says, a few firms with a dinner arrangement. If so the covered volume falls toward $20M and the Sabatini building income falls by about a third.

### 3.4A Contract values by job type (new in revision 2; Part IV 8.7)

**Design.** The $330M of city construction put in place (3.4) is split by job type. "Typical prime contract" is the size of one contract to a general or prime contractor. "Club covers" is the share of that type's volume that is large enough, and public or institutional enough, for the Club to reach. The Club's take is 2 percent of covered value, as in 3.4.

| Job type | Volume, $M a year | Typical prime contract | Contracts a year | Club covers | Covered, $M | Club take at 2%, $M |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Public buildings (schools, hospitals, county and city) | 60 | $2.0M ($0.5M to $8M) | 30 | 21% | 12.6 | 0.25 |
| Roads, bridges, sewers, water | 70 | $1.5M ($0.3M to $6M) | 47 | 15% | 10.5 | 0.21 |
| Port and Port Authority works | 35 | $3.0M ($0.8M to $12M) | 12 | 8% | 2.8 | 0.06 |
| Large private (hotel, office, apartment, hospital wing) | 55 | $4.0M ($1M to $15M) | 14 | 20% | 11.0 | 0.22 |
| Renewal and federally aided public housing | 40 | $2.5M ($0.6M to $9M) | 16 | 10% | 4.0 | 0.08 |
| Small private (houses, shops, alterations, repair) | 70 | $80,000 ($10,000 to $400,000) | about 875 | 0% | 0 | 0 |
| **Total** | **330** | | | | **40.9** | **0.82** |

The covered total rounds to the $40M default of 3.4 and the take to its $0.8M. On one covered job the take is $40,000 on a $2.0M school, $30,000 on a $1.5M sewer, $80,000 on a $4.0M private building. A contractor's net margin of **2 to 6 percent** (4.4) on a $2.0M job is **$40,000 to $120,000**, so a $40,000 take is **33 to 100 percent** of his profit, the same result as 3.4: it is paid out of the rigged overcharge, not out of an honest margin. The surety premium on a $2.0M contract at 1 to 2.5 percent [memory, to verify] is $20,000 to $50,000. In 1980 dollars the same jobs cost 1.53 times as much (1.1). **Weather (moderate, 1978 onward):** the Atlantic City hotel boom raises the large-private volume and the hotel-locals labor flow (section 10).

### 3.5 Open from this section

- How much of the book is the families' own money and how much is borrowed from a pension fund or a bank (Part IV 2.6). **Open.**
- Whether any shark is a woman. **Open** (Part I 12.4 bars induction, not lending).
- The state usury figures. [memory, to verify].

## 4. Labor and Business Flows

### 4.1 What is sourced

**Anchor [verified, Christian Science Monitor, 1 February 1984].** The New York-area guaranteed annual income (GAI) began in 1966 at a cost of $1.7 million a year and cost an estimated $70 million in 1983. It guarantees 2,080 hours or equivalent pay, with eligibility based on 700 hours worked **(the base year is read two ways: the year before, or before the program began in 1966; to verify against the article)**, and stops at age 70, on full-time work elsewhere, or on Social Security. In 1984 the fringe benefit cost per container was $249 in New York and New Jersey, against $11.46 in southern Florida and $57.44 in Montreal. The ILA master contract then covered 36 ports and about 50,000 members. The pension for a man of 62 or more with 40 years rose to $950 a month.

**Anchor [verified, FRASER ILA chronology, BLS].** Basic hourly rate $6.80 (October 1974), $7.40, $8.00, $8.80, $9.60, $10.40 (October 1979). Section 1.3.

**Anchor [verified, GAO summary].** In the 1970s the Labor Department, the IRS and the Justice Department investigated the Teamsters' Central States pension fund for fiduciary breaches under ERISA. A consent decree followed in September 1982. The fund then held under half the money needed to cover its liabilities. No 1970s asset or contribution figure was found.

Everything else in this section is **Design** or **[memory, to verify]**, and says which.

### 4.2 Wages, dues, funds and officers

All **Design**, scaled to the ILA and construction anchors in section 1.3.

|---|---|---|---|

| Jobs in the city | **690,000** | **620,000 to 760,000** | **Part I 9.1. Revision 1's 550,000 is withdrawn (1.3)** |
| Union members, all unions | **193,000** | **140,000 to 255,000** | **arithmetic at 28 percent** |
| Dues, building and trucking locals | $9 a month | $6 to $14 | **Design** |
| Dues, ILA locals | $8 a month plus work dues | | **Design** |
| Pension and welfare contribution, Teamsters, per member per week | $32 | $22 to $45 | **Design** |
| Pension and welfare, construction, as a share of the wage package | 18 percent | 12 to 25 | **Design** |
| Container fringe cost, New York and New Jersey, 1975 | $100 | $70 to $130 | **Design**, from the verified $249 of 1984, which is $129 in 1975 dollars if the real cost had held, and it rose |
| Business agent pay | $17,000 | $13,000 to $24,000 | **Design** |
| Local president pay | $28,000 | $18,000 to $45,000 | **Design** |
| Fund assets, all union funds in the city | **$375M** | **$240M to $590M** | **Design**, at $2,500 per active **private-sector** member (about 150,000). **Public employees' pensions are State-run (Part IV 6.8) [memory, to verify]** |

**Flag (revised).** Revision 1 cut the job count to 550,000 and the fund assets to $385M. That cut is withdrawn (1.3). **The fund assets are now the weaker figure: $2,500 a member is Design, and the real Central States fund held under half the money needed in 1982 [verified, GAO summary], so assets and promises can differ widely. Members who live in the counties and work in the city count here.**

**Tendency.** A fund of **$375M** held in trust is the largest pool of lendable money in the city. At 7 percent a year its income is **$26M**. The Teamsters-style practice of lending fund money to friends is what the 1974 ERISA law and the Labor Department's cases were aimed at (Part IV 2.6).

It ends at age 70, on full-time work elsewhere, or on Social Security | | [verified, Christian Science Monitor, 1 Feb 1984; rate from FRASER]; the dollar figure is computed |

**Tendency.** A Teamsters pension of $450 a month is $5,400 a year, 42 percent of a driver's $12,900 wage (6.2 times 2,080 hours). It is a promise made by a fund that may not keep it, which is why a man in trouble with the fund is a man the Life can reach. A longshoreman's guaranteed income is a promise made by an employers' association, and 1983's cost of $70M a year (verified above) is why employers fight it.

### 4.3 What the families take from labor

The take is a share of flows the union already moves. The drivers:

| Local | Driver | Default flow to a family, net | Range | Holder |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Docks, Pellegrino side | Payroll of about **7,000 men (Part I 9.2) at about $12,000 is $84M**. Stevedore peace payments, hiring-boss kickbacks, fund and royalty skim, no-show jobs | **$3.0M** total docks, of which Pellegrino 75 percent = $2.25M | $1.4M to $4.8M | Pellegrino (cross-river 50/50, Part V) |
| Trucking, Teamsters locals | Fund loans, job sales, short-weight and dues skim | **$2.0M** | $0.9M to $3.4M | Ferrante 55 percent, others 15 each |
| Building trades, Sabatini | Job placements, shop-steward pay, fund loans, no-show | **$1.5M** | $0.7M to $2.6M | Sabatini |
| Garment locals | Shop kickbacks, trucker and contractor arrangements | **$0.6M** | $0.25M to $1.0M | Ferrante |
| Hotel and restaurant locals | Dues and vendor kickbacks | **$0.4M** | $0.15M to $0.7M | Sabatini and Vannucci |
| Mills (the Steelworkers are not controlled, Part IV 2.7); the smaller shop locals | Vendor and supply arrangements | **$0.6M** | $0.25M to $1.1M | Vannucci |
| **Total** | | **$8.1M flow, of which the families $7.35M and the Brigades $0.75M (8.1)** | | |

**Counterargument.** The recent literature [memory, to verify] finds that corruption of this kind lives with a few officers and not with a family, and that most of the flow ends in a handful of pockets. The model treats each line as family money after the officers' own shares. A reader who thinks that is too generous should cut the labor lines by a third. Section 9 shows the effect.

**Drift.** Fixed fees decay with prices (section 1.1). Percentage-of-payroll flows rise with wages, which in 1975 dollars is flat to 6 percent higher by 1990. **Weather (strength strong, 1976 onward, Open per run):** the prosecutions and civil trusteeships of the 1980s [memory, to verify, Part IV carries the dates] **press on family labor flows. Design default response if nothing in the run deflects it (10.1), labor flow** in 1975 dollars: 1975 $8.1M, 1980 $8.1M, 1985 $6.5M, 1990 $5.0M, **of which the families hold 91 percent ($7.35M, $7.35M, $5.9M, $4.5M)**. **Design.**

### 4.4 Sales, rents, wages and margins by trade

Part IV 3.2 gave Design ranges for "left for the owner", which it defines as what remains after goods, labor, rent and other costs and **before** the owner's income tax, debt service and any payment to a family or the Pad. **Revision 1 gave a "net margin" lower than Part IV's and did not say why. The difference is the owner's own pay. Part IV's figure is the owner's living. Revision 1's figure is what is left after paying the owner-manager a wage, which is the business's economic net. Both are shown below, and Part VI's later sections use the economic net.** No sales or margin figure was found in a source (the Census of retail trade was searched for and not reached, 11.3). Everything in this section is **Design**.

**Sales, staff, wages and rent (typical firm, 1975 dollars)**

| Trade | Sales a year | Range | Staff | Staff pay per hour | Rent a month |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Neighborhood tavern | **$90,000** | $50,000 to $180,000 | 3 | $3.00 | $450 |
| Sit-down restaurant | **$150,000** | $70,000 to $400,000 | 8 | $2.60 | $600 |
| Grocery | **$120,000** | $60,000 to $300,000 | 2 | $3.00 | $350 |
| Bakery or pork store | **$110,000** | $60,000 to $220,000 | 4 | $3.20 | $400 |
| Supper club or nightclub | **$500,000** | $200,000 to $1,500,000 | 14, plus about $70,000 of talent | $3.00 and tips | $2,500 |
| Funeral home | **$180,000** | $110,000 to $350,000 | 5 | $4.00 | owned, or $900 |
| Laundromat | **$75,000** | $45,000 to $130,000 | 1.5 | $2.40 | $700 |
| Vending or jukebox route | **$120,000** | $60,000 to $250,000 | 3 | $3.50 | $400 (yard) |
| Small trucking firm, 6 to 8 trucks | **$400,000** | $150,000 to $1,500,000 | 10 | $6.20 (1.3) | $1,200 (yard) |
| Concrete or masonry subcontractor | **$600,000** | $200,000 to $3,000,000 | 12 | $8.00 (union scale, 1.3) | $800 |
| Garment contracting shop | **$250,000** | $100,000 to $700,000 | 25 | $3.00 (piece-rate equivalent) | $900 |
| Carting or waste firm | **$500,000** | $200,000 to $2,000,000 | 8 | $5.00 | $1,000 (yard) |

The staff counts are set so that labor lands inside Part IV 3.2's shares of sales (tavern 21 percent, restaurant 29, bakery 24, funeral home 23, trucking 32, concrete subcontractor 33, garment 62).

**Margins (share of sales)**

| Trade | Left for the owner (Part IV 3.2 definition) | Owner-manager's own pay | Net after the owner's pay | Range of the net | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Neighborhood tavern | 12% (Part IV: 10 to 20) | 5% | **7%** | 4 to 12 | [memory, to verify] |
| Sit-down restaurant | 7% (5 to 10) | 3% | **4%** | 2 to 7 | [memory, to verify] |
| Grocery | 5% (not in Part IV) | 3.5% | **1.5%** | 0.5 to 3 | [memory, to verify] |
| Bakery or pork store | 10% (8 to 15) | 6% | **4%** | 2 to 7 | Design |
| Supper club or nightclub | 15% (10 to 20) | 5% | **10%** | 3 to 18 | Design |
| Funeral home | 25% (20 to 30) | 13% | **12%** | 7 to 18 | Design |
| Laundromat | 28% (25 to 35) | 10% | **18%** | 10 to 26 | Design |
| Vending or jukebox route | 20% (15 to 25) | 6% | **14%** | 8 to 22 | Design |
| Small trucking firm | 5% (3 to 8) | 2% | **3%** | 1 to 6 | [memory, to verify] |

Revision 1's single laundromat-and-vending figure of 14 percent is split: laundromat 18, vending 14.

**Use.** A weekly payment of $25 is $1,300 a year. A tavern that sells $90,000 leaves its owner $10,800 and nets $6,300 after his pay. The payment is 12 percent of his living and 21 percent of the net. A grocery that sells $120,000 leaves its owner $6,000 and nets $1,800 after his pay. The payment is 22 percent of his living and 72 percent of the net. His living of $6,000 is already under a grocery clerk's wage of $6,240 ($3.00 times 2,080 hours), so a $1,300 payment leaves him worse off than working for someone else. The tendency in the engine should be: groceries and pork stores are the businesses that cannot pay, and the ones that do are the ones with cash and a license.

### 4.5 Bond premiums, sales tax and the legitimate fronts

| Item | Figure | Mark |
|---|---|---|
| New Jersey sales tax | **5 percent from 1970, raised from 3 percent by chapter 7 of the laws of 1970; 6 percent from 1990** | **[verified, New Jersey State Library record, enacted 1970]; the 1990 rise to 6 percent is [memory, to verify]** |
| New Jersey state income tax | **passed in July 1976; first withholding 3 September 1976 [verified, Part IV 6.7]** | Weather, section 10 |
| Contract surety premium | 1 to 2.5 percent of contract value | [memory, to verify] |
| Legitimate-front profit, Sabatini | **$2.5M** | $1.0M to $4.0M, **Design** |
| Legitimate-front profit, Ferrante | **$1.2M** | $0.6M to $2.0M, **Design** |
| Legitimate-front profit, Pellegrino | **$0.5M** | $0.2M to $1.0M, **Design** |
| Legitimate-front profit, Vannucci | **$0.8M** | $0.3M to $1.4M, **Design** |

The fronts are real businesses (Part III 7.8). Their profit is what the family may spend without laundering. **Flag.** The four front figures are the least-grounded in the model, and are the one family income that survives a prosecution of the rackets. **The $2.5M of Sabatini front profit does not include the Sabatini construction firms (section 3.4, $1.0M), which are counted under the Concrete Club line in 9.3A. The two are separate and are not to be added twice.**

Other commercial flows, all **Design**: carting and waste to the Vannucci, $4.5M (range $2.0M to $7.0M), of which the family keeps $3.6M; the fish market to Pellegrino, $1.5M ($0.8M to $2.6M); Tri-State Freight to Pellegrino, $0.4M ($0.2M to $0.7M); the Strip to Vannucci mostly, $3.6M ($2.0M to $6.0M); the bolita tax and the **fees and shares that the Jewish specialists pay to the families (Part V 10.1; section 8)** at **$0.06M** and $0.3M. These are carried to section 9 under their names.

**Counterargument.** The carting figure is high. A 11 percent margin on a $40M city carting market is $4.4M, so the family would hold all of the market's profit. The honest reading is that Vannucci holds a share, and the default should be read as the top of the plausible range.

## 5. Theft and Fencing

No method. Values, discounts and flows only.

### 5.1 What is sourced

**Anchor [verified, FRED, State Government Tax Collections, tobacco products, New Jersey].** Annual collections in thousands of dollars: 166,640 (1975), 167,508 (1976), 168,780 (1977), 168,940 (1978), 170,274 (1979), 170,572 (1980), 176,203 (1981), 177,445 (1982), then 214,543 (1983), 217,214, 216,626, 214,203, 210,955, 221,826, 212,173, 202,596 (1990). These are collections, not a rate.

**Inference.** Collections were flat from 1975 to 1982 and stepped up 20.9 percent in 1983. A rate rise from 19 cents to 23 cents a pack is a 21 percent rise. That fits Part III 7.5, which puts New Jersey at 23 cents around 1982 [verified, from a 1982 report, as Part III states it]. The 1975 rate is therefore likely 19 cents [inference, rate itself memory, to verify]. The 1983 date for the 23-cent rate is likewise inferred.

**Margin on untaxed cigarettes.** North Carolina at 2 cents against New Jersey at 19 cents is a 17-cent gap a pack, **$1.70 a carton**, in 1975 [inference]. Part III 7.5 says smugglers made up to a dollar a carton around 1982, when the gap was 21 cents ($2.10 a carton). The 1975 gap is therefore about 80 percent of the 1982 gap, so the margin in 1975 is smaller, as Part III says. **The Contraband Cigarette Trafficking Act, enacted 2 November 1978 (Public Law 95-575), with its trafficking provision in effect about 1 March 1979 [verified in outline],** cuts it. Default smuggler net **(range $0.50 to $1.10 a carton)**: **$0.80 in 1975 and 1978; $0.56 from 1979 after the federal act; $0.70 from 1983 as the New Jersey rate rises.** **Design.**

### 5.2 Fence discounts by good (Part III 7.5)

The fence pays a fraction of the goods' regular wholesale value. **Design**, tied to Part III 7.5's example of 35 percent on a load.

| Good | Fence pays | Range | Resale by the dealer, as a share of wholesale value |
|---|---|---|---|
| Clothing, general | 30 percent | 22 to 38 | 55 percent |
| Appliances, televisions | 32 percent | 25 to 40 | 58 percent |
| Liquor, cigarettes | 55 percent | 45 to 65 | 75 percent |
| Food, meat | 40 percent | 30 to 50 | 65 percent |
| Electronics | 30 percent | 22 to 38 | 55 percent |
| Jewelry, furs | 20 percent | 12 to 28 | 45 percent |
| Auto parts | 30 percent | 20 to 40 | 55 percent |
| Metals, copper | 45 percent | 35 to 55 | 70 percent |
| **Default blended** | **30 percent** | 22 to 38 | **55 percent** |

**Cargo value by class, in general terms (Design):** a loaded general-merchandise trailer $40,000 to $150,000; consumer electronics or high-value apparel $120,000 to $300,000; a loaded marine container $50,000 to $200,000; a truckload of liquor or cigarettes $40,000 to $120,000; a mixed small shipment $5,000 to $20,000. Default load $90,000 (range $60,000 to $130,000).

### 5.3 The crew's split

On Part III 7.5's illustration, a $200,000 load fenced at 35 percent brings $70,000. The finder's fee is 10 percent ($7,000). The driver and costs are 10.7 percent ($7,500). The remainder to the crew and its sponsor is **79 percent of the fence price**. A further loss on the whole book is **40 percent** of fenced value, which covers loads that fail, goods seized, and goods that cannot be sold. **Design.**

| Item | Default | Range |
|---|---|---|
| Loads a year in Italian-held theft | 200 | 120 to 320 |
| Average cargo value | $90,000 | $60,000 to $130,000 |
| Fence percentage | 30 percent | 22 to 38 |
| Crew's net share after finder, driver, costs and failure | 0.79 times 0.60 = 47 percent of fence price | |
| **Crew and sponsor net, city** | **$2.56M** | **$0.75M to $7.5M (inputs at their ends)** |

Allocation (Design): Ferrante 50 percent, Pellegrino 25 (ship-side, Part V 3), Vannucci 15, Sabatini 10.

**Counterargument.** Cargo theft in a port-and-trucking city may be much larger than 200 loads a year. The same figure could instead cover a smaller number of high-value loads. The model is not sensitive to the split, only to value stolen.

### 5.4 The Hunts Row dealer

**Anchor (Part V 7.1):** the Jewish dealers and financiers of Hunts Row handle the garment fence. Their class cannot be made (Part III 5.5).

The Row buys at the fence price and sells at the resale price, and its volume is the Italian-held loads times a factor of 1.5, because the Row also handles other sources. **Design.**

| Item | Default | Range |
|---|---|---|
| Goods bought, valued at wholesale | $18M times 1.5 = $27M | |
| Bought at 30 percent | $8.1M | |
| Resold at 55 percent | $14.85M | |
| Gross markup | **$6.75M** | |
| Net after carrying costs, bad debts and the Row's own financing (30 percent of markup) | **$4.73M** | $2.0M to $9.0M |
| Tax to Ferrante, 10 percent of dealer net | **$0.47M** | $0.2M to $0.9M |
| **Fees and shares paid to the families by the Row's financiers and specialists (section 8; Part V 10.1)** | $0.30M | $0.15M to $0.5M |

**User burglary adds $25M to $33M of small goods a year, fenced mostly outside the Row (pawnshops, bars, street buyers).**

**Flag.** $4.7M is a large dealer income and exceeds the crew income it rests on. The Row's turnover includes the legitimate garment trade. If the dealer net were half, Ferrante's tax would fall from $0.47M to $0.24M, which does not alter section 9. It does alter the Row's weight in section 8.

### 5.5 Cars, chop shops, untaxed goods and arson

| Item | Default | Range | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|
| Chop-shop net to the families, city | $0.50M | $0.25M to $0.90M | **Design** |
| Allocation | Vannucci and Ferrante, half each | | **Design** |
| Untaxed cigarettes and liquor net to the families | $0.20M | $0.05M to $0.5M | **Design** |

The rest of burglary, robbery, arson and insurance fraud is freelance (Part III 7.5) and not family income. It enters the model only as the value stolen.

### 5.6 Drift

Cargo value rises with prices, so the theft flow in nominal dollars follows the deflator. In 1975 dollars the theft net is held flat to 1978, then falls: containerization and tighter seals reduce loads, the 1978 federal trafficking law (in effect about March 1979) cuts cigarettes, and federal cases hit the Row [memory, to verify]. **Design** default of the crew net in 1975 dollars: 1975 $2.56M, 1980 $2.4M, 1985 $1.9M, 1990 $1.5M. Cigarette smuggling margin: see 5.1.

### 5.7 Open from this section

- The real discount rates for each good. Unchecked.
- The 19 and 23 cent New Jersey rates and their dates. [memory, to verify], with the inference above.
- Whether the Row is as large as the default. **Open**.

## 6. Drugs

Market levels and money flows only. No synthesis, cutting, cooking, smuggling or concealment detail (Part I 0.5, principle 7). Nothing here says how to make, move or hide anything.

### 6.1 What is sourced, and what is not

**Anchor [verified, DEA, drug price and purity tables, 1991 to March 1994, via the NCJRS copy].** National street prices per gram of cocaine: $35 to $175 (1991), $15 to $150 (1992), $15 to $200 (1993). Cocaine purity: 59 percent (1991), 64 (1992), 63 (1993). Powder heroin per gram: $40 to $450 (1991), $100 to $600 (1992). Heroin purity on purchases: 30 percent (1991), 37 (1992), 58 (1993), 53 (early 1994).

**Anchor [verified, ONDCP, *The Price of Illicit Drugs: 1981 through the Second Quarter of 2000*, Tables 1 and 2, smallest purchase level].** Prices are per pure gram in 2000 dollars. I converted them with the CPI-U (1.1) to nominal and to 1975 dollars. The figures are annual means of the four quarters, as read.

| Year | Heroin, 2000 $ per pure gram | Heroin purity | Heroin, nominal | Heroin, 1975 $ | Cocaine, 2000 $ per pure gram | Cocaine purity | Cocaine, nominal | Cocaine, 1975 $ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1981 | 3,295 | 4% | 1,739 | 1,029 | 423 | 36% | 223 | 132 |
| 1983 | 3,652 | 6% | 2,113 | 1,141 | 399 | 40% | 231 | 125 |
| 1985 | 3,146 | 8% | 1,966 | 983 | 328 | 40% | 205 | 102 |
| 1987 | 3,306 | 11% | 2,181 | 1,033 | 292 | 64% | 193 | 91 |
| 1990 | 2,924 | 16% | 2,219 | 913 | 267 | 69% | 203 | 84 |

**Reading.** From 1981 to 1990 the real price of a pure gram of heroin fell about 11 percent, which is roughly flat, while purity rose fourfold. The real price of a pure gram of cocaine fell about 37 percent while purity roughly doubled. The report notes missing cells in some quarters and says the smallest heroin level is an injection-level purchase, so a misaligned cell is possible. **Revision 1 assumed that heroin fell 88 percent in real terms from 1975 to 1990. That is withdrawn.**

**What the search found and did not find.** The ONDCP series starts in 1981. A price and purity series for 1975 to 1980 was not found in the sources reached, so every 1975 to 1980 price and purity figure below is **Design**, a mild decline into the 1981 level. User counts and spend per user are **Design** in every year. **The ONDCP spending study for 1988 to 1998 was found in revision 1 but not read in full, and its user counts are not used.**

**Anchor (carried, Part III 7.6) [verified]:** the Pizza Connection case, arrests April 1984, trial September 1985 to March 1987, prosecutors' claim of about $1.65 billion of heroin imported into the Northeast and Midwest from 1975 to 1984. That is about $165 million a year across a region of more than 100 million people. Saint Jude's 1.1 million is about 1 percent of it, so one such network accounts for about $1.7 million a year of import value against a city heroin retail market of $20M to $30M. This is consistent: a single network is a small part of one city's heroin supply.

### 6.2 Users, prices and purity, 1975 to 1990

User rates are **regular users per 1,000 residents** (a user who buys most weeks). All **Design**. Dollars are nominal unless stated. "Per pure gram" is the standard comparison across years. **The rates are set against the 1975 population of 1.1 million, and the user counts they give are held as headcounts. As the city shrinks (Part I 4.1), the rate per 1,000 of the people who remain rises, and the markets in 6.3 do not change. If the run slows or reverses the fall, the headcounts still hold unless the cause of the change itself moves the drug market, which is the run's to decide.**

**Heroin**

| Year | Regular users per 1,000 | Retail price per pure gram, nominal | Same, 1975 dollars | Street purity | Spend per user per year, 1975 dollars | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1975 | 5.0 (3 to 9) | $1,500 ($1,000 to $2,500) | $1,500 | 5% (3 to 8) | $5,400 | Design |
| 1977 | 4.8 | $1,600 | $1,420 | 5% | $5,200 | Design |
| 1980 | 4.5 | $1,700 | $1,110 | 4% (3 to 6) | $4,600 | Design |
| **1981** | 4.4 | **$1,739** | **$1,029** | **4%** | **$4,500** | **price and purity [verified, ONDCP]** |
| 1983 | 4.2 | **$2,113** | **$1,141** | **6%** | **$4,300** | **price and purity [verified]** |
| 1985 | 4.0 | **$1,966** | **$983** | **8%** | **$4,100** | **price and purity [verified]** |
| **1987** | 4.2 | **$2,181** | **$1,033** | **11%** | **$3,900** | **price and purity [verified]** |
| 1990 | 4.5 (3 to 7) | **$2,219** | **$913** | **16%** | **$3,800** | **price and purity [verified]** |

**The 1981 to 1990 prices and purities are [verified, ONDCP, 6.1]. The real price of a pure gram is roughly flat, about $1,030 in 1981 and $910 in 1990 in 1975 dollars, and purity rises fourfold. The street gram, which is the price per pure gram times purity, is about **$70** in 1981 and $355 in 1990. The DEA's range for 1991 was $40 to $450 a gram at 30 percent purity [verified, above], which brackets the 1990 figure. Users per 1,000 are Design. Spend per user is Design and is revised in this round: it falls from $5,400 to $3,800 and no longer rests on a falling price per pure gram. The fall stands for lighter and part-year use, and it is an assumption.**

**Cocaine and crack**

| Year | Regular users per 1,000 | Price per street gram, nominal | Purity | Per pure gram, nominal | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1975 | **2 (1 to 5)** | $110 ($70 to $170) | 30% (20 to 45) | $367 | **Design, lowered from 4 (flag below)** |
| 1980 | **6 (3 to 10)** | $90 | 35% | $257 | **Design, lowered from 8** |
| 1983 | **12** | **$92** | **40%** | **$231** | **[verified, derived from ONDCP]** |
| 1985 | 20 (10 to 30) | **$82** | **40%** | **$205** | **[verified, derived]** |
| **1987** | 19 | **$124** | **64%** | **$193** | **[verified, derived]** |
| 1990 | 18 (10 to 28) | **$141** ($35 to $175 in 1991) | **69%** | **$203** | **[verified, derived; the DEA's 1991 range is the check]** |

**A street gram is the price per pure gram times purity (arithmetic). The users per 1,000 are Design.**

**Flag against Part II 0.2 (resolved in this pass, on plausibility).** Part II calls cocaine in 1975 an expensive amusement of the rich and of the entertainment world. Revision 2 had 4 regular users per 1,000, about 4,400 buyers, and that stretches the reading. **I lowered the 1975 default to 2 per 1,000 (range 1 to 5), about 2,200 buyers concentrated on the Strip, the Heights and Crown Harbor, and a market of $3.1M, 6 percent of drug spend. 1980 falls from 8 to 6 and 1983 from 14 to 12. 1985 and 1990 are unchanged, so the cocaine boom after 1983 is as large as before and starts from a smaller base. Reasons: a street gram at $110 is about two days of the 1975 median family income ($13,720 a year, about $53 a day), so a regular habit is a rich or entertainment-trade habit, which is Part II 0.2 read in dollars; and Part II is the older, source-checked description of the Period Mind, while the user count is mine. The cost of the change is small: the four families' default net falls by $0.1M and supportable made men stay at 381 (section 9) **in revision 2; revision 3's figure is 358**. Counterargument: Saint Jude has a Strip, a casino-adjacent trade and a harbor, and a coastal entertainment city could carry more cocaine than a typical one. The top of the range, 5 per 1,000, leaves room for that. No source for a 1975 local user count was found, so the figure is Design and remains the user's call.**

Crack (rock cocaine) appears about 1984 to 1985 (carried, Part V). **Design:** a rock of about $20 in 1985, $10 to $15 by 1987 (range $5 to $25). Regular crack users per 1,000: 2 (1985), **6 (1987, range 3 to 11), 6.5 (1990, range 4 to 12)**, concentrated in the core and the Basin. Spend per crack user $2,500 a year in 1975 dollars (range $1,200 to $5,000). **Flag:** the crack boom is the largest single break in the price book after 1984, and what it does to the Eastside, **the Strip** and the Pad is **Open** to the run.

**Marijuana, pills, crank (Design)**

| Item | 1975 | 1980 | 1985 | 1990 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Marijuana, monthly users | 78,000 (7% of residents) | 99,000 (9%) | 77,000 (7%) | 44,000 (4%) |
| Marijuana, commercial ounce, nominal | $30 ($20 to $45) | $45 | $60 | $90 |
| Marijuana, spend per user, 1975 dollars | $150 | $150 | $130 | $110 |
| Pills (barbiturates, depressants, diverted prescription stimulants and painkillers), market, 1975 dollars | $8.0M ($4M to $15M) | $9.0M | $6.0M | $4.0M |
| Crank, market, 1975 dollars | $1.8M ($0.6M to $4M) | $2.5M | $3.0M | $3.5M |

### 6.3 The city market, 1975 dollars

Retail spend by drug, city, 1975 dollars. Users times spend, except crack, which is added as a market. **Design.**

| Year | Heroin | Cocaine and crack | Marijuana | Pills | Crank | **Total** | Nominal total |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1975 | $29.7M | **$3.1M** | $11.7M | $8.0M | $1.8M | **$54.3M** | **$54.3M** |
| 1980 | **$22.8M** | **$9.9M** | $14.8M | $9.0M | $2.5M | **$59.0M** | **$90.3M** |
| 1985 | **$18.0M** | $38.0M | $10.0M | $6.0M | $3.0M | **$75.0M** | **$150.0M** |
| 1990 | **$18.8M** | $47.7M | $4.8M | $4.0M | $3.5M | **$78.8M** | **$191.4M** |

Per resident in 1975 that is $49 a year. The shape: **heroin falls about 40 percent in real dollars from 1975 to 1985, because users thin and use lightens (spend per user, 6.2), and then edges up as users return. It does not fall because the price per pure gram falls, since after 1981 that price is flat [verified].** Cocaine and then crack take its place from 1980 and 1985. **Total real spend rises about 40 percent by 1985 and then holds.**

**Counterargument.** National studies [memory, to verify] show the number of heroin users did not fall as sharply as this table makes it. **The verified ONDCP series removes revision 1's explanation for falling spend, which was a falling price per pure gram. What remains is an assumption of lighter and part-year use. If spend per user stayed at $5,400, the 1990 heroin market would be $26.7M and not $18.8M, and the family drug take of **$0.99M** in 1990 would be about **$1.03M**.**

**Check against the national total.** ONDCP put US retail drug spending at $51.6 billion in 1988 and $40.4 billion in 1990 [verified, NCJRS abstract]. At a US population of about 245 and 249 million [memory, to verify], that is about $96 and $67 a resident in 1975 dollars. Saint Jude sits at or below the national mean, which is low for a port city with a heroin core. The default stands. The range for 1985 to 1990 runs from the default to 2.5 times it (Design).**

### 6.4 The chain's margin (Part V 9.3)

Split of the consumer's dollar. **Design**, one set of shares for all five markets.

| Role | Gross share of the consumer dollar | Net share, after losses, costs, own use, risk and rent | Who |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source and import (offstage) | 52 percent | not modeled | Overseas and New York or Miami suppliers |
| Wholesale and middle levels | 18 percent | **9.1 percent** | A few crews, Cuban or New York-linked suppliers (Part V 9.3) |
| Retail | 30 percent | **18 percent** | Eastside men, the blocks, small dealers, the Strip |
| Financing return (paid out of the source and wholesale shares) | | 3 percent | Lenders, including families at dial step 1 or above |

**Counterargument.** An 18 percent net to retail is high for a dealer who is also a user, who is cheated by his own cut, and who goes to prison. The literature [memory, to verify] finds low incomes for street sellers. The model's 18 percent is a pool, shared among many sellers, not one man's income. The family never sees that pool, only the tax on it.

### 6.5 The tax at each dial step

The Part V dial is not changed. The money attached to each step, for each **$10 million of retail spend** on a family's ground:

| Step | Meaning (Part V 9.2) | Family take per $10M of retail spend | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Forbidden and enforced | **$0**, and a cost: the family must police its men | |
| 1 | Finance tolerated | **$180,000** a year **on each $10M of retail spend on the family's own ground: 60 percent of the 3 percent financing pool on that ground (40 percent goes to loss and the front man). Financing off Italian ground goes to other lenders. A family at step 2 or 3 takes that step's take and not the financing return as well** | Design |
| 2 | Crews permitted with a tax | **$407,000** | 15 percent (range 10 to 25) of the crews' net (wholesale 9.1 plus retail 18 = 27.1 percent of spend) |
| 3 | Run by the family | **$770,000** | 45 percent of wholesale net plus 20 percent of retail net |

**Territorial rule.** A step 2 or step 3 family takes only on its own ground. Only part of any drug market lies on Italian ground. The rest is the Eastside, the Puerto Rican blocks, the Brigades' streets, Chinatown and the suburbs. **Design shares of the city market on Italian ground:** heroin 25 percent, cocaine 50, marijuana 45, pills 55, crank 40. The ground is split among the families: Sabatini 30 percent, Pellegrino 15, Vannucci 25, Ferrante 30.

**Result, at the Part V dial defaults, in each family's annual take (1975 dollars):**

| Year | Sabatini | Pellegrino | Vannucci | Ferrante | All four | Share of city drug spend |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1975 | **$0.14M** | **$0.03M** | **$0.19M** | **$0.18M** | **$0.54M** | 1.0% |
| 1980 | **$0.17M** | **$0.05M** | **$0.23M** | **$0.24M** | **$0.68M** | 1.2% |
| 1985 | **$0.20M** | **$0.07M** | **$0.32M** | **$0.36M** | **$0.95M** | 1.3% |
| 1990 | **$0.19M** | **$0.08M** | **$0.34M** | **$0.38M** | **$0.99M** | 1.3% |

The dials are held at the Part V defaults for the table. A real run will move them (Part V 9.2).

**Where the money makes a setting implausible (flags, not changes):**

1. **Vannucci's reason.** Part V 9.2 said narcotics was the easy money for the Vannucci. At 1975 prices their whole drug take is about **$0.2M, under 2 percent** of their modeled net of **$11.1M** (section 9). Heroin alone at step 2 gives them about $76,000. The default is not made impossible, but the motive is thin: the Vannucci would be taking a federal risk for about a sixtieth of their income. The motive grows only with cocaine after 1980 and crack after 1985. **Part V 9.2 now says the reason is risk appetite and the Strip's need for cash, not size of income (Part VI pass).**
2. **Step 3 anywhere.** No family is at step 3 in 1975. If one were, say the Vannucci on cocaine, the take on their ground would be about $0.77M per $10M of retail spend on that ground. On the 1985 cocaine and crack market of $38M, half of it on Italian ground and a quarter of that Vannucci's, the take is about $0.37M. A step-3 jump nearly doubles the step-2 take and does not rescue a poor family.
3. **Step 1 for the Sabatini.** The Sabatini lend at step 1 for heroin, cocaine and pills. Their financing return is about **$0.07M** a year across the drugs, **$0.04M** of it heroin. That is small against their lawyers' bills (section 9) and it carries the federal risk of a lender in a narcotics conspiracy. The restraint costs them little, which is why it survives.
4. **Pellegrino at step 0 for heroin.** It costs them nothing in 1975, since the Pellegrino's ground is the docks and parishes, and the heroin trade is on the Eastside and the blocks.

### 6.6 The habit against an Eastside wage (Part V 9.3)

| Item | Figure | Mark |
|---|---|---|
| Full-time heroin habit, per day | **$30** (range $15 to $60) | Design |
| Per year | **$10,950** | arithmetic |
| Against median family income 1975 ($13,720) | 80 percent | [memory, to verify] for the income |
| Against a minimum-wage full year ($2.10 times 2,080 = $4,368) | 2.5 times | arithmetic |
| Against a garment operator ($3.00 times 2,080 = $6,240) | 1.75 times | Design wage |
| Against a construction laborer ($5.50 times 2,080 = $11,440) | 0.96 times | Design wage |
| Stolen goods needed a year, at a 30 percent fence rate (section 5.2) | $36,500, or about $700 a week | arithmetic |

**Result.** A full-time habit costs more than a full-time earner makes on the Eastside, except for a union laborer. Users support the habit by theft, by dealing small, by a spouse's earnings or by part-year use. The annual spend per user in the table ($5,400 in 1975) is about half the full-time habit, which is a mix of full-time and part-year use. **This ties to section 5, and it is a warning.** About 5,500 dependent users each fencing the $18,000 of goods that the average spend implies would be about $99M of stolen goods a year, or $90 a resident. That is several times the national burglary and larceny loss per head of roughly $14 [memory, to verify], and far larger than the organized cargo theft in section 5.3 ($18M of cargo). It cannot all be theft. The rest of the habit is paid by dealing small, family money, welfare, sex work and part-year use. **Design rule:** a quarter to a third of the habit money (about $7M to $10M of habit money, which is $25M to $33M of goods at a 30 percent fence rate) comes through theft and fencing. The Storyteller should treat user burglary as one main source of fenced small goods, and organized cargo as the main source of large loads.

**Habit in later years (nominal, default).** The cost of a full-time habit stays near $30 a day in nominal dollars as purity rises, because a user spends what he has. Against a minimum-wage full year it is 1.7 times in 1980 ($6,448) and 1.6 times in 1985 and 1990 ($6,968). **Design.** **Arithmetic at the verified prices: $30 buys about 17 milligrams of pure heroin in 1981 ($30 over $1,739 a gram) and about 14 in 1990 ($30 over $2,219).**

### 6.7 Drift summary

- **Heroin:** **real price per pure gram roughly flat from 1981 to 1990 (down 11 percent, 1975 dollars) [verified], a mild fall into 1981 [Design]; purity from 4 to 16 percent [verified]; users flat; real spend down about 40 percent from 1975 to 1985 and then flat to up [Design].**
- **Cocaine:** **real price per pure gram down 37 percent from 1981 to 1990 ($132 to $84, 1975 dollars) and purity from 36 to 69 percent [verified]. The street gram is flat in real terms, about $47 in 1981 and $58 in 1990 in 1975 dollars (computed). Users up four to five times [Design], the largest market by 1985.**
- **Crack:** from 1984 to 1985, cheap and concentrated in the core, a market of $5M in 1985 and $18M in 1990 (1975 dollars).
- **Marijuana:** rises to 1980, falls after (enforcement and attitudes, 1986 law [memory, to verify]).
- **Pills:** methaqualone and depressants peak in the late 1970s and fall [memory, to verify].
- **Crank:** small, rising, tied to the Horsemen (Part V 6).

### 6.8 Open from this section

- **Every price and purity figure before 1981 (Design). 1981 to 1990 is verified. User counts and spend per user are Design in every year.**
- The ground shares, 25 to 55 percent. **Open** to the run, and the single most important driver of family drug income.
- Whether any family moves to step 3 on cocaine in 1980 to 1985. **Open**.
- **The fate of the Eastside and the Strip under crack (Part III 13, Part V 9.5).** **Open**.

## 7. Government and the Upperworld

No Saint Jude official is named. Fiscal year follows Part I 3.5 (1 July); real New Jersey municipalities commonly use the calendar year [memory, to verify], and Part IV follows Saint Jude's choice, as this Part does.

### 7.1 What is sourced

**No city budget, debt, assessment or official-pay figure was found in a source, in either revision. Searches in revision 2 for New Jersey salaries, 1970s funeral costs and bail rules returned only links (11.3).** Real 1975 figures for a New Jersey city's budget, debt, assessments and officials' pay are **[memory, to verify]** or **Design**. The verified links are to section 1: the deflator (CPI-U, BLS), the federal and State minimum wages, **the prime rate and the mortgage rate, median family income, and the State income tax year (1976) and sales tax (5 percent from 1970), section 4.5**. The Design figures are scaled from ratios that hold across old Northeastern cities in 1975: payroll and benefits about three-quarters of an operating budget (Part IV 6.2, [memory, rough]), and a staff of 20 to 30 city and county employees per 1,000 residents [memory, to verify].

### 7.2 The city's money, 1 July 1974 to 30 June 1975

City and county are one government (Part I 1.1), so these figures carry county functions.

| Item | Default | Range | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|
| Operating budget | **$540M** ($490 a resident) | $420M to $700M | **Design** |
| Employees | 25,000 | 20,000 to 31,000 | **Design** (SJPD about 4,800, Part II 2.1) |
| Average pay | $12,500 | $10,500 to $15,000 | **Design**, scaled to section 1.3 |
| Wages | $312M | | arithmetic |
| Benefits and pension (30 percent on wages) | $94M | | **Design** |
| Wages plus benefits | $406M, **75 percent** of budget | | arithmetic, matches Part IV 6.2 |
| Debt service | $65M, 12 percent | $45M to $90M | **Design** |
| Everything else (supplies, contracts, repairs) | $69M, 13 percent | | arithmetic |
| Debt outstanding | **$600M** ($545 a resident) | $450M to $800M | **Design** |
| Pension fund employer contribution (inside benefits) | $45M | $30M to $65M | **Design** |

**Revenue (Design):** property tax $330M (61 percent); State aid $110M (20); federal aid $50M (9); fees, fines, licenses $50M (9). The sum is $540M. **The State income tax of 1976 [verified in outline] is the lever that can raise State aid later; how much of it reaches Saint Jude is Open (section 10).**

| Item | Default | Range | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|
| Property-tax levy | $330M | $260M to $420M | **Design** |
| True value of taxable property | $7.3B ($6,600 a resident) | $5.5B to $9B | **Design** |
| Effective rate on true value | 4.5 percent | 3.5 to 6 | **Design**, [memory, to verify] for old New Jersey cities |
| Assessment ratio to true value | 45 percent | 30 to 60 | **Design**, assessments lag |
| Federal aid, 1975 | $50M, about $45 a resident | $30M to $75M | **Design**, from block grants (Part I 9.5), revenue sharing and the 1973 job-program act [memory, to verify] |
| Municipal bond yield, high grade, 1975 | about 6 to 6.5 percent | | [memory, to verify]; **the prime rate that year was 10.05 percent in January and 7.26 in December [verified, section 1.5]** |
| Municipal bond yield, Saint Jude paper (low-rated, Part I 9.10) | 8 to 9.5 percent | | **Design**, [memory, to verify] for the spread |

**Flag.** The ratios are meant to be checked against an actual city. A Newark-sized city in 1975 had a higher property-tax rate, a bigger State share and a worse bond market than the Design default. The engine should treat the budget as a pressure, not a precise fact.

**Pressure (Part I 4.5, strong, beginning 1975).** A 1 percent fall in the tax base is $3.3M in the levy. A 5 percent fall in staff is 1,250 jobs and $19M of payroll. A short-term borrowing refused is a missed payroll. Layoffs land at 1 July.

### 7.4 Campaign costs (Part IV 4.10)

| Race | Default | Range | Mark |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mayoral election, contested, both stages | **$400,000** | $150,000 to $1,000,000 | **Design** |

| Cost per vote cast, mayoral | about $2.50 | $1 to $6 | **Design**, from 160,000 votes at turnout near 45 percent |

Campaign-finance laws of 1971 to 1974 are unchecked (Part IV lists them). A family's gifts are cash through intermediaries and are not counted in the family's modeled net. They would be a charge on it. **Design:** the Sabatini spend about $150,000 in an election year on candidates and committees, averaged to $75,000 a year.

### 7.5 The Pad (Part II 3)

The Pad is paid by those who run vice and gambling to police who look away. Federal agencies are not on the Pad. **All Design.** The Pad survives the Dolan reforms by changing shape (Part II 3), so the table is a 1975 starting point.

| Rank | Per week, default | Range | What it buys |
|---|---|---|---|
| Patrolman, beat | $30 | $15 to $60 | Not looking at a bar after hours or a numbers pickup |
| **Public Morals detective (Vice), after the Dolan reform** | $150 | $75 to $400 | A tipoff, a warning, a slow raid. **The old plainclothes squads were broken up (Part II 2.6); the money now travels through an intermediary, and the rule is to pay the position, not the man (Part II 3.2)** |
| Sergeant | $100 | $50 to $200 | Scheduling, a delayed response |
| Lieutenant, precinct squad | $250 | $120 to $500 | A case that is lost |
| Captain, precinct | **$400** | **$200 to $1,000** | The precinct's attention itself. **A captain earns $24,000 (Part II 2.2). Revision 1's $600 a week was $31,000 a year, more than his pay, and is lowered** |
| Commander and above (the "bag", through an intermediary, **Part II 3.2**) | $1,500 | $500 to $4,000 | Orders, transfers; very rare |

**Annual cost to the families, all together: $1.6M (range $0.9M to $2.7M), split Sabatini $0.41M, Pellegrino $0.30M, Vannucci $0.53M (the 14th Precinct Vice's price, Part III 4.4), Ferrante $0.36M. Revision 2's default was $2.7M; at the default envelopes that bought about 500 officers, a tenth of the force, which Part II 3.2 ("Fewer men are in it") does not allow. $1.6M buys about 300.** The total is paid by the families for their own rackets (cards and dice, the Strip, after-hours, loans). Payments by numbers banks and bookmakers sit inside their margins (2.2, 2.4) and are not part of it. This is about **2.2** percent of the SJPD's wage bill (**about $73M at 4,800 officers and a $15,200 average across Part II 2.2's ranks, Design**), a plausible size for a graft system, but not a documented one. **The annual total is a separate Design figure and is not the sum of the weekly table. Its default is about 300 officers on the Pad at a mean envelope of about $5,300 a year (about $100 a week). The table prices each rank, and the mix of ranks that gives that mean is left to the run.** The sum is taken as a cost against family revenue in section 9.

**What it cannot buy.** A federal case. A prosecutor outside the county's office. A cop who is not on the Pad. A scene the press has already seen. The Storyteller should show the Pad as a relation and not a purchase.

**Drift.** The Pad's envelope sizes follow the deflator (section 1.1) with a lag. **The 1975 table is already post-Dolan (Part II 2.6): the reforms of 1970 to 1972 happened before the baseline. In 1975 to 1977 the hiring freeze and layoffs make hungry cops (Part II 2.1) and push the asking price up by 10 to 20 percent in real terms. A new reform, a federal case or a new commander cuts it by a third. By 1985 it returns to about **$1.3M** in a different form (a few intermediaries, fewer men).** **In 1975 dollars: 1975 $1.6M, 1977 $1.8M, 1985 $1.3M.** **Design.** *(Revision 1 had the Dolan fall after 1975, which contradicted Part II.)*

**Counterargument.** A mid-level case "fixed" with a judge is more often a sentence reduced through a prosecutor or a probation report. The Period Mind (Part II, 0) says people believe in fixes more than they happen. The table prices the attempt. It does not price the result.

**Lawyer costs in the model.** The scale test (section 9) allows $150,000 a year per family at the lean standard, $250,000 at the default and $400,000 at the comfortable. That is one federal-level defense every two or three years plus routine matters.

| Year | Budget, 1975 dollars | Federal aid, 1975 dollars | Note |

| 1990 | $540M | $30M | Back to the 1975 real level |

**Design.** The nominal budget in 1980 is $485M times 1.532, about $743M, a rise of 38 percent over five years. The Open question is whether the city defaults (Part I 4.5). The bond market is the sign.

## 8. Outside-Group Money

Part V gives the groups as institutions (sections 3 to 8) and lists the money it needs (12.7). Each flow below is a **Design** range tied to a driver. Net, 1975 dollars. None of it is sourced, and the groups' internal events are not modeled. No real club's or gang's real history is used.

**Revision 2 re-read Part V sections 3 to 8 and 10.1 line by line and corrected what conflicted. The changes are in bold below.** (1) The Brigades pay **no tribute** to the Italians (Part V 3.6 and 10.1), so revision 1's $0.1M tribute is removed. The 50/50 is a cross-river share and is not tribute. (2) The Horsemen pay a courtesy and not a tribute, and any rent is work, so revision 1's $0.05M cash rent is removed. (3) Chinatown is about 1 percent of the city (Part V 7.2), so revision 1's eight halls at $150,000 were too large. (4) The Jewish specialists pay the families (Part V 10.1), and revision 1 had the direction reversed. Part V 12.7 had the same slip and is corrected there. (5) The Russians' tax is about two cents a gallon (Part III 5.3, carried in Part V 5.3). Revision 1 gave one to two cents. **I also found two arithmetic slips: the crank pool was 0.5M and is 0.3M, and the combine's cocaine, marijuana and pills pool was halved a second time and is 3.6M, not 1.8M.**

### 8.1 The Charlestown Brigades

| Flow | Default | Range | Driver |
|---|---|---|---|
| Port and union: the Irish side of the docks (Local 1201, Part V 3) | **$0.75M** | $0.4M to $1.6M | **The 25 percent of the $3.0M docks flow that does not go to Pellegrino (section 4.3). It includes the Brigades' half of the cross-river items (tugs, lighterage and ship-side pilferage, the 50/50, Part V section 1 #1). The Pellegrino half sits inside the other 75 percent** |
| Numbers, policy, small banks | **$0.32M** | $0.12M to $0.6M | Charlestown and Kerrytown handle, $4.0M (section 2.2) at the 8 percent net **(revision 1 had $0.4M)** |
| Loans and protection on their own blocks | **$0.46M** | **$0.15M to $0.75M** | **A tenth of the loansharking net (section 3.2, $2.47M) and a tenth of the protection (3.3, $2.13M), taken out of the families' shares** |
| Tribute to an Italian family | **$0** | **$0** | **Part V 3.6 and 10.1: the Brigades pay no tribute. The 50/50 is a cross-river share, not tribute** |
| **Total** | **$1.5M** | **$0.7M to $3.0M** | |

**Counterargument.** The Brigades are a set of loyalties and not a roster (Part V 3.1), so the money belongs to men, and a "Brigade total" overstates what any group can spend. The engine should draw each man's income, not the group's.

### 8.2 The Eastside combine (Part V 4)

| Flow | Default | Range | Driver |
|---|---|---|---|
| Numbers banks, net, Eastside share | $0.83M | $0.15M to $1.5M | 64 percent of the $25M handle (section 2.2) at 8 percent, less the Italian share of 35 percent, which is counted under the Italian families |
| Bookmaking, net to the combine | **$0.32M** | **$0.1M to $0.8M** | **20 percent of $1.6M (section 2.4). The other 25 percent ($0.4M) is the Jewish books, the Irish pubs and the Sixth Ward (Part III 5.5; Part V 3.2, 8.4)** |
| **Combine's own lines** | **$1.15M** | **$0.25M to $2.3M** | |
| Tribute to the Italians | already in sections 2 and 6 | | The numbers share (35 percent of net) and the dial taxes. **Part V 4.3: a negotiated slice, not a tribute** |

**The drug pool on non-Italian ground (shared, not a payroll).**

| Pool | Default | Range | Driver |
|---|---|---|---|
| Heroin, retail and wholesale net | **$6.0M** | $2M to $12M | 75 percent of the $29.7M city market lies off Italian ground, times the 27.1 percent net pool (section 6.4) |
| Cocaine, marijuana and pills, retail and wholesale net | **$3.1M** | $1.3M to $7M | **$11.6M of the $22.8M of those three markets lies off Italian ground (section 6.5 ground shares), times 27.1 percent. Revision 1 halved this twice and gave $1.8M; revision 2 gave $3.6M before cocaine was lowered (6.2)** |

**Who shares the pool.** The Eastside combine, the Puerto Rican blocks, the Brigades' streets, Chinatown, the Strip's independent sellers and the suburbs. Part V 9.3 lists the retail sellers as the Eastside men, the blocks, small Italian and Irish dealers, the Strip's clubs and the Horsemen at roadhouses. The pool is spread over many sellers. It is not a payroll, and no group's share of it is set here.

**Pick-3 effect** (section 2.3) cuts the combine's numbers net by the same percentage as the city's: **24** percent by 1980 and **34** percent by 1990, because part of the banks' costs does not fall with handle. If the loss is carried by the weaker banks (Part V 4.5), the combine thins and the stronger bankers gain.

**Counterargument.** The $6.0M heroin pool is the largest single flow in the outside groups and the least reliable one. It rests on the 25 percent Italian-ground share (section 6.8), so a different ground share moves it directly. At 40 percent on Italian ground it would be **$4.8M** (60 percent of the $29.7M market is non-Italian ground, times the 27.1 percent net pool).

### 8.3 The Russian handful (Part V 5)

**Part V 5.3: no such figure exists in January 1975. The pressure toward one rises from late 1975 and is strong by 1978 to 1980.** The flows below are therefore **Weather**, with dates, and the 1975 figure is zero.

| Flow | Default, with date | Range | Driver |
|---|---|---|---|
| Extortion and protection on the émigré community | **$0 in January 1975; $0.15M by 1980** | $0.05M to $0.3M | A few hundred small businesses and professionals in Brighton Point, a few dollars a week each, and fear of a stranger **(the real anchor, tens of thousands of dollars a week in New York by 1980, is for a much larger community [Part III 5.3, carried])** |
| Medical-insurance and auto fraud | **$0.3M by 1980** | $0.1M to $0.6M | Part V 5.6 (a skill the group brought) |
| Fuel excise evasion, from about 1982 [memory, to verify] | $1M in 1983 rising to **$3M** by 1987 | $0.5M to $8M | A tax on each gallon moved. Federal and State excise together were about 12 cents a gallon in the early 1980s [memory, to verify]. Volume is a Design draw |
| **Tax to the Italian family (Part V 5.5, Open which)** | **About 2 cents a gallon [carried anchor, Part III 5.3]. At 30 to 50 million gallons a year that is $0.6M to $1.0M by 1987** | | **Design volume. 2 cents is about a sixth of the evaded excise and a fifth to a third of the Russians' net if their net is 6 to 10 cents a gallon** |

**Flag.** The fuel scheme is a Weather item (strength strong, 1982 onward, section 10). Its Italian tax is a new pressure on one family, and which family takes it is **Open** (Part V 5.5). **Nothing in this table enters the 1975 scale test (section 9).**

**Reading.** The outside groups' own lines come to about $9.2M, and the shared drug pool adds about $9.1M, about $18.3M in all. The Italian families' modeled net is **$35.9M** after the Pad (section 9), so the outside groups hold about a third of the two combined and the Italian families about two thirds. The count leaves out the many retail sellers' own incomes and all freelance crime, so the Italian share of the city's illegal income is lower than two thirds. That fits Reuter and Rubinstein (section 2.1) and Part V's picture of independent groups that meet the families only at points of need. **The outside groups' flows to the Italian families are small: the Hunts Row tax ($0.47M), the Jewish specialists' fees ($0.30M), the *bolita* tax (**$0.06M**), the numbers and bookmaking shares (sections 2.2 and 2.4), and the dial taxes (6.5). The Brigades and the Horsemen pay none.**

- Whether the combine, the Brigades or the Horsemen are eclipsed by 1990 (Part V 12.3 lists each as Open).
- The Russians' fuel scheme and the family that taxes it.
